# Chapter 1: Executive Summary

## 1.1 Project Title

**NutriLogic: A Hybrid Neuro-Symbolic Expert System for Personalized Nutrition and Fitness Recommendations in Kenya**

---

## 1.2 Executive Summary

NutriLogic is a hybrid Neuro-Symbolic Expert System designed to deliver personalized, explainable, and culturally relevant nutrition and fitness recommendations for the Kenyan population. The system combines symbolic artificial intelligence, implemented using SWI-Prolog, with modern web technologies built on Django REST Framework and React. Its objective is to bridge the gap between evidence-based nutritional guidelines and intelligent decision support by integrating deterministic clinical reasoning with future machine learning capabilities.

Unlike conventional recommendation systems that rely primarily on statistical models or opaque machine learning algorithms, NutriLogic adopts a neuro-symbolic architecture. The symbolic component represents domain knowledge as explicit facts and logical rules derived from authoritative nutrition guidelines, while the neural component (planned for later phases) will provide probabilistic predictions, perception, and pattern recognition. The symbolic inference engine then validates, explains, and contextualizes these predictions before generating recommendations.

The current implementation is based on the **Kenya Ministry of Health (MOH) 2025 Kenya Nutrient Profile Model**, which provides the policy foundation for food classification and healthy dietary recommendations. To extend the system beyond food classification into comprehensive clinical nutrition decision support, the project will incorporate knowledge from the **Clinical Nutrition and Dietetics Manual (2010)** as a structured source of assessment methodologies, nutrition diagnoses, therapeutic diets, monitoring procedures, and disease-specific nutrition interventions. These sources will be treated as complementary: the MOH 2025 model supplies contemporary nutrient profiling and food guidance, while the clinical manual supplies structured reasoning processes and intervention workflows.

The resulting system is intended to support healthcare practitioners, nutritionists, dietitians, students, community health workers, and individuals seeking personalized dietary guidance. Rather than producing recommendations solely from reported symptoms, NutriLogic will implement a complete Nutrition Care Process consisting of assessment, diagnosis, intervention, monitoring, and evaluation. This approach increases clinical relevance, supports longitudinal care, and enables transparent reasoning.

The proposed architecture separates responsibilities across five major layers:

1. **Presentation Layer** – A React-based user interface providing dashboards, assessment forms, meal planning tools, progress monitoring, and explanation views.
2. **Application Layer** – Django REST services responsible for authentication, user management, orchestration, persistence, and API exposure.
3. **Knowledge Layer** – Modular Prolog knowledge bases representing nutrition knowledge, disease rules, life-stage recommendations, therapeutic diets, food ontologies, and explanation rules.
4. **Inference Layer** – A symbolic reasoning engine that performs assessment, diagnosis, recommendation generation, conflict resolution, and explanation.
5. **Learning Layer** – Future neural components responsible for image recognition, food recognition, nutrition risk prediction, natural language processing, and adaptive personalization.

The redesign proposed in this implementation plan transforms NutriLogic from a symptom-driven recommendation engine into a comprehensive Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System (CNDSS). Rather than treating nutrition as isolated food recommendations, the redesigned platform models the entire clinical reasoning process performed by trained nutrition professionals.

A central design principle throughout the project is **Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI)**. Every recommendation generated by the system must be traceable to explicit knowledge sources, logical rules, and supporting evidence. Users should be able to inspect why a recommendation was produced, which clinical observations contributed to the conclusion, and which nutritional principles or guidelines were applied during inference. This transparency promotes trust, facilitates validation by healthcare professionals, and differentiates NutriLogic from black-box recommendation systems.

Personalization constitutes another core objective of the platform. User profiles will progressively incorporate demographic, physiological, behavioural, environmental, and socioeconomic information, including age, sex, pregnancy status, anthropometric measurements, county of residence, food availability, dietary preferences, physical activity, medical history, medications, and long-term health goals. These factors will influence symbolic reasoning to produce recommendations tailored to the realities of Kenyan communities rather than generic international guidance.

The project also adopts a modular knowledge engineering strategy. Instead of maintaining a monolithic Prolog knowledge base, domain knowledge will be organised into independent modules representing diseases, life stages, nutrition assessment, diagnosis, interventions, monitoring, therapeutic diets, medications, food composition, and ontological definitions. This modular architecture improves maintainability, enables independent testing, supports collaborative development, and simplifies future expansion as national nutrition guidelines evolve.

Beyond software engineering, NutriLogic is designed as a research platform for neuro-symbolic artificial intelligence in healthcare. The system provides an environment for investigating hybrid reasoning techniques, explainable decision support, knowledge representation, ontology engineering, and machine-assisted nutrition assessment within the context of low- and middle-income countries. Its architecture is intentionally extensible, allowing future integration of wearable devices, electronic health records, laboratory information systems, food image recognition, predictive analytics, and large language models while preserving the transparency and auditability of symbolic reasoning.

Ultimately, NutriLogic seeks to demonstrate that clinically meaningful nutrition decision support can be achieved through the principled integration of symbolic knowledge, structured clinical workflows, and intelligent learning systems. By grounding recommendations in authoritative Kenyan nutrition guidance and explicit logical reasoning, the project aspires to contribute both a practical healthcare application and a reusable framework for future neuro-symbolic expert systems.

---

## 1.3 Vision Statement

To develop Kenya's leading explainable neuro-symbolic clinical nutrition decision support platform that delivers personalized, culturally appropriate, evidence-based nutrition and fitness recommendations through transparent artificial intelligence.

---

## 1.4 Mission Statement

To combine authoritative nutritional knowledge, symbolic reasoning, and intelligent learning techniques into a modular decision support system that improves nutritional assessment, clinical reasoning, patient education, and long-term health outcomes while remaining understandable, verifiable, and extensible.

---

## 1.5 Long-Term Vision

The long-term vision extends beyond the implementation of a nutrition recommendation engine. NutriLogic is intended to evolve into a comprehensive digital nutrition ecosystem capable of supporting preventive healthcare, chronic disease management, public health surveillance, nutrition education, clinical decision support, and AI research within Kenya and potentially across Sub-Saharan Africa.

Future versions may integrate wearable health devices, laboratory systems, national electronic health records, food recognition models, predictive analytics, and multilingual conversational interfaces while preserving explainability through symbolic reasoning.

# Chapter 2: Architectural Principles

## 2.1 Introduction

The architecture of NutriLogic is guided by a set of fundamental principles that govern every design decision throughout the platform. These principles provide a shared architectural philosophy that ensures consistency, maintainability, explainability, and long-term sustainability.

Unlike implementation decisions, which may evolve over time, architectural principles represent enduring design commitments that shape the evolution of the platform.

Every Architecture Decision Record (ADR) defined within this implementation plan can be traced back to one or more of these principles.

---

# 2.2 Purpose

The Architectural Principles serve to:

* Guide software evolution.
* Promote consistency.
* Improve maintainability.
* Support clinical safety.
* Enable explainable AI.
* Preserve knowledge quality.
* Encourage modular development.
* Facilitate future research.

---

# 2.3 Principle 1 — Knowledge First

**Statement**

Knowledge is the primary asset of NutriLogic.

Application logic exists to acquire, manage, reason over, and explain knowledge rather than embed domain expertise within software code.

**Implications**

* Clinical expertise resides in the knowledge base.
* Business logic orchestrates knowledge rather than replacing it.
* Knowledge evolves independently of software implementation.

---

# 2.4 Principle 2 — Separation of Knowledge and Reasoning

Knowledge and inference are independent architectural concerns.

Knowledge defines *what is known*.

Reasoning defines *how conclusions are reached.*

**Implications**

* Rule modules remain independent from inference algorithms.
* The inference engine contains no domain-specific knowledge.
* Knowledge modules remain reusable.

---

# 2.5 Principle 3 — Explainability by Design

Every recommendation generated by NutriLogic shall be explainable.

Users and clinicians must understand:

* supporting evidence,
* applied rules,
* intermediate conclusions,
* knowledge sources,
* final recommendations.

Explainability is considered a core system capability rather than an optional feature.

---

# 2.6 Principle 4 — Clinical Safety First

Patient safety takes precedence over recommendation completeness, performance, and convenience.

Safety mechanisms include:

* contraindication checking,
* allergy validation,
* conflict detection,
* evidence verification,
* conservative fallback behaviour.

No recommendation should knowingly violate established clinical constraints.

---

# 2.7 Principle 5 — Ontology-Driven Design

All semantic concepts originate from the Nutrition Ontology.

The ontology acts as the authoritative vocabulary for:

* symbolic reasoning,
* APIs,
* database mappings,
* knowledge graph,
* explanation engine.

Semantic duplication is prohibited.

---

# 2.8 Principle 6 — Semantic Consistency

Every concept must possess one canonical representation.

Duplicate terminology, inconsistent predicates, and ambiguous relationships are avoided through ontology governance.

---

# 2.9 Principle 7 — Modular Evolution

Every subsystem should evolve independently.

Modules communicate through stable interfaces.

Internal implementation changes must not affect unrelated modules.

---

# 2.10 Principle 8 — Layered Architecture

Each architectural layer has a single primary responsibility.

Presentation, application, integration, semantic reasoning, knowledge management, and infrastructure remain independent.

Dependencies flow only downward.

---

# 2.11 Principle 9 — Domain-Driven Design

Software organization reflects the nutrition domain rather than technical frameworks.

Business domains such as assessment, diagnosis, intervention, monitoring, and knowledge management own their data, services, and interfaces.

---

# 2.12 Principle 10 — Hexagonal Integration

External technologies communicate with the platform through well-defined ports and adapters.

Application services depend on abstractions rather than specific implementations of SWI-Prolog, databases, or future AI components.

---

# 2.13 Principle 11 — Governance by Design

Governance is embedded within every architectural layer.

Governance encompasses:

* knowledge stewardship,
* auditability,
* privacy,
* security,
* quality assurance,
* clinical review.

---

# 2.14 Principle 12 — Privacy by Design

Personally identifiable information shall be protected throughout its lifecycle.

Privacy considerations include:

* data minimization,
* access control,
* secure storage,
* purpose limitation,
* retention management.

---

# 2.15 Principle 13 — Interoperability

The architecture should facilitate integration with future healthcare systems.

Semantic interoperability is encouraged through:

* standardized terminology,
* ontology mappings,
* structured APIs,
* portable knowledge representations.

---

# 2.16 Principle 14 — Testability

Every architectural component should be independently testable.

Testing includes:

* unit tests,
* integration tests,
* clinical validation,
* knowledge regression,
* performance evaluation.

---

# 2.17 Principle 15 — Extensible Neuro-Symbolic Intelligence

The symbolic reasoning engine constitutes the deterministic core of NutriLogic.

Future machine learning components may augment—but never obscure or replace—the explainable symbolic reasoning process without appropriate governance and validation.

---

# 2.18 Principle 16 — Evidence-Based Decision Support

Recommendations shall be derived from validated nutrition knowledge and authoritative clinical guidance.

Knowledge sources should be traceable, versioned, and reviewable.

---

# 2.19 Principle 17 — Continuous Knowledge Evolution

The knowledge base is expected to evolve.

New:

* diseases,
* foods,
* interventions,
* biomarkers,
* nutrition guidelines,
* research findings

should be incorporated through governed knowledge engineering processes rather than ad hoc software modifications.

---

# 2.20 Principle 18 — Sustainability

The architecture shall promote long-term maintainability through modularity, documentation, version control, automated testing, and clear governance processes.

---

# 2.21 Relationship to Architecture Decision Records

Every Architecture Decision Record (ADR) within NutriLogic shall reference one or more Architectural Principles.

Illustrative mapping:

| Architectural Principle               | Representative ADRs |
| ------------------------------------- | ------------------- |
| Knowledge First                       | ADR-010, ADR-012    |
| Separation of Knowledge and Reasoning | ADR-009, ADR-013    |
| Explainability by Design              | ADR-008, ADR-009    |
| Clinical Safety First                 | ADR-014             |
| Ontology-Driven Design                | ADR-011, ADR-012    |
| Layered Architecture                  | ADR-015             |
| Hexagonal Integration                 | ADR-015             |
| Governance by Design                  | ADR-014             |

This traceability ensures architectural consistency throughout the platform lifecycle.

---

# 2.22 Chapter Summary

The Architectural Principles establish the enduring design philosophy of NutriLogic. They provide the foundation for every architectural decision, ensuring that the platform evolves consistently as a knowledge-centric, explainable, clinically oriented, neuro-symbolic decision support system. Together, these principles unify software engineering, artificial intelligence, knowledge engineering, and health informatics into a coherent architectural vision.


# Chapter 3: Background and Motivation

## 3.1 Introduction

Nutrition is one of the most significant determinants of health across the human lifespan. Appropriate nutrition supports growth, development, disease prevention, recovery from illness, and overall quality of life. Conversely, poor dietary practices contribute to undernutrition, overweight, obesity, micronutrient deficiencies, and the growing burden of non-communicable diseases (NCDs) such as diabetes mellitus, hypertension, cardiovascular disease, chronic kidney disease, and certain cancers.

Kenya, like many low- and middle-income countries, faces a **double burden of malnutrition** in which undernutrition and micronutrient deficiencies coexist with increasing rates of overweight, obesity, and diet-related chronic diseases. This presents a complex public health challenge requiring individualized, evidence-based nutrition interventions that account for demographic, physiological, socioeconomic, cultural, and environmental factors.

Despite the increasing availability of digital health applications, many nutrition recommendation systems remain generic, opaque, or poorly adapted to local dietary practices. Most commercial nutrition applications are developed using international food databases and dietary guidelines that do not adequately represent Kenyan foods, cultural eating patterns, regional food availability, or national clinical practice guidelines. Consequently, there is a need for intelligent nutrition systems specifically designed around Kenyan healthcare requirements.

NutriLogic addresses this need by combining explicit symbolic reasoning with future machine learning capabilities to produce explainable, personalized, and clinically grounded nutrition recommendations.

---

# 3.2 Background

Artificial Intelligence has transformed many areas of healthcare, including medical imaging, disease prediction, electronic health record analysis, and personalized medicine. Within nutrition, AI has primarily been applied to calorie estimation, food image recognition, dietary tracking, and recommendation systems.

However, many existing nutrition systems rely heavily on statistical learning techniques that function as "black boxes." While these models often achieve high predictive performance, they rarely explain how recommendations are generated. This lack of transparency presents significant challenges in healthcare, where recommendations must be understandable, auditable, and clinically defensible.

Healthcare professionals require systems capable of answering questions such as:

* Why was this recommendation generated?
* Which clinical findings influenced the decision?
* Which nutritional guideline supports this advice?
* Which patient characteristics affected the recommendation?
* What evidence contradicts alternative recommendations?

Traditional expert systems remain particularly valuable in domains where expert knowledge can be represented explicitly as logical rules. Clinical nutrition is one such domain because much of the reasoning performed by dietitians follows structured assessment procedures, evidence-based guidelines, therapeutic protocols, and standardized care pathways.

Rather than replacing symbolic reasoning with purely statistical methods, recent advances in Artificial Intelligence have renewed interest in **Neuro-Symbolic AI**, which integrates machine learning with symbolic knowledge representation. This hybrid paradigm seeks to combine the perceptual strengths of neural networks with the transparency, logical consistency, and explainability of symbolic inference.

NutriLogic adopts this philosophy by positioning symbolic reasoning as the authoritative decision-making layer while reserving neural methods for future perception and prediction tasks.

---

# 3.3 Motivation

The motivation for NutriLogic arises from several practical and research challenges.

## 3.3.1 Limited Personalization

Many existing nutrition applications generate recommendations using only a small subset of user information, often limited to age, weight, or calorie targets. Clinical nutrition, however, requires consideration of numerous interacting factors, including:

* Life stage
* Pregnancy status
* Lactation
* Medical history
* Current medications
* Anthropometric measurements
* Dietary habits
* Physical activity
* Socioeconomic circumstances
* Food accessibility
* Cultural dietary practices
* Long-term health objectives

A recommendation that is appropriate for one individual may be unsuitable—or even harmful—for another with different clinical characteristics. Personalized reasoning therefore requires explicit modelling of these contextual factors.

---

## 3.3.2 Need for Explainability

Clinical recommendations must be transparent.

Healthcare professionals need systems that can justify every recommendation through evidence and logical reasoning rather than statistical probability alone.

For example, instead of simply recommending increased vegetable consumption, an explainable system should identify:

* the nutritional assessment findings,
* the inferred nutrition diagnosis,
* the relevant therapeutic guideline,
* the applicable dietary principle,
* and the reasoning steps leading to the recommendation.

Explainability promotes trust, supports professional validation, facilitates education, and improves accountability in clinical decision support systems.

---

## 3.3.3 Kenyan Context

Most nutrition applications are designed for high-income countries and frequently rely on foreign food composition databases, international dietary guidelines, and culturally unfamiliar meal recommendations.

NutriLogic instead places Kenyan healthcare knowledge at the centre of its reasoning process.

The system is grounded in:

* the Kenya Ministry of Health 2025 Kenya Nutrient Profile Model for food classification and nutrient profiling;
* Kenyan clinical nutrition practices documented in the Clinical Nutrition and Dietetics Manual;
* locally consumed foods;
* culturally relevant dietary practices;
* regional food availability;
* and public health priorities specific to Kenya.

This localisation aims to improve both recommendation quality and user acceptance.

---

## 3.3.4 Knowledge Preservation

Clinical nutrition expertise is often distributed across textbooks, policy documents, treatment guidelines, and experienced practitioners.

Without structured representation, much of this knowledge remains inaccessible to computational systems.

NutriLogic seeks to preserve this expertise by transforming clinical nutrition knowledge into formal symbolic representations consisting of facts, logical rules, ontologies, and inference mechanisms.

This process supports knowledge preservation, reuse, maintenance, and continuous improvement.

---

## 3.3.5 Research Opportunity

Neuro-symbolic Artificial Intelligence remains an active research area.

Clinical nutrition presents an ideal application because it combines:

* structured expert knowledge,
* complex reasoning,
* uncertainty,
* personalization,
* longitudinal monitoring,
* and increasing availability of health data.

NutriLogic therefore serves not only as a software application but also as an experimental platform for investigating explainable hybrid reasoning systems in healthcare.

---

# 3.4 Why a Neuro-Symbolic Architecture?

Purely symbolic systems possess excellent reasoning capabilities but struggle with perception tasks such as recognising foods from images or identifying behavioural patterns from large datasets.

Conversely, purely neural systems excel at perception and prediction but generally lack explicit reasoning, transparency, and verifiable decision pathways.

NutriLogic combines the strengths of both paradigms.

The symbolic component provides:

* explicit knowledge representation,
* logical inference,
* deterministic reasoning,
* rule validation,
* explanation generation,
* guideline compliance,
* and clinical traceability.

Future neural components will provide:

* food image recognition,
* nutrition risk prediction,
* meal estimation,
* behavioural pattern recognition,
* recommendation ranking,
* natural language understanding,
* and adaptive personalization.

The symbolic reasoning engine remains the authoritative decision-making component, ensuring that neural predictions are interpreted within established clinical guidelines.

---

# 3.5 Guiding Design Principles

The architecture of NutriLogic is governed by the following principles:

1. **Clinical correctness before computational complexity.**
2. **Explainability before automation.**
3. **Personalization through structured reasoning.**
4. **Modular and maintainable knowledge engineering.**
5. **Evidence-based recommendations grounded in Kenyan guidance.**
6. **Separation of knowledge, inference, application logic, and presentation.**
7. **Extensibility for future machine learning integration.**
8. **Support for continuous knowledge evolution as national guidelines change.**
9. **Reproducible and testable inference.**
10. **Human-centred design for both clinicians and the public.**

---

# 3.6 Chapter Summary

This chapter has established the public health, technological, and research motivations underlying the development of NutriLogic. It argues that effective nutrition decision support requires more than generic recommendation algorithms: it requires structured clinical reasoning, explainable inference, personalization, and localisation to the Kenyan healthcare context.

These motivations directly inform the architectural decisions presented in subsequent chapters, where the system is redesigned as a modular neuro-symbolic Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System capable of integrating symbolic knowledge with future intelligent learning components.

# Chapter 4: Problem Statement, Research Gap, System Objectives, and Architectural Decisions

---

# 4.1 Introduction

Having established the motivation for NutriLogic, this chapter formally defines the problem the project seeks to solve. It identifies the limitations of existing nutrition decision support systems, establishes the research and engineering gaps, defines the project objectives, and records the first architectural decisions that will guide implementation.

The purpose of this chapter is to translate the project's vision into measurable engineering goals.

---

# 4.2 Problem Statement

Nutrition-related diseases are increasing in prevalence across Kenya while access to individualized nutrition counselling remains limited. Professional dietitians and nutritionists are concentrated in urban healthcare facilities, leaving many individuals without timely access to evidence-based dietary guidance.

Although numerous nutrition applications exist, most exhibit one or more of the following limitations:

* Recommendations are generic rather than individualized.
* Clinical reasoning is hidden from users.
* Recommendations cannot be audited or explained.
* Local Kenyan foods are poorly represented.
* National nutrition guidelines are not incorporated.
* Recommendations are generated without structured nutritional assessment.
* Disease-specific nutrition therapy is often simplified or omitted.
* Longitudinal monitoring and follow-up are limited.
* Knowledge cannot easily be updated as clinical guidelines evolve.

Consequently, existing systems frequently function as calorie calculators or meal trackers rather than comprehensive Clinical Nutrition Decision Support Systems (CNDSS).

NutriLogic addresses this gap by providing a modular neuro-symbolic expert system capable of explicit reasoning, personalized assessment, explainable recommendations, and knowledge-driven clinical decision support grounded in Kenyan nutrition guidance.

---

# 4.3 Current System Assessment

The existing implementation provides an excellent foundation but remains an early-stage expert system.

## Current Strengths

The current architecture already demonstrates several important strengths:

* Separation between frontend, backend, and inference engine.
* SWI-Prolog integration through PySWIP.
* RESTful API architecture.
* JWT authentication.
* Recommendation history.
* Shared domain vocabulary.
* Modular service layer.
* Integration of the MOH 2025 Kenya Nutrient Profile Model.
* Explainable symbolic inference.
* Expandable knowledge base.

These design choices significantly reduce future implementation effort.

---

## Current Limitations

However, several important limitations remain.

### Limited User Personalization

Although user profiles are stored, profile attributes currently have minimal influence on inference.

Recommendations primarily depend upon:

* symptoms
* conditions

rather than comprehensive patient context.

---

### Simplified Clinical Workflow

Current reasoning follows approximately:

```text
Condition
        ↓
Prolog Rules
        ↓
Recommendation
```

Real clinical nutrition practice follows a richer process:

```text
Patient
      ↓
Nutrition Assessment
      ↓
Nutrition Diagnosis
      ↓
Nutrition Intervention
      ↓
Monitoring
      ↓
Evaluation
```

Supporting this workflow is one of the primary objectives of the redesign.

---

### Monolithic Knowledge Base

Current knowledge is concentrated within a single Prolog file.

As knowledge expands to include:

* pregnancy
* diabetes
* hypertension
* HIV
* chronic kidney disease
* obesity
* paediatric nutrition
* geriatric nutrition

the current organisation becomes increasingly difficult to maintain.

---

### Limited Explainability

Although symbolic reasoning is inherently explainable, current explanations are largely implicit.

Future recommendations should include:

* evidence used
* rules triggered
* diagnoses inferred
* contraindications considered
* alternative recommendations rejected
* confidence and supporting rationale

---

### Limited Longitudinal Care

Recommendation history is stored.

However, the system currently lacks:

* follow-up assessment
* progress tracking
* behavioural monitoring
* adherence evaluation
* outcome measurement

These capabilities are essential for nutrition care.

---

# 4.4 Research Gap

Most existing AI-based nutrition systems emphasize prediction rather than reasoning.

Conversely, traditional expert systems often lack:

* personalization,
* modern web architecture,
* extensibility,
* machine learning integration,
* user-centred interfaces.

There remains an opportunity to develop a system that combines:

* explicit symbolic reasoning,
* modular knowledge engineering,
* explainable AI,
* personalized nutrition,
* Kenyan clinical guidelines,
* future neural intelligence,

within a unified architecture.

NutriLogic addresses this gap through a hybrid neuro-symbolic approach in which symbolic reasoning remains the authoritative decision-making mechanism while neural models augment perception and prediction.

---

# 4.5 Research Questions

The project is guided by the following research questions.

### RQ1

How can symbolic reasoning be used to produce explainable personalized nutrition recommendations for Kenyan populations?

---

### RQ2

How can Kenyan nutrition guidelines be represented as modular symbolic knowledge suitable for automated inference?

---

### RQ3

How can structured clinical nutrition reasoning be modelled computationally using the Nutrition Care Process?

---

### RQ4

How can neural prediction models complement symbolic reasoning without compromising explainability?

---

### RQ5

How can modular knowledge engineering simplify long-term maintenance of nutrition expert systems?

---

# 4.6 Project Objectives

## General Objective

To design and implement a modular hybrid neuro-symbolic Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System that delivers personalized, explainable, and evidence-based nutrition recommendations for Kenya.

---

## Specific Objectives

The project seeks to:

1. Develop a structured nutrition assessment engine.

2. Implement symbolic nutrition diagnosis.

3. Generate individualized therapeutic interventions.

4. Produce explainable recommendations.

5. Model Kenyan nutrition knowledge using modular Prolog knowledge bases.

6. Personalize recommendations using demographic, clinical, behavioural, and environmental information.

7. Support monitoring and longitudinal nutrition care.

8. Enable future integration of neural learning models.

9. Preserve transparency throughout the inference process.

10. Provide an extensible architecture for future clinical modules.

---

# 4.7 Functional Objectives

The completed system shall support:

* User registration and authentication.
* Profile management.
* Nutrition assessment.
* Nutrition diagnosis.
* Disease-specific reasoning.
* Meal planning.
* Therapeutic diet generation.
* Physical activity recommendations.
* Behaviour change guidance.
* Progress monitoring.
* Recommendation history.
* Clinical explanations.
* Administrative knowledge management.

---

# 4.8 Non-Functional Objectives

The system shall satisfy the following quality attributes.

## Maintainability

Knowledge modules should be independently maintainable.

---

## Explainability

Every recommendation must be traceable to explicit reasoning.

---

## Scalability

The architecture should support new diseases, foods, and clinical guidelines without major redesign.

---

## Reliability

Inference should produce deterministic and reproducible results given identical inputs.

---

## Extensibility

Future machine learning modules should integrate without modifying symbolic reasoning.

---

## Security

Patient information shall be protected through authentication, authorization, and secure communication.

---

# 4.9 Success Criteria

The redesigned system will be considered successful if it demonstrates:

* Modular knowledge organisation.
* Comprehensive nutrition assessment.
* Nutrition diagnosis before recommendation.
* Explainable inference chains.
* Personalized recommendations.
* Disease-specific therapeutic reasoning.
* Longitudinal monitoring.
* Extensible architecture.
* Integration readiness for machine learning.
* Compliance with Kenyan nutrition guidance.

---

# 4.10 Architecture Decision Records (ADR)

The following Architectural Decision Records capture foundational design choices. Each ADR documents the context, decision, rationale, trade-offs, and implementation guidance.

---

## ADR-001: Adopt a Modular Prolog Knowledge Base

### Status

Accepted

### Context

The current system stores symbolic knowledge in a single `kb.pl` file. While appropriate for an initial prototype, this approach becomes increasingly difficult to maintain as new diseases, life stages, therapeutic diets, assessment methods, and monitoring rules are added.

### Decision

The knowledge base shall be reorganized into independent, domain-specific modules (e.g., `assessment/`, `diagnosis/`, `diseases/`, `interventions/`, `monitoring/`, `ontology/`) loaded through a central bootstrap file.

### Rationale

* Improves maintainability.
* Enables independent testing.
* Supports incremental development.
* Simplifies knowledge acquisition.
* Reduces merge conflicts during collaborative development.

### Trade-offs

* Introduces a module-loading mechanism.
* Requires careful dependency management.

### Implementation Notes

Create a root loader (`knowledge/load.pl`) responsible for importing modules in dependency order and exposing a stable public interface to the Django bridge.

---

## ADR-002: Separate Knowledge from Application Logic

### Status

Accepted

### Context

Business logic, clinical knowledge, and infrastructure concerns evolve at different rates. Mixing them increases maintenance cost and complicates validation.

### Decision

Clinical knowledge shall reside exclusively within the symbolic knowledge layer. Django services will orchestrate requests, persist data, and interface with the inference engine, but they will not encode clinical rules.

### Rationale

* Preserves a single source of truth for clinical reasoning.
* Simplifies guideline updates.
* Facilitates knowledge review by domain experts.
* Improves testability.

### Consequences

Changes to nutrition guidelines require updates only to the knowledge layer, minimizing risk to the application layer.

---

## ADR-003: Model the Nutrition Care Process Explicitly

### Status

Accepted

### Context

The current prototype maps symptoms directly to recommendations, omitting intermediate clinical reasoning.

### Decision

The system shall model the Nutrition Care Process explicitly:

1. Assessment
2. Diagnosis
3. Intervention
4. Monitoring
5. Evaluation

Each stage will produce structured outputs that become inputs to the next stage.

### Rationale

* Aligns the system with established clinical workflows.
* Improves explainability.
* Supports longitudinal care.
* Enables richer auditing and validation.

### Implementation Notes

Represent intermediate outputs (e.g., nutrition diagnoses, identified risks, therapeutic goals) as explicit symbolic facts that can be persisted, explained, and monitored over time.

---

This chapter has translated the project's vision into a concrete engineering problem, identified the limitations of the current prototype, articulated the research questions and objectives, and established the first architectural decisions that will guide the remainder of the implementation.

The next chapter will define the complete **System Requirements Specification (SRS)**, including functional, non-functional, clinical, regulatory, data, usability, and interoperability requirements. These requirements will serve as the traceable foundation for the architecture and implementation described in subsequent chapters.

# Chapter 5: System Requirements Specification (SRS)

## 5.1 Introduction

This chapter defines the complete Software Requirements Specification (SRS) for NutriLogic. It establishes the functional, non-functional, clinical, security, interoperability, and knowledge engineering requirements that govern the design and implementation of the system.

The requirements presented herein provide the contractual foundation for all subsequent architectural decisions, database models, knowledge engineering activities, user interface design, testing, and deployment.

Each requirement is uniquely identified to support traceability throughout the development lifecycle.

---

# 5.2 System Overview

NutriLogic is a web-based Hybrid Neuro-Symbolic Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System (CNDSS) that assists users in obtaining personalized nutrition and fitness recommendations through explicit symbolic reasoning.

The system consists of five primary subsystems:

* Presentation Layer (React)
* Application Layer (Django REST)
* Knowledge Layer (SWI-Prolog)
* Inference Layer
* Persistence Layer (PostgreSQL)

Future releases will introduce a sixth subsystem:

* Neural Intelligence Layer

---

# 5.3 Stakeholders

The primary stakeholders include:

## Patients / General Users

Responsibilities

* Register accounts
* Complete nutrition assessments
* Receive recommendations
* Monitor progress
* View explanations

---

## Nutritionists and Dietitians

Responsibilities

* Review recommendations
* Validate inference
* Monitor patient progress
* Update nutrition goals
* Provide counselling

---

## Healthcare Professionals

Responsibilities

* Interpret clinical reports
* Integrate recommendations into treatment plans
* Monitor nutrition interventions

---

## Knowledge Engineers

Responsibilities

* Encode clinical knowledge
* Validate symbolic rules
* Maintain ontologies
* Update guidelines

---

## System Administrators

Responsibilities

* User management
* Security
* Knowledge deployment
* Monitoring
* Backup

---

# 5.4 Functional Requirements

Each functional requirement is assigned a unique identifier.

---

## FR-001 User Registration

The system shall allow new users to create accounts.

Priority

High

---

## FR-002 Authentication

The system shall authenticate users using JWT authentication.

Priority

High

---

## FR-003 Profile Management

The system shall maintain comprehensive user profiles including:

* age
* sex
* height
* weight
* BMI
* county
* pregnancy status
* activity level
* dietary preferences
* allergies
* chronic diseases
* medications
* health goals

Priority

High

---

## FR-004 Nutrition Assessment

The system shall conduct structured nutrition assessments consisting of:

* Anthropometric assessment
* Clinical assessment
* Dietary assessment
* Lifestyle assessment
* Medical assessment
* Behavioural assessment

Output:

Structured assessment facts.

---

## FR-005 Nutrition Diagnosis

The system shall infer nutrition diagnoses from assessment findings.

Examples

* Underweight
* Obesity risk
* Iron deficiency risk
* High sodium intake
* Poor fibre intake
* Protein-energy malnutrition risk

---

## FR-006 Therapeutic Recommendation

The system shall generate evidence-based recommendations including:

* Foods
* Meal plans
* Therapeutic diets
* Supplements
* Physical activity
* Behaviour change advice

---

## FR-007 Explainability

Every recommendation shall include:

* triggered rules
* supporting evidence
* inferred diagnosis
* recommendation rationale
* contraindications considered

---

## FR-008 Meal Planning

The system shall generate culturally appropriate meal plans using Kenyan foods.

Meal plans shall consider:

* nutrient requirements
* food availability
* affordability
* preferences
* allergies
* therapeutic restrictions

---

## FR-009 Monitoring

The system shall monitor:

* weight
* BMI
* adherence
* nutrition goals
* recommendation history
* health progress

---

## FR-010 Follow-up

The system shall schedule follow-up assessments.

---

## FR-011 Recommendation History

The system shall maintain complete recommendation history.

---

## FR-012 Behaviour Change Support

The system shall assist users through:

* SMART goals
* reminders
* education
* progress tracking

---

## FR-013 Clinical Reports

The system shall generate downloadable nutrition reports summarizing:

* assessment
* diagnosis
* interventions
* monitoring
* recommendations

---

## FR-014 Knowledge Base Management

Authorized users shall be able to update symbolic knowledge without modifying application logic.

---

## FR-015 Future Machine Learning Integration

The architecture shall expose interfaces allowing future neural components to submit symbolic facts into the inference engine.

---

# 5.5 Clinical Requirements

The system shall support:

* Nutrition Care Process
* Nutrition Assessment
* Nutrition Diagnosis
* Nutrition Intervention
* Monitoring
* Evaluation

The system shall support disease-specific nutrition management including:

* Diabetes
* Hypertension
* Obesity
* HIV/AIDS
* Tuberculosis
* Pregnancy
* Lactation
* Paediatric nutrition
* Geriatric nutrition

Additional conditions may be incorporated through modular knowledge expansion.

---

# 5.6 Knowledge Engineering Requirements

The symbolic knowledge base shall support:

* modular rule organisation
* ontology-driven reasoning
* inheritance
* explanation generation
* knowledge versioning
* conflict resolution
* rule testing

The knowledge layer shall remain independent from application logic.

---

# 5.7 Data Requirements

The system shall persist:

## User Data

* demographics
* authentication
* preferences

---

## Clinical Data

* assessments
* diagnoses
* medications
* biomarkers
* allergies

---

## Nutrition Data

* meal plans
* recommendations
* dietary intake
* food journal

---

## Monitoring Data

* weight history
* BMI history
* adherence
* goals
* progress

---

## Knowledge Data

* symbolic rules
* ontologies
* clinical guidelines
* food classifications

---

# 5.8 Non-Functional Requirements

## Performance

* Recommendation generation ≤ 2 seconds under normal load.
* API response time ≤ 500 ms for non-inference endpoints.
* Concurrent user support scalable through horizontal application-layer scaling.

---

## Reliability

The inference engine shall produce deterministic results given identical inputs and knowledge versions.

---

## Availability

The production system should target 99.5% availability, excluding scheduled maintenance.

---

## Maintainability

Clinical knowledge shall be maintainable independently of application code.

---

## Scalability

The architecture shall support:

* new diseases
* new foods
* new assessment modules
* new knowledge sources
* future ML components

without redesigning the core system.

---

## Explainability

Every inference shall be reproducible and traceable.

No recommendation shall be presented without an explanation pathway.

---

## Security

The system shall implement:

* JWT authentication
* HTTPS
* password hashing
* role-based authorization
* audit logging
* encrypted sensitive data at rest where applicable

---

## Privacy

The system shall minimize collection of personally identifiable information and support user consent for storing health-related data.

---

# 5.9 External Interfaces

## User Interface

React SPA

Responsive

Accessible

Mobile-friendly

---

## API

REST API

JSON

JWT Authentication

Versioned endpoints

---

## Knowledge Interface

PySWIP

SWI-Prolog

Knowledge loader

---

## Database

PostgreSQL

Future support:

Redis

Object storage

Analytics warehouse

---

# 5.10 Constraints

The implementation shall:

* remain open-source
* support offline knowledge development
* separate knowledge from infrastructure
* preserve explainability
* use modular symbolic reasoning
* support future neural augmentation

---

# 5.11 Assumptions

The following assumptions guide system design:

* Clinical guidelines evolve over time.
* Users may have incomplete information.
* Internet connectivity may be intermittent.
* Recommendations supplement—not replace—professional medical advice.
* Knowledge modules can be updated independently.

---

# 5.12 Requirement Traceability

Every major architectural component shall map to one or more requirements.

| Requirement Category  | Primary Architecture Component |
| --------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| User Management       | Django Authentication Services |
| Nutrition Assessment  | Assessment Engine              |
| Diagnosis             | Symbolic Inference Engine      |
| Recommendations       | Intervention Engine            |
| Monitoring            | Monitoring Module              |
| Explainability        | Explanation Engine             |
| Knowledge Maintenance | Modular Prolog Knowledge Base  |
| Future AI             | Neural Integration Layer       |

---

# 5.13 Acceptance Criteria

The system shall be considered compliant with this specification when it demonstrates:

* Successful execution of the complete Nutrition Care Process.
* Personalized recommendations using profile and assessment data.
* Explainable inference chains for every recommendation.
* Modular, independently testable knowledge bases.
* Generation of Kenyan-context meal plans.
* Persistent monitoring and follow-up.
* Successful integration between React, Django, and SWI-Prolog.
* Readiness for future neural module integration.

---

# 5.14 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes the complete requirements specification for NutriLogic. These requirements define the capabilities, constraints, quality attributes, and architectural expectations of the system and provide a traceable foundation for all subsequent design and implementation activities.

The next chapter translates these requirements into the overall system architecture, defining how the presentation, application, inference, knowledge, persistence, and future neural layers collaborate to implement the requirements specified herein.

# Chapter 6: System Architecture

## 6.1 Introduction

This chapter defines the logical, physical, and functional architecture of NutriLogic. It describes how the system components collaborate to implement the requirements defined in Chapter 4 while preserving modularity, explainability, maintainability, and extensibility.

Rather than viewing NutriLogic as a traditional web application, this design treats it as a **Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System (CNDSS)** built around the Nutrition Care Process (NCP). The architecture separates concerns into distinct layers so that user interfaces, application logic, clinical knowledge, inference, and future machine learning components evolve independently.

The architecture follows the principles established in the Architecture Decision Records (ADRs) introduced in Chapter 3, particularly the separation of clinical knowledge from application logic and the adoption of a modular symbolic knowledge base.

---

# 6.2 Architectural Goals

The architecture is designed to satisfy the following goals:

* Explicit and explainable clinical reasoning.
* Modular knowledge engineering.
* Personalization using comprehensive user profiles.
* Scalability for future diseases, foods, and interventions.
* Independence between clinical knowledge and infrastructure.
* Support for hybrid neuro-symbolic reasoning.
* Maintainability through clear separation of concerns.
* Integration with future machine learning components.

---

# 6.3 Architectural Style

NutriLogic adopts a layered architecture combined with domain-driven design principles.

The major architectural layers are:

```text
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
|                  Presentation Layer (React)                 |
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
|             Application Layer (Django REST API)             |
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
|          Clinical Orchestration & Service Layer             |
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
|        Symbolic Inference Layer (SWI-Prolog Engine)         |
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
|          Knowledge Layer (Modular Prolog Modules)           |
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
|        Persistence Layer (PostgreSQL / Future Redis)        |
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
|      Future Neural Intelligence Layer (ML Components)       |
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
```

Each layer has clearly defined responsibilities and communicates through stable interfaces.

---

# 6.4 High-Level System Workflow

The overall workflow follows the Nutrition Care Process rather than a simple request-response model.

```text
User
 │
 ▼
Authentication
 │
 ▼
Profile Retrieval
 │
 ▼
Nutrition Assessment
 │
 ▼
Assessment Facts
 │
 ▼
Symbolic Diagnosis
 │
 ▼
Therapeutic Reasoning
 │
 ▼
Recommendation Generation
 │
 ▼
Explanation Generation
 │
 ▼
Persistence
 │
 ▼
Monitoring & Follow-up
```

Each stage produces structured outputs that become inputs to the next stage, ensuring traceability throughout the reasoning process.

---

# 6.5 Presentation Layer

The Presentation Layer is implemented as a React Single Page Application (SPA).

Its primary responsibilities include:

* User authentication.
* Profile management.
* Nutrition assessment forms.
* Meal planning interface.
* Progress dashboards.
* Recommendation visualization.
* Explanation views.
* Clinical report generation.

Importantly, this layer contains **no clinical reasoning logic**. All decisions originate from the symbolic inference engine.

---

# 6.6 Application Layer

The Application Layer is implemented using Django REST Framework.

Responsibilities include:

* Authentication and authorization.
* API routing.
* Input validation.
* Data persistence.
* Session management.
* Request orchestration.
* Communication with the inference engine.
* Report generation.

The Application Layer acts as the coordinator between the user interface, persistence layer, and symbolic reasoning engine.

---

# 6.7 Clinical Orchestration Layer

A dedicated orchestration layer coordinates the complete Nutrition Care Process.

Its responsibilities include:

1. Validating assessment completeness.
2. Transforming user data into symbolic facts.
3. Invoking Prolog inference.
4. Collecting diagnoses.
5. Requesting intervention planning.
6. Generating explanations.
7. Persisting results.
8. Scheduling monitoring activities.

This layer deliberately contains **workflow logic**, not **clinical knowledge**.

---

# 6.8 Symbolic Inference Layer

The Symbolic Inference Layer is the core decision-making component.

Responsibilities include:

* Nutrition assessment reasoning.
* Nutrition diagnosis.
* Therapeutic intervention planning.
* Meal recommendation.
* Behaviour change reasoning.
* Contraindication detection.
* Conflict resolution.
* Explanation generation.

The inference engine executes deterministic logical reasoning using SWI-Prolog.

Unlike machine learning systems, every inference can be reproduced and explained.

---

# 6.9 Knowledge Layer

The Knowledge Layer contains all nutrition expertise.

Knowledge is organised into modular Prolog packages.

```text
knowledge/

    ontology/
        foods.pl
        nutrients.pl
        diseases.pl
        symptoms.pl
        medications.pl

    assessment/
        anthropometry.pl
        dietary.pl
        lifestyle.pl
        biochemical.pl

    diagnosis/
        nutrition_diagnosis.pl
        risk_scoring.pl

    interventions/
        therapeutic_diets.pl
        meal_plans.pl
        supplements.pl
        physical_activity.pl

    diseases/
        diabetes.pl
        hypertension.pl
        obesity.pl
        hiv.pl
        tb.pl
        pregnancy.pl
        anaemia.pl

    monitoring/
        follow_up.pl
        goal_tracking.pl

    explanations/
        explanation_rules.pl

    utilities/
        certainty.pl
        validation.pl

    load.pl
```

This modular structure supports independent maintenance, testing, and versioning.

---

# 6.10 Persistence Layer

Persistent data are managed by PostgreSQL.

The persistence layer stores:

* User accounts.
* Profiles.
* Assessments.
* Diagnoses.
* Recommendations.
* Meal plans.
* Monitoring records.
* Behaviour goals.
* Recommendation history.
* Audit logs.

Clinical knowledge is **not stored** in the relational database.

Instead, symbolic knowledge remains within the Prolog knowledge modules.

---

# 6.11 Neural Intelligence Layer (Future)

The Neural Intelligence Layer will complement symbolic reasoning without replacing it.

Planned components include:

* Food image recognition.
* Meal portion estimation.
* Nutrition risk prediction.
* Physical activity recognition.
* Natural language meal logging.
* Recommendation ranking.

Neural models will never produce final recommendations directly.

Instead, they generate observations that are translated into symbolic facts.

Example:

```text
CNN

↓

"Likely Ugali"

↓

food_detected(ugali).

↓

Prolog reasoning

↓

Clinical recommendation
```

This preserves explainability while benefiting from machine learning.

---

# 6.12 Information Flow

The architecture separates information flow into two categories.

## Operational Flow

User → Django → Prolog → Django → React

## Knowledge Flow

Clinical Guideline

↓

Knowledge Engineering

↓

Prolog Rules

↓

Inference Engine

↓

Recommendations

Knowledge updates occur independently from software deployment whenever possible.

---

# 6.13 Separation of Responsibilities

| Layer          | Responsibility              | Must NOT Do                   |
| -------------- | --------------------------- | ----------------------------- |
| React          | User interaction            | Clinical reasoning            |
| Django         | Orchestration & persistence | Encode nutrition rules        |
| Prolog Engine  | Logical inference           | Store user sessions           |
| Knowledge Base | Clinical knowledge          | Handle HTTP requests          |
| Database       | Persistent records          | Contain reasoning rules       |
| ML Layer       | Prediction & perception     | Make final clinical decisions |

This separation minimizes coupling and simplifies maintenance.

---

# 6.14 Architectural Quality Attributes

The architecture is designed to achieve:

* Explainability
* Maintainability
* Modularity
* Extensibility
* Testability
* Reproducibility
* Scalability
* Security
* Clinical traceability

Each architectural decision can be traced to one or more requirements defined in Chapter 4.

---

# 6.15 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-004: Adopt a Layered Neuro-Symbolic Architecture

### Status

Accepted

### Context

The system must integrate modern web technologies, deterministic symbolic reasoning, and future machine learning while preserving explainability and maintainability.

### Decision

Adopt a layered architecture that separates presentation, application, orchestration, inference, knowledge, persistence, and neural intelligence into independent layers with well-defined interfaces.

### Rationale

* Minimizes coupling.
* Supports modular development.
* Simplifies testing.
* Preserves clinical reasoning.
* Enables independent evolution of knowledge and infrastructure.
* Facilitates future ML integration.

### Trade-offs

* Slightly increased architectural complexity.
* Additional coordination between layers.
* Greater initial design effort.

These trade-offs are justified by improved scalability, maintainability, and long-term flexibility.

---

# 6.16 Chapter Summary

This chapter defines the overall architecture of NutriLogic as a layered neuro-symbolic Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System. It establishes the responsibilities of each architectural layer, defines the flow of information and knowledge through the system, and records the architectural decisions that preserve modularity, explainability, and extensibility.

Subsequent chapters will refine each architectural component in detail, beginning with the Knowledge Engineering Framework, which specifies how clinical nutrition expertise is represented, validated, versioned, and maintained within the symbolic knowledge base.

# Chapter 7: Knowledge Engineering Framework

## 7.1 Introduction

Knowledge is the most valuable asset of any expert system. Unlike conventional software, where functionality is primarily implemented through procedural code, the behaviour of NutriLogic is largely determined by the quality, structure, and integrity of its knowledge base.

This chapter defines the methodology for acquiring, modelling, validating, implementing, maintaining, and evolving clinical nutrition knowledge. It establishes the processes that transform authoritative nutrition guidelines into executable symbolic rules while preserving clinical correctness, explainability, and maintainability.

The framework is designed to ensure that NutriLogic remains adaptable as nutritional science, Kenyan Ministry of Health policies, and clinical practice guidelines evolve.

---

# 7.2 Knowledge Engineering Objectives

The Knowledge Engineering Framework pursues the following objectives:

1. Capture authoritative nutrition knowledge in a structured form.
2. Represent clinical reasoning explicitly using symbolic logic.
3. Preserve traceability between recommendations and their knowledge sources.
4. Separate knowledge from application logic.
5. Support modular expansion of the knowledge base.
6. Enable independent validation of rules.
7. Facilitate continuous knowledge maintenance.
8. Provide a foundation for explainable reasoning.
9. Support integration with future machine learning models.

---

# 7.3 Knowledge Sources

Knowledge incorporated into NutriLogic shall originate from authoritative, evidence-based sources.

The primary sources are:

## Primary Sources

* Kenya Ministry of Health 2025 Kenya Nutrient Profile Model.
* Clinical Nutrition and Dietetics Manual (2010).

These documents provide the core symbolic knowledge used for food classification, nutrition assessment, therapeutic diets, disease management, and clinical workflows.

---

## Secondary Sources

Future versions may incorporate additional sources such as:

* Kenya Food Composition Tables.
* National Clinical Guidelines.
* WHO nutrition recommendations.
* Peer-reviewed nutrition research.
* Domain expert validation workshops.

Any secondary source must undergo formal review before incorporation into the knowledge base.

---

# 7.4 Knowledge Acquisition Process

Knowledge acquisition is the process of transforming narrative clinical guidance into structured symbolic representations.

The proposed workflow is:

```text
Clinical Guideline
        ↓
Domain Analysis
        ↓
Concept Extraction
        ↓
Ontology Construction
        ↓
Fact Identification
        ↓
Rule Identification
        ↓
Constraint Identification
        ↓
Rule Formalization
        ↓
Validation
        ↓
Knowledge Base Integration
```

Each step shall be documented to preserve traceability and support future audits.

---

# 7.5 Knowledge Representation

Clinical knowledge shall be represented using several complementary constructs.

## Facts

Facts describe observations or immutable truths.

Examples:

```prolog
age_group(adult).

condition(diabetes).

pregnancy_status(second_trimester).

food(omena).

contains(omena, calcium).
```

Facts represent the current state of the user or the nutrition domain.

---

## Rules

Rules capture expert reasoning.

Example:

```prolog
condition(diabetes),
high_added_sugar_intake
    ->
recommend(low_glycemic_meals).
```

Rules are the primary mechanism through which recommendations are generated.

---

## Constraints

Constraints prevent unsafe recommendations.

Example:

```prolog
pregnant(User),
contains(food_x, alcohol)
    ->
reject(food_x).
```

Constraints have higher priority than ordinary recommendation rules.

---

## Meta-Rules

Meta-rules govern reasoning itself.

Examples include:

* conflict resolution,
* rule prioritization,
* explanation generation,
* certainty handling.

These rules determine how the inference engine behaves rather than what it concludes.

---

# 7.6 Nutrition Ontology

The symbolic knowledge base will be organised around a formal ontology.

Major ontology domains include:

```text
Person

Lifestyle

Assessment

Diagnosis

Disease

Food

Food Group

Nutrient

Meal

Therapeutic Diet

Medication

Allergy

Biomarker

Physical Activity

Behaviour Goal

Monitoring

Recommendation

Explanation
```

Relationships among these concepts will be explicitly modelled.

Examples:

```text
Food
 ├── contains → Nutrient
 ├── belongs_to → Food Group
 ├── contraindicated_for → Disease
 └── recommended_for → Life Stage

Diagnosis
 ├── derived_from → Assessment
 ├── managed_by → Intervention
 └── monitored_by → Follow-up
```

This ontology provides the semantic backbone for reasoning.

---

# 7.7 Knowledge Granularity

Knowledge shall be represented at multiple levels.

### Level 1

Atomic facts.

Example:

```prolog
bmi(32).
```

---

### Level 2

Derived observations.

```prolog
obese(User).
```

---

### Level 3

Nutrition diagnoses.

```prolog
nutrition_diagnosis(obesity).
```

---

### Level 4

Interventions.

```prolog
recommend(calorie_restriction).
```

---

### Level 5

Behaviour plans.

```prolog
goal(weight_loss).
```

Higher-level conclusions are always derived from lower-level evidence.

---

# 7.8 Rule Classification

Rules will be organised into categories.

## Assessment Rules

Transform observations into findings.

---

## Diagnostic Rules

Infer nutrition diagnoses.

---

## Intervention Rules

Generate recommendations.

---

## Contraindication Rules

Identify unsafe recommendations.

---

## Monitoring Rules

Evaluate progress over time.

---

## Explanation Rules

Generate human-readable reasoning.

---

## Behaviour Rules

Recommend behavioural modifications.

---

## Meta-Rules

Control inference execution.

---

# 7.9 Rule Lifecycle

Every symbolic rule progresses through a defined lifecycle.

```text
Identify

↓

Draft

↓

Expert Review

↓

Formalize

↓

Unit Test

↓

Integration Test

↓

Deployment

↓

Monitoring

↓

Revision

↓

Retirement
```

No rule should enter production without validation.

---

# 7.10 Knowledge Versioning

Knowledge evolves independently from application code.

Each release of the knowledge base shall include:

* Version identifier.
* Source guideline.
* Date introduced.
* Author.
* Reviewer.
* Supporting evidence.
* Deprecation status.

This supports reproducibility and clinical governance.

---

# 7.11 Knowledge Validation

Validation occurs at multiple levels.

## Syntax Validation

Ensure Prolog correctness.

---

## Semantic Validation

Verify consistency with source guidelines.

---

## Clinical Validation

Review by qualified nutrition professionals.

---

## Integration Validation

Verify interoperability with existing modules.

---

## Regression Validation

Ensure updates do not break existing reasoning.

---

# 7.12 Knowledge Governance

Knowledge governance defines responsibility for maintaining the symbolic knowledge base.

Roles include:

* Knowledge Engineer.
* Clinical Nutrition Expert.
* Software Engineer.
* Reviewer.
* Release Manager.

Each change should follow a documented review and approval workflow.

---

# 7.13 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-005: Knowledge as an Independent System Asset

### Status

Accepted

### Context

Clinical knowledge changes more frequently than application infrastructure. Embedding rules within application code increases maintenance costs and complicates validation.

### Decision

Treat clinical knowledge as an independent, version-controlled asset managed through formal knowledge engineering processes.

### Rationale

* Simplifies updates to clinical guidance.
* Enables independent testing.
* Improves traceability.
* Supports collaboration between software engineers and nutrition experts.
* Preserves a single source of truth.

### Consequences

Knowledge updates can be released independently of application deployments, provided the public knowledge interface remains stable.

---

# 7.14 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes the Knowledge Engineering Framework that underpins NutriLogic. It defines the sources, acquisition process, representation techniques, ontology, validation strategy, governance model, and lifecycle for symbolic knowledge.

By treating clinical knowledge as an independent engineering asset rather than embedded application logic, the system becomes more maintainable, explainable, and resilient to future changes in nutrition science and national guidelines.

The next chapter will apply this framework to design the **Nutrition Ontology**, providing the formal vocabulary and semantic relationships that all symbolic reasoning modules will use.

# Chapter 8: Nutrition Ontology and Semantic Knowledge Model

## 8.1 Introduction

A Clinical Decision Support System depends not only on logical rules but also on a shared understanding of the concepts those rules manipulate. Without a consistent semantic model, knowledge becomes fragmented, duplicate concepts emerge, and reasoning becomes increasingly difficult to maintain.

This chapter defines the formal Nutrition Ontology that serves as the semantic foundation of NutriLogic. The ontology establishes the vocabulary, entities, relationships, and constraints used throughout the system. Every symbolic rule, database model, API endpoint, and user interface ultimately derives its meaning from this ontology.

Rather than functioning solely as documentation, the ontology represents a machine-readable conceptual model that enables consistent reasoning, knowledge reuse, interoperability, and future knowledge graph construction.

---

# 8.2 Objectives

The Nutrition Ontology has the following objectives:

* Establish a common vocabulary across the system.
* Eliminate semantic ambiguity.
* Standardize symbolic predicates.
* Support modular knowledge engineering.
* Enable explainable inference.
* Facilitate future knowledge graph development.
* Support interoperability with future clinical systems.

---

# 8.3 Ontology Design Principles

The ontology follows several guiding principles.

### Clinical Accuracy

Concepts shall correspond to accepted nutrition and clinical terminology used in Kenyan healthcare guidance.

---

### Single Source of Truth

Every concept shall have one canonical representation.

For example:

Correct:

```prolog
condition(diabetes).
```

Avoid:

```prolog
diabetic(User).
has_diabetes(User).
is_diabetic(User).
```

Alternative forms should be represented through derived predicates rather than duplicated concepts.

---

### Separation of Concepts

Entities represent things.

Relationships describe how entities interact.

Rules derive new knowledge from entities and relationships.

---

### Extensibility

New diseases, foods, biomarkers, interventions, and guidelines shall be incorporated without redesigning the ontology.

---

# 8.4 Core Ontology Domains

The ontology is organised into interconnected domains.

```text
Person

Health Profile

Assessment

Diagnosis

Disease

Symptom

Food

Food Group

Nutrient

Meal

Lifestyle

Medication

Physical Activity

Biomarker

Intervention

Goal

Monitoring

Recommendation

Explanation
```

Each domain is independently maintainable while remaining semantically connected.

---

# 8.5 Person Ontology

The Person entity represents the subject receiving nutrition recommendations.

Primary attributes include:

* Person ID
* Age
* Sex
* Pregnancy Status
* Lactation Status
* County
* Height
* Weight
* BMI
* Occupation
* Activity Level
* Socioeconomic Context
* Dietary Preferences
* Allergies
* Medical History

Example symbolic facts:

```prolog
person(user123).

age(user123,34).

sex(user123,female).

county(user123,nairobi).

activity_level(user123,moderate).
```

The Person entity is the root of nearly all reasoning.

---

# 8.6 Assessment Ontology

Assessment represents structured observations collected during the Nutrition Care Process.

Assessment categories include:

## Anthropometric

* Height
* Weight
* BMI
* MUAC
* Waist Circumference

---

## Clinical

* Symptoms
* Medical Conditions
* Physical Signs

---

## Dietary

* Meal Frequency
* Food Intake
* Water Intake
* Fruit Intake
* Vegetable Intake

---

## Lifestyle

* Physical Activity
* Smoking
* Alcohol
* Sleep

---

## Biochemical

Future integration:

* HbA1c
* Blood Glucose
* Blood Pressure
* Lipid Profile
* Haemoglobin

Assessments never generate recommendations directly.

They provide evidence for diagnosis.

---

# 8.7 Diagnosis Ontology

Nutrition diagnoses represent interpreted assessment findings.

Examples include:

* Obesity Risk
* Protein-Energy Malnutrition
* Iron Deficiency Risk
* Excess Sodium Intake
* Poor Dietary Diversity
* Low Physical Activity

Relationships:

```text
Assessment

↓

Diagnosis

↓

Intervention
```

Every diagnosis must be traceable to supporting assessment evidence.

---

# 8.8 Disease Ontology

Diseases represent medical conditions influencing nutrition recommendations.

Examples include:

* Diabetes Mellitus
* Hypertension
* Chronic Kidney Disease
* HIV/AIDS
* Tuberculosis
* Anaemia
* Obesity
* Pregnancy-related Conditions

Diseases influence:

* nutrient requirements,
* contraindications,
* meal planning,
* monitoring,
* therapeutic interventions.

---

# 8.9 Food Ontology

Foods are central to recommendation generation.

Each food should contain structured metadata.

Example:

```text
Food

├── Food Group

├── Nutrient Composition

├── Glycaemic Index

├── Nutrient Profile Category

├── Cultural Context

├── County Availability

├── Cost Category

├── Seasonal Availability

├── Preparation Methods

└── Contraindications
```

This ontology enables recommendations beyond simple food names.

---

# 8.10 Nutrient Ontology

Each nutrient contains semantic properties.

Examples:

Protein

Iron

Calcium

Vitamin A

Vitamin C

Vitamin D

Folate

Zinc

Dietary Fibre

Added Sugar

Saturated Fat

Sodium

Each nutrient links to:

* foods,
* deficiencies,
* diseases,
* interventions,
* biomarkers.

---

# 8.11 Intervention Ontology

Interventions describe actions recommended by the inference engine.

Categories include:

* Meal Plans
* Therapeutic Diets
* Supplementation
* Physical Activity
* Behaviour Change
* Nutrition Education
* Clinical Referral

Each intervention references:

* diagnosis,
* goals,
* contraindications,
* expected outcomes.

---

# 8.12 Monitoring Ontology

Monitoring supports longitudinal care.

Entities include:

* Weight Record
* BMI Trend
* Goal Progress
* Adherence
* Follow-up Assessment
* Outcome Measure

Monitoring links interventions to future assessments.

---

# 8.13 Explanation Ontology

Explainability is treated as a first-class concept.

Each explanation references:

* observations,
* triggered rules,
* inferred diagnoses,
* recommendations,
* supporting evidence,
* source guideline.

Thus every recommendation is accompanied by a structured reasoning chain rather than free-text justification.

---

# 8.14 Semantic Relationships

The ontology defines explicit relationships between concepts.

```text
Person
 │
 ├── has → Assessment
 │
 ├── has → Condition
 │
 ├── consumes → Food
 │
 ├── performs → Physical Activity
 │
 └── receives → Recommendation

Assessment
 │
 ├── supports → Diagnosis
 │
 └── measures → Biomarker

Diagnosis
 │
 ├── managed_by → Intervention
 │
 ├── monitored_by → Monitoring Plan
 │
 └── explained_by → Explanation

Food
 │
 ├── contains → Nutrient
 ├── belongs_to → Food Group
 ├── recommended_for → Diagnosis
 └── contraindicated_for → Disease
```

---

# 8.15 Ontology Layering

The ontology is organised into four semantic layers.

```text
Foundation Layer
    Person
    Food
    Nutrient
    Disease

↓

Clinical Layer
    Assessment
    Diagnosis
    Intervention

↓

Behaviour Layer
    Goals
    Lifestyle
    Monitoring

↓

Explanation Layer
    Evidence
    Rule Trace
    Recommendation
```

Each higher layer depends only on lower layers, reducing complexity and improving maintainability.

---

# 8.16 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-006: Ontology-Driven Knowledge Engineering

### Status

Accepted

### Context

As the knowledge base expands, inconsistent terminology and duplicated concepts can undermine reasoning quality and maintainability.

### Decision

Adopt a formal nutrition ontology as the semantic foundation for all symbolic knowledge. Every predicate, database model, API contract, and user-facing concept shall map to ontology entities and relationships.

### Rationale

* Ensures semantic consistency.
* Simplifies rule development.
* Enables future knowledge graph integration.
* Improves interoperability.
* Supports explainable reasoning.

### Consequences

Knowledge engineers must extend the ontology before introducing new symbolic concepts, ensuring long-term consistency and reducing duplication.

---

# 8.17 Chapter Summary

This chapter defines the Nutrition Ontology that underpins NutriLogic. By establishing a shared semantic model for people, assessments, diagnoses, diseases, foods, nutrients, interventions, monitoring, and explanations, the ontology provides the common language through which every system component communicates.

The ontology serves as the bridge between authoritative clinical knowledge and executable symbolic reasoning, laying the groundwork for a future knowledge graph while preserving deterministic inference through SWI-Prolog.

# Chapter 9: Nutrition Knowledge Graph and Symbolic Knowledge Representation

## 9.1 Introduction

While the Nutrition Ontology defined the vocabulary and conceptual structure of NutriLogic, it does not itself contain clinical knowledge. The ontology specifies *what kinds of things exist* (e.g., Person, Food, Nutrient, Disease, Assessment), whereas the Knowledge Graph captures *how those things are related*.

This chapter defines the Nutrition Knowledge Graph (NKG), which serves as the semantic backbone of the system. The graph stores relationships among foods, nutrients, diseases, interventions, life stages, medications, assessments, biomarkers, and recommendations. The symbolic inference engine then reasons over these relationships to derive explainable clinical recommendations.

The Knowledge Graph is not intended to replace SWI-Prolog. Instead, it provides a structured semantic layer upon which symbolic reasoning operates.

---

# 9.2 Objectives

The Nutrition Knowledge Graph is designed to:

* Represent nutrition knowledge as interconnected entities and relationships.
* Eliminate duplication of facts across rule modules.
* Improve knowledge reuse.
* Simplify reasoning over complex clinical relationships.
* Support future semantic querying.
* Enable graph visualization.
* Facilitate future interoperability with external healthcare systems.

---

# 9.3 Position Within the Architecture

The Knowledge Graph occupies the semantic layer between the ontology and the symbolic inference engine.

```text
Clinical Guidelines
        │
        ▼
Knowledge Engineering
        │
        ▼
Nutrition Ontology
        │
        ▼
Nutrition Knowledge Graph
        │
        ▼
SWI-Prolog Symbolic Reasoner
        │
        ▼
Explainable Recommendations
```

The ontology defines concepts.

The graph stores relationships.

The inference engine derives conclusions.

---

# 9.4 Graph Components

The graph consists of three primary components.

## Nodes

Nodes represent entities.

Examples include:

* Person
* Food
* Nutrient
* Disease
* Medication
* Biomarker
* Recommendation
* Meal
* Goal
* County
* Food Group
* Lifestyle Factor
* Physical Activity

Each node has a unique identifier and associated attributes.

---

## Relationships

Relationships connect entities.

Examples:

```text
Ugali
    CONTAINS
Carbohydrate

Beans
    RICH_IN
Protein

Spinach
    RICH_IN
Iron

Hypertension
    REQUIRES
Low Sodium Diet

Pregnancy
    INCREASES_REQUIREMENT_FOR
Iron

Walking
    IMPROVES
Cardiovascular Health
```

Relationships carry semantic meaning that can be used by the inference engine.

---

## Properties

Nodes and relationships may contain additional metadata.

Example:

Food

* Glycaemic Index
* Cost Category
* Preparation Methods
* Seasonal Availability
* County Availability

Relationship

RECOMMENDED_FOR

Properties

* Evidence Level
* Source Guideline
* Effective Date
* Confidence
* Reviewer

---

# 9.5 Core Graph Domains

The graph is organised into interconnected domains.

```text
Person
│
├── Assessment
├── Diagnosis
├── Goals
├── Lifestyle
├── Conditions
└── Monitoring

Food
│
├── Nutrient
├── Food Group
├── Preparation
├── Availability
└── Cost

Disease
│
├── Contraindications
├── Therapeutic Diets
├── Monitoring
└── Biomarkers

Intervention
│
├── Meal Plan
├── Education
├── Supplements
└── Physical Activity
```

Each domain is independently extensible.

---

# 9.6 Relationship Types

Relationship types define the semantics of the graph.

Examples include:

| Relationship        | Meaning                            |
| ------------------- | ---------------------------------- |
| CONTAINS            | Food contains nutrient             |
| DEFICIENT_IN        | Person lacks nutrient              |
| HAS_CONDITION       | Person has disease                 |
| BELONGS_TO          | Food belongs to group              |
| REQUIRES            | Disease requires intervention      |
| CONTRAINDICATED_FOR | Food unsuitable for condition      |
| IMPROVES            | Intervention improves outcome      |
| CAUSED_BY           | Diagnosis derived from observation |
| MEASURED_BY         | Biomarker measures condition       |
| RECOMMENDED_FOR     | Food suitable for diagnosis        |
| TARGETS             | Intervention addresses diagnosis   |

---

# 9.7 Example Knowledge Graph

```text
User
 │
 ├── HAS_CONDITION ─────────────► Hypertension
 │
 ├── CONSUMES ──────────────────► Processed Meat
 │
 └── HAS_GOAL ──────────────────► Reduce Blood Pressure

Processed Meat
 │
 └── HIGH_IN ───────────────────► Sodium

Hypertension
 │
 ├── REQUIRES ──────────────────► Low Sodium Diet
 │
 └── MONITORED_BY ──────────────► Blood Pressure

Low Sodium Diet
 │
 ├── RECOMMENDS ────────────────► Fruits
```
