```text
 ├── RECOMMENDS ────────────────► Vegetables
 └── LIMITS ────────────────────► Processed Foods
```

This graph allows multiple reasoning paths to converge on the same recommendation.

---

# 9.8 Mapping the Graph to Prolog

The graph is represented symbolically using predicates.

Example:

```prolog
food(spinach).

contains(spinach, iron).

recommended_for(spinach, anaemia).

contraindicated_for(processed_meat, hypertension).

requires(hypertension, low_sodium_diet).
```

The graph is therefore a semantic interpretation of symbolic facts rather than a separate reasoning engine.

---

# 9.9 Reasoning Over the Graph

The inference engine traverses graph relationships to derive conclusions.

Example reasoning chain:

```text
User
 │
 ▼
Diagnosis
 │
 ▼
Required Diet
 │
 ▼
Allowed Foods
 │
 ▼
Meal Plan
 │
 ▼
Recommendation
```

Every step remains explainable.

---

# 9.10 Explainability Through Graph Traversal

One advantage of graph-based knowledge representation is the ability to reconstruct reasoning paths.

Example explanation:

```text
Recommendation:
Increase spinach intake.

Reasoning

Spinach

↓

Rich in Iron

↓

Iron supports haemoglobin synthesis

↓

Assessment identified low haemoglobin

↓

Risk of iron deficiency

↓

Recommendation generated
```

Each step corresponds to explicit graph relationships and symbolic rules.

---

# 9.11 Knowledge Graph Evolution

The graph is expected to grow continuously.

Future additions include:

* Kenyan food composition datasets.
* County-specific food availability.
* Seasonal food variations.
* Traditional Kenyan recipes.
* Medication-food interactions.
* Laboratory biomarkers.
* Clinical pathways.
* Behavioural interventions.

Because relationships are modular, new domains can be integrated without redesigning existing structures.

---

# 9.12 Knowledge Graph Governance

Changes to the graph follow the same governance model defined for symbolic rules.

Each modification shall include:

* Source reference.
* Date of introduction.
* Version identifier.
* Reviewer.
* Validation status.
* Supporting evidence.

This ensures reproducibility and accountability.

---

# 9.13 Integration with Future Neural Components

Machine learning models will not interact directly with recommendation rules.

Instead, they contribute observations that become graph entities or relationships.

Example:

```text
Food Recognition Model

↓

Detected:
Ugali

↓

CREATE NODE

Food(Ugali)

↓

LINK

CONSUMED_BY(User)

↓

Symbolic Reasoning
```

This design preserves explainability while allowing advanced perception capabilities.

---

# 9.14 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-007: Knowledge Graph as the Semantic Backbone

### Status

Accepted

### Context

As NutriLogic expands to include hundreds of foods, diseases, nutrients, interventions, medications, and life-stage recommendations, maintaining isolated symbolic rules becomes increasingly difficult.

### Decision

Adopt a Nutrition Knowledge Graph as the primary semantic representation of domain knowledge. SWI-Prolog remains the inference engine, operating over graph-derived symbolic facts and relationships.

### Rationale

* Improves scalability.
* Reduces duplication.
* Enhances semantic consistency.
* Simplifies knowledge maintenance.
* Enables future graph analytics and semantic interoperability.

### Consequences

Knowledge engineers must define entities and relationships within the graph before introducing new reasoning rules, ensuring that the symbolic knowledge base remains semantically coherent.

---

# 9.15 Chapter Summary

This chapter introduces the Nutrition Knowledge Graph as the semantic foundation of NutriLogic. Together with the Nutrition Ontology and the SWI-Prolog inference engine, it forms a layered knowledge architecture in which concepts are formally defined, relationships are explicitly represented, and clinical reasoning remains deterministic and explainable.

The Knowledge Graph enables NutriLogic to evolve from a traditional rule-based expert system into a scalable knowledge-centric neuro-symbolic platform capable of supporting advanced reasoning, future machine learning integration, and semantic interoperability.

# Chapter 10: Clinical Decision Pipeline (Nutrition Care Process Engine)

## 10.1 Introduction

The effectiveness of a Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System depends not only on the quality of its knowledge but also on the structure of its reasoning process. Professional dietitians do not make recommendations directly from symptoms; instead, they follow a systematic workflow that begins with comprehensive assessment, progresses through diagnosis and intervention planning, and continues with monitoring and evaluation.

NutriLogic adopts this workflow by implementing the **Nutrition Care Process (NCP)** as a symbolic Clinical Decision Pipeline. Each stage transforms structured evidence into increasingly refined clinical knowledge, producing transparent, reproducible, and explainable recommendations.

The pipeline forms the operational core of the system and coordinates all symbolic reasoning performed by the SWI-Prolog inference engine.

---

# 10.2 Objectives

The Clinical Decision Pipeline aims to:

* Model the reasoning process of professional nutritionists.
* Ensure recommendations are evidence-based and explainable.
* Separate assessment, diagnosis, intervention, and monitoring into independent reasoning stages.
* Improve modularity and maintainability.
* Enable longitudinal nutrition care.
* Support future integration of machine learning without compromising explainability.

---

# 10.3 Pipeline Overview

The Clinical Decision Pipeline consists of nine sequential stages.

```text id="r8g4q1"
Patient Context
        │
        ▼
Nutrition Assessment
        │
        ▼
Problem Identification
        │
        ▼
Nutrition Diagnosis
        │
        ▼
Goal Formulation
        │
        ▼
Intervention Planning
        │
        ▼
Meal Plan Generation
        │
        ▼
Behaviour Change Support
        │
        ▼
Monitoring
        │
        ▼
Outcome Evaluation
```

Each stage receives structured symbolic facts from the previous stage and produces new facts for the next stage.

---

# 10.4 Stage 1 – Patient Context

The Patient Context stage establishes the baseline profile used throughout the reasoning process.

Inputs include:

* Demographic information.
* Anthropometric measurements.
* Medical history.
* Family history.
* Pregnancy or lactation status.
* County of residence.
* Socioeconomic factors.
* Dietary preferences.
* Allergies.
* Medications.
* Physical activity.
* Health goals.

Example symbolic facts:

```prolog id="z1pq9a"
person(user123).
age(user123, 34).
sex(user123, female).
county(user123, kisumu).
condition(user123, hypertension).
goal(user123, weight_loss).
```

Outputs:

A complete patient context represented as symbolic facts.

---

# 10.5 Stage 2 – Nutrition Assessment

This stage evaluates the patient's nutritional status using multiple assessment domains.

## Anthropometric Assessment

Examples:

* BMI
* Weight
* Height
* MUAC
* Waist Circumference

## Clinical Assessment

Examples:

* Symptoms
* Diagnosed diseases
* Physical signs

## Dietary Assessment

Examples:

* Meal frequency
* Food diversity
* Fruit and vegetable intake
* Water consumption
* Processed food intake

## Lifestyle Assessment

Examples:

* Physical activity
* Sleep
* Smoking
* Alcohol consumption

## Biochemical Assessment (Future)

Examples:

* HbA1c
* Blood glucose
* Blood pressure
* Haemoglobin
* Lipid profile

Outputs:

Validated assessment facts.

---

# 10.6 Stage 3 – Problem Identification

Assessment findings are analysed to identify nutrition-related problems.

Examples include:

* Excess sodium intake.
* Inadequate dietary diversity.
* Low protein intake.
* Excess added sugar consumption.
* Low physical activity.
* Obesity risk.

This stage does not diagnose; it identifies evidence requiring interpretation.

Outputs:

Problem facts.

---

# 10.7 Stage 4 – Nutrition Diagnosis

The Diagnosis Engine transforms identified problems into formal nutrition diagnoses.

Examples:

* Excessive Energy Intake.
* Inadequate Iron Intake.
* Overweight.
* Obesity.
* Protein-Energy Malnutrition Risk.
* High Cardiovascular Risk.

Each diagnosis must reference:

* supporting assessments,
* contributing factors,
* evidence,
* confidence,
* applicable guideline.

Outputs:

Structured diagnosis facts.

---

# 10.8 Stage 5 – Goal Formulation

Before generating recommendations, the system establishes measurable nutrition goals.

Goals follow the SMART principle.

Examples:

* Lose 5 kg within six months.
* Reduce sodium intake below recommended limits.
* Increase fruit intake to two servings daily.
* Achieve normal BMI.
* Improve dietary diversity.

Goals become explicit symbolic objects.

Example:

```prolog id="g3kt82"
goal(user123, reduce_sodium).
target(user123, sodium, "<2000mg").
```

Outputs:

Goal facts.

---

# 10.9 Stage 6 – Intervention Planning

The Intervention Planner determines evidence-based actions required to achieve established goals.

Interventions include:

* Therapeutic diets.
* Meal plans.
* Food substitutions.
* Supplement recommendations.
* Physical activity.
* Behavioural counselling.
* Nutrition education.
* Referral to healthcare professionals when appropriate.

The planner resolves conflicts and prioritises interventions according to clinical importance.

Outputs:

Intervention facts.

---

# 10.10 Stage 7 – Meal Plan Generation

Meal planning translates interventions into culturally appropriate dietary plans.

Generation considers:

* Nutrient requirements.
* Medical conditions.
* Therapeutic diets.
* Allergies.
* Food preferences.
* County availability.
* Cost.
* Seasonality.
* Preparation methods.

Outputs include:

* Daily meal plans.
* Weekly meal plans.
* Shopping suggestions.
* Food substitutions.

Meal plans are generated using Kenyan foods and recipes whenever possible.

---

# 10.11 Stage 8 – Behaviour Change Support

Long-term nutrition improvement depends on sustained behaviour change.

The Behaviour Support module generates:

* SMART goals.
* Educational messages.
* Motivational feedback.
* Adherence reminders.
* Progress summaries.
* Lifestyle recommendations.

Future versions may incorporate adaptive coaching based on user engagement and progress.

Outputs:

Behaviour support facts and user guidance.

---

# 10.12 Stage 9 – Monitoring

Monitoring evaluates progress after interventions are implemented.

Indicators include:

* Weight trends.
* BMI trends.
* Blood pressure.
* Dietary adherence.
* Goal completion.
* Physical activity.
* Recommendation adherence.

Monitoring data are persisted to support longitudinal care.

Outputs:

Monitoring records.

---

# 10.13 Stage 10 – Outcome Evaluation

The final stage evaluates the effectiveness of interventions.

Possible outcomes include:

* Goals achieved.
* Partial improvement.
* No improvement.
* Deterioration.
* New nutrition problems.

Evaluation results influence future assessments and recommendations, creating a continuous feedback loop.

Outputs:

Outcome facts and revised care plans.

---

# 10.14 Explainability Across the Pipeline

Each stage contributes to a complete reasoning chain.

Example:

```text id="v6ns2k"
Assessment:
High processed food intake

↓

Problem:
Excess sodium consumption

↓

Diagnosis:
Nutrition diagnosis: High Sodium Intake

↓

Goal:
Reduce sodium intake

↓

Intervention:
Low-sodium diet

↓

Meal Plan:
Increase fresh vegetables and legumes

↓

Monitoring:
Weekly blood pressure tracking

↓

Outcome:
Improved blood pressure control
```

Every recommendation presented to the user can therefore be traced through each stage of the pipeline.

---

# 10.15 Integration with Symbolic Reasoning

Each pipeline stage is implemented as an independent symbolic module.

```text id="n5f7ye"
assessment.pl

↓

problem_identification.pl

↓

diagnosis.pl

↓

goals.pl

↓

interventions.pl

↓

meal_planner.pl

↓

behaviour_support.pl

↓

monitoring.pl

↓

evaluation.pl
```

Each module exposes predicates that consume structured facts and produce new facts, allowing independent testing and maintenance.

---

# 10.16 Pipeline Orchestration

The Django Application Layer coordinates pipeline execution.

Workflow:

1. Receive assessment data via the REST API.
2. Validate input.
3. Convert input to symbolic facts.
4. Invoke the appropriate Prolog modules sequentially.
5. Collect outputs from each stage.
6. Persist intermediate and final results.
7. Return recommendations together with explanations and monitoring plans.

This orchestration layer manages execution but contains no clinical reasoning logic.

---

# 10.17 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-008: Explicit Nutrition Care Process Pipeline

### Status

Accepted

### Context

Directly mapping symptoms to recommendations limits explainability, personalization, and alignment with clinical practice.

### Decision

Implement the Nutrition Care Process as a staged Clinical Decision Pipeline in which assessment, diagnosis, goal formulation, intervention, monitoring, and evaluation are modelled as independent symbolic reasoning modules.

### Rationale

* Mirrors professional clinical workflows.
* Improves transparency and traceability.
* Supports longitudinal care.
* Enables modular testing and maintenance.
* Provides clear extension points for future AI components.

### Consequences

Every recommendation must be derived through the pipeline rather than produced directly from user input, ensuring consistency and auditability.

---

# 10.18 Chapter Summary

This chapter defines the Clinical Decision Pipeline that operationalises the Nutrition Care Process within NutriLogic. By decomposing reasoning into explicit stages—from patient context through assessment, diagnosis, intervention, monitoring, and outcome evaluation—the system achieves a high degree of explainability, modularity, and clinical fidelity.

The pipeline serves as the execution model for the symbolic inference engine and provides a clear framework for integrating future machine learning capabilities while maintaining deterministic, guideline-based decision making.

# Chapter 11: Symbolic Reasoning Engine

## 11.1 Introduction

The Symbolic Reasoning Engine is the core intelligence component of NutriLogic. It transforms structured clinical observations into explainable nutrition recommendations using deterministic logical inference rather than statistical prediction.

Unlike conventional recommendation systems, the reasoning engine does not rely on opaque machine learning models to produce decisions. Instead, it applies explicit symbolic rules derived from authoritative nutrition guidelines, enabling every recommendation to be reproduced, audited, validated, and explained.

The reasoning engine operates over the Nutrition Knowledge Graph and Nutrition Ontology defined in previous chapters while implementing the Clinical Decision Pipeline described in Chapter 9.

---

# 11.2 Objectives

The Symbolic Reasoning Engine is designed to:

* Produce deterministic recommendations.
* Support transparent clinical reasoning.
* Maintain consistency with Kenyan nutrition guidelines.
* Resolve conflicting recommendations.
* Generate human-readable explanations.
* Support modular knowledge engineering.
* Enable incremental knowledge expansion.
* Integrate future machine learning observations.

---

# 11.3 Engine Architecture

The reasoning engine consists of several cooperating components.

```text
                    Assessment Facts
                           │
                           ▼
                 Fact Validation Engine
                           │
                           ▼
                 Working Memory Manager
                           │
                           ▼
                  Rule Matching Engine
                           │
                           ▼
                 Inference Controller
                           │
            ┌──────────────┴──────────────┐
            ▼                             ▼
     Forward Chaining             Backward Chaining
            │                             │
            └──────────────┬──────────────┘
                           ▼
               Conflict Resolution Engine
                           │
                           ▼
                Explanation Generator
                           │
                           ▼
                  Recommendation Output
```

Each component has a well-defined responsibility, allowing independent testing and maintenance.

---

# 11.4 Working Memory

Working Memory stores all facts relevant to the current reasoning session.

It contains:

* Patient profile.
* Assessment findings.
* Diagnosed conditions.
* Goals.
* Intermediate conclusions.
* Triggered rules.
* Generated recommendations.

Working Memory exists only for the duration of an inference session and is distinct from the persistent database.

Example symbolic facts:

```prolog
age(user123,34).

condition(user123,hypertension).

fruit_intake(user123,low).

physical_activity(user123,sedentary).

goal(user123,weight_loss).
```

As inference progresses, new derived facts are added.

---

# 11.5 Rule Matching

The Rule Matching Engine continuously compares available facts against symbolic rules.

Example:

```prolog
condition(User, hypertension),
high_sodium_intake(User)
    :-
recommend(User, low_sodium_diet).
```

The engine identifies all rules whose preconditions are satisfied.

Matching rules become candidates for execution.

---

# 11.6 Inference Strategies

NutriLogic employs two complementary inference strategies.

## Forward Chaining

Forward chaining is data-driven.

The engine begins with known facts and derives new conclusions until no additional rules can fire.

Example:

```text
Assessment

↓

BMI = 32

↓

Obesity

↓

Weight Loss Goal

↓

Reduced Energy Diet

↓

Meal Plan
```

Forward chaining is well suited to comprehensive nutrition assessment.

---

## Backward Chaining

Backward chaining is goal-driven.

The engine begins with a hypothesis and attempts to prove it.

Example:

Goal:

```text
Should this patient receive an iron-rich diet?
```

The engine searches for evidence supporting the recommendation.

Backward chaining is useful for:

* explanation,
* clinical validation,
* diagnostic verification,
* user queries.

---

# 11.7 Rule Execution Lifecycle

Each symbolic rule follows a consistent execution lifecycle.

```text
Rule Loaded

↓

Rule Matched

↓

Conditions Verified

↓

Priority Evaluated

↓

Conflict Detection

↓

Rule Executed

↓

New Facts Generated

↓

Explanation Recorded

↓

Working Memory Updated
```

This lifecycle ensures reproducibility and auditability.

---

# 11.8 Rule Prioritisation

Some rules are clinically more important than others.

The engine therefore assigns priorities.

Suggested priority hierarchy:

| Priority | Rule Category             |
| -------- | ------------------------- |
| Critical | Contraindications         |
| High     | Medical safety            |
| High     | Nutrition diagnoses       |
| Medium   | Therapeutic interventions |
| Medium   | Meal planning             |
| Low      | Education                 |
| Low      | Behaviour support         |

Critical rules always execute before advisory rules.

---

# 11.9 Conflict Resolution

Conflicting recommendations may arise.

Example:

Rule A:

Recommend increased protein intake.

Rule B:

Limit protein intake because of Chronic Kidney Disease.

The Conflict Resolution Engine resolves such conflicts using ordered policies.

Priority order:

1. Patient safety.
2. Contraindications.
3. Clinical guideline precedence.
4. Disease severity.
5. Therapeutic goals.
6. Lifestyle optimisation.

Only the highest-priority compatible recommendations proceed.

---

# 11.10 Constraint Satisfaction

Certain recommendations are prohibited under specific conditions.

Examples include:

* Allergies.
* Pregnancy restrictions.
* Drug-food interactions.
* Religious dietary restrictions.
* Chronic disease contraindications.

Constraint rules override ordinary recommendation rules.

Example:

```prolog
allergy(User, peanuts)
    ->
reject(peanut_products).
```

---

# 11.11 Meta-Reasoning

The reasoning engine also reasons about its own reasoning.

Meta-rules determine:

* Which rule should execute first.
* Whether additional evidence is required.
* Whether recommendations are complete.
* Whether conflicts remain unresolved.
* Whether uncertainty exceeds acceptable thresholds.

Meta-reasoning improves robustness without altering domain knowledge.

---

# 11.12 Explanation Engine

Every recommendation is accompanied by a structured explanation.

Each explanation records:

* Supporting facts.
* Triggered rules.
* Intermediate conclusions.
* Final recommendation.
* Knowledge sources.

Example:

```text
Recommendation

Increase leafy green vegetables.

Reason

↓

Assessment:
Low vegetable intake

↓

Diagnosis:
Low dietary iron

↓

Rule:
Iron-rich foods recommended

↓

Food:
Spinach

↓

Guideline:
Clinical Nutrition Manual
```

This explanation chain supports transparency and clinician review.

---

# 11.13 Reasoning Trace

The engine records a complete reasoning trace.

Example:

```text
Assessment

↓

Rule 12 Fired

↓

Diagnosis Created

↓

Rule 48 Fired

↓

Goal Established

↓

Rule 93 Fired

↓

Meal Generated

↓

Recommendation Delivered
```

The trace supports debugging, auditing, and research.

---

# 11.14 Certainty Handling

Most nutrition guidance is deterministic, but some recommendations may involve uncertainty due to incomplete or conflicting information.

The engine therefore distinguishes between:

* Confirmed facts.
* Derived facts.
* Assumed facts.
* Missing information.

Rather than assigning arbitrary probabilities, uncertainty is handled through explicit evidence states and requests for additional information when required.

Future versions may extend this mechanism with certainty factors or probabilistic reasoning, provided explainability is preserved.

---

# 11.15 Temporal Reasoning

Nutrition care is inherently longitudinal.

The reasoning engine therefore incorporates temporal reasoning to support follow-up care.

Examples:

* Weight change over time.
* Dietary adherence trends.
* Goal progress.
* Medication changes.
* Pregnancy progression.
* Growth monitoring in children.

Temporal rules compare historical observations with current assessments to determine whether interventions should be maintained, modified, or discontinued.

---

# 11.16 Performance Optimisation

To maintain responsiveness, the engine incorporates several optimisation techniques.

These include:

* Rule indexing.
* Modular knowledge loading.
* Query caching where safe.
* Lazy evaluation of expensive rule sets.
* Incremental inference using only changed facts.
* Reuse of validated intermediate conclusions.

Optimisations must never alter the correctness of clinical reasoning.

---

# 11.17 Integration with Django

The Django orchestration layer communicates with the reasoning engine through a stable interface.

Typical workflow:

1. Validate incoming assessment data.
2. Convert validated input into symbolic facts.
3. Load relevant knowledge modules.
4. Execute inference.
5. Retrieve diagnoses, interventions, explanations, and monitoring plans.
6. Persist outputs.
7. Return structured responses to the frontend.

This separation ensures that clinical reasoning remains isolated from application infrastructure.

---

# 11.18 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-009: Hybrid Forward and Backward Chaining Inference

### Status

Accepted

### Context

Clinical nutrition requires both comprehensive reasoning from available evidence and the ability to justify or verify specific recommendations.

### Decision

Adopt a hybrid inference strategy that combines forward chaining for recommendation generation with backward chaining for explanation, validation, and goal-directed reasoning.

### Rationale

* Mirrors clinical reasoning workflows.
* Supports efficient recommendation generation.
* Improves explainability.
* Enables diagnostic verification.
* Facilitates user queries such as "Why was this recommendation made?"

### Consequences

Knowledge engineers must design rules that support both data-driven and goal-driven inference, ensuring that every recommendation remains reproducible and explainable.

---

# 11.19 Chapter Summary

This chapter defines the architecture and operation of the Symbolic Reasoning Engine that powers NutriLogic. By combining structured working memory, hybrid forward and backward chaining, conflict resolution, meta-reasoning, explanation generation, and temporal reasoning, the engine delivers deterministic, transparent, and clinically aligned recommendations.

The reasoning engine operationalises the Nutrition Knowledge Graph and Clinical Decision Pipeline while remaining extensible to future neuro-symbolic enhancements.

# Chapter 12: Modular Knowledge Base Design

## 12.1 Introduction

The symbolic knowledge base is the core intellectual asset of NutriLogic. It captures the clinical expertise, national nutrition guidelines, therapeutic interventions, and reasoning processes that enable explainable decision support.

As the system expands to include additional diseases, life stages, biomarkers, interventions, and Kenyan dietary knowledge, maintaining a single monolithic knowledge base becomes impractical. This chapter defines a modular architecture that organizes symbolic knowledge into cohesive, independently maintainable components.

The modular design promotes scalability, collaborative development, independent testing, and traceability while preserving deterministic reasoning through SWI-Prolog.

---

# 12.2 Design Principles

The knowledge base shall adhere to the following principles:

* **Single Responsibility**: Each module addresses one domain.
* **High Cohesion**: Closely related rules reside together.
* **Low Coupling**: Modules communicate through well-defined predicates.
* **Traceability**: Rules reference their source guidelines.
* **Versionability**: Modules evolve independently.
* **Testability**: Each module has its own unit tests.
* **Explainability**: Rules expose metadata for explanations.

---

# 12.3 Proposed Directory Structure

```text id="kb-structure"
prolog/
└── knowledge/
    ├── load.pl
    ├── metadata.pl
    ├── ontology/
    │   ├── person.pl
    │   ├── food.pl
    │   ├── nutrient.pl
    │   ├── disease.pl
    │   ├── diagnosis.pl
    │   ├── intervention.pl
    │   └── monitoring.pl
    ├── assessment/
    │   ├── anthropometry.pl
    │   ├── dietary.pl
    │   ├── lifestyle.pl
    │   ├── clinical.pl
    │   └── biochemical.pl
    ├── diagnosis/
    │   ├── nutrition_diagnosis.pl
    │   ├── risk_assessment.pl
    │   └── differential_diagnosis.pl
    ├── interventions/
    │   ├── therapeutic_diets.pl
    │   ├── meal_planning.pl
    │   ├── supplementation.pl
    │   ├── physical_activity.pl
    │   └── behaviour_change.pl
    ├── diseases/
    │   ├── diabetes.pl
    │   ├── hypertension.pl
    │   ├── obesity.pl
    │   ├── anaemia.pl
    │   ├── hiv.pl
    │   ├── tuberculosis.pl
    │   ├── pregnancy.pl
    │   └── kidney_disease.pl
    ├── monitoring/
    │   ├── follow_up.pl
    │   ├── adherence.pl
    │   ├── outcomes.pl
    │   └── alerts.pl
    ├── explanations/
    │   ├── rule_trace.pl
    │   ├── evidence.pl
    │   └── explanation_builder.pl
    ├── validation/
    │   ├── constraints.pl
    │   ├── allergies.pl
    │   ├── contraindications.pl
    │   └── medication_interactions.pl
    ├── utilities/
    │   ├── loaders.pl
    │   ├── priorities.pl
    │   ├── certainty.pl
    │   └── helpers.pl
    └── tests/
        ├── assessment_tests.pl
        ├── diagnosis_tests.pl
        ├── intervention_tests.pl
        └── regression_tests.pl
```

This structure replaces the current single `kb.pl` with a modular, domain-driven organization.

---

# 12.4 Module Categories

### Ontology Modules

Define canonical concepts and relationships.

Outputs:

* Shared predicates
* Domain vocabulary

---

### Assessment Modules

Transform raw observations into validated assessment findings.

Examples:

* BMI calculation
* Dietary diversity scoring
* Physical activity classification

Outputs:

Assessment facts.

---

### Diagnosis Modules

Infer nutrition diagnoses based on assessment findings.

Outputs:

Structured diagnosis facts.

---

### Intervention Modules

Generate:

* Therapeutic diets
* Meal plans
* Supplement advice
* Physical activity plans
* Behavioural interventions

---

### Disease Modules

Contain disease-specific nutrition knowledge.

Each disease module includes:

* assessment criteria
* contraindications
* nutrition goals
* intervention rules
* monitoring rules

---

### Monitoring Modules

Evaluate intervention effectiveness over time.

Outputs:

* progress summaries
* adherence metrics
* alerts
* revised goals

---

### Explanation Modules

Construct structured reasoning chains for every recommendation.

---

### Validation Modules

Prevent unsafe recommendations by enforcing:

* allergy rules
* medication-food interactions
* pregnancy restrictions
* disease contraindications

These modules execute before intervention generation.

---

# 12.5 Module Dependencies

Dependencies should flow in one direction.

```text id="module-deps"
Ontology
    ↓
Assessment
    ↓
Diagnosis
    ↓
Intervention
    ↓
Monitoring
    ↓
Explanation
```

Lower layers must never depend on higher layers.

This prevents circular reasoning and simplifies maintenance.

---

# 12.6 Public Interfaces

Each module exposes a stable public interface.

Example:

```prolog id="module-api"
run_assessment(User).

nutrition_diagnosis(User, Diagnosis).

generate_intervention(User, Plan).

monitor_progress(User, Report).

build_explanation(User, Explanation).
```

Modules should hide internal predicates wherever possible.

---

# 12.7 Metadata Model

Each symbolic rule should include metadata linking it to its source.

Suggested metadata fields:

* Rule ID
* Module
* Source document
* Guideline section
* Version
* Author
* Reviewer
* Date introduced
* Priority
* Safety classification

Example:

```prolog id="rule-meta"
rule_metadata(
    rule_htn_001,
    hypertension,
    moh2025,
    "Section 4.2",
    "v1.0",
    critical
).
```

This supports auditing, governance, and explanation.

---

# 12.8 Knowledge Loading Strategy

A single bootstrap file (`load.pl`) is responsible for loading modules in dependency order.

```text id="loader-flow"
Ontology
    ↓
Validation
    ↓
Assessment
    ↓
Diagnosis
    ↓
Interventions
    ↓
Monitoring
    ↓
Explanation
```

The inference service requests only the modules required for the current reasoning session, reducing startup time and memory usage.

---

# 12.9 Testing Strategy

Every module must include automated tests.

Testing levels include:

* Syntax validation
* Predicate unit tests
* Integration tests
* Regression tests
* Clinical validation scenarios

Representative patient cases should be used to verify expected recommendations and explanation chains.

---

# 12.10 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-010: Domain-Driven Modular Knowledge Base

### Status

Accepted

### Context

A monolithic knowledge base becomes difficult to maintain as clinical scope expands.

### Decision

Organize symbolic knowledge into domain-driven modules with explicit interfaces, dependency rules, and metadata.

### Rationale

* Improves maintainability.
* Supports parallel development.
* Simplifies testing.
* Enables incremental expansion.
* Preserves semantic consistency.

### Consequences

All new knowledge must be added through the appropriate module rather than extending a monolithic file, ensuring long-term scalability and governance.

---

# 12.11 Migration Strategy

The existing `kb.pl` should be migrated incrementally:

1. Extract ontology predicates.
2. Separate assessment rules.
3. Move diagnosis rules.
4. Create intervention modules.
5. Isolate disease-specific logic.
6. Introduce validation modules.
7. Implement explanation metadata.
8. Replace direct `consult('kb.pl')` calls with `load.pl`.

This staged migration minimizes risk and allows continuous testing throughout the refactoring process.

---

# 12.12 Chapter Summary

This chapter defines the modular organization of NutriLogic's symbolic knowledge base. By separating ontology, assessment, diagnosis, intervention, disease, monitoring, validation, and explanation into cohesive modules with explicit interfaces and metadata, the system becomes scalable, maintainable, and suitable for long-term evolution.

The modular knowledge base forms the implementation foundation upon which the ontology, knowledge graph, inference engine, and application services operate.

# Chapter 13: Nutrition Ontology Implementation

## 13.1 Introduction

The Nutrition Ontology provides the semantic foundation of NutriLogic. While Chapter 7 introduced the conceptual ontology, this chapter specifies its implementation within the symbolic reasoning environment. The implementation establishes canonical predicates, entity hierarchies, relationships, namespaces, and governance rules that ensure semantic consistency across the knowledge base.

The ontology implementation acts as the shared vocabulary for the Knowledge Graph, Prolog inference engine, Django services, REST APIs, and future machine learning components.

---

# 13.2 Objectives

The implementation aims to:

* Define canonical symbolic predicates.
* Standardize entity naming conventions.
* Prevent semantic duplication.
* Support ontology-driven reasoning.
* Enable interoperability between modules.
* Facilitate future semantic web integration.

---

# 13.3 Ontology Package Structure

```text
prolog/
└── knowledge/
    └── ontology/
        ├── ontology.pl
        ├── entities.pl
        ├── relationships.pl
        ├── classes.pl
        ├── hierarchy.pl
        ├── constraints.pl
        ├── metadata.pl
        └── namespaces.pl
```

Each file has a distinct responsibility.

---

# 13.4 Entity Model

Every concept in the ontology is represented as an entity.

Examples include:

```prolog
entity(person).
entity(food).
entity(food_group).
entity(nutrient).
entity(condition).
entity(symptom).
entity(diagnosis).
entity(intervention).
entity(goal).
entity(meal).
entity(activity).
entity(county).
entity(biomarker).
```

These entities define the vocabulary of the expert system.

---

# 13.5 Entity Hierarchies

Entities are organised into inheritance hierarchies.

Example:

```text
Food
 ├── Fruit
 ├── Vegetable
 ├── Legume
 ├── Cereal
 ├── Dairy
 ├── Animal Protein
 ├── Beverage
 └── Traditional Food
```

Similarly:

```text
Condition
 ├── Diabetes
 ├── Hypertension
 ├── Obesity
 ├── Anaemia
 ├── HIV
 ├── Tuberculosis
 └── Kidney Disease
```

Hierarchies enable generalized reasoning while preserving specialization.

---

# 13.6 Relationship Vocabulary

Relationships are represented using canonical predicates.

Examples:

```prolog
contains(Food,Nutrient).

belongs_to(Food,FoodGroup).

recommended_for(Food,Condition).

contraindicated_for(Food,Condition).

requires(Condition,Intervention).

measures(Biomarker,Condition).

supports(Nutrient,BodyFunction).

available_in(Food,County).

seasonal_in(Food,Season).

substitutes(FoodA,FoodB).
```

These predicates form the semantic language of the Knowledge Graph.

---

# 13.7 Naming Conventions

To ensure consistency:

* Predicate names use lowercase snake_case.
* Entity identifiers use lowercase atoms.
* Relationship predicates are verbs.
* Class names remain singular.
* One canonical predicate represents one concept.

Examples:

Correct:

```prolog
food(spinach).
contains(spinach,iron).
recommended_for(spinach,anaemia).
```

Avoid:

```prolog
is_food(spinach).
food_item(spinach).
good_for(spinach,anaemia).
```

---

# 13.8 Ontology Constraints

The ontology enforces semantic constraints.

Examples:

* A Food must belong to at least one Food Group.
* A Nutrient must have at least one physiological function.
* Every Diagnosis must originate from one or more Assessments.
* Every Recommendation must reference supporting evidence.

Constraint predicates validate ontology integrity during development.

---

# 13.9 Metadata

Ontology entities include descriptive metadata.

Example:

```prolog
entity_metadata(
    spinach,
    food,
    "Spinacia oleracea",
    "Leafy Green Vegetable",
    kenya_food_table,
    version("1.0")
).
```

Metadata supports documentation, explainability, multilingual interfaces, and future interoperability.

---

# 13.10 Namespaces

Namespaces prevent naming conflicts.

Examples:

```text
person:

food:

nutrient:

diagnosis:

intervention:

monitoring:
```

Namespaces also simplify integration with external ontologies in future work.

---

# 13.11 Validation

Ontology validation includes:

* Entity uniqueness.
* Relationship integrity.
* Constraint satisfaction.
* Hierarchy consistency.
* Circular dependency detection.

Validation executes automatically during knowledge base loading.

---

# 13.12 Integration with the Knowledge Graph

The ontology provides the schema for the Nutrition Knowledge Graph.

For example:

```text
Ontology

Food
    │
    ▼
Graph Node

Spinach
    │
contains
    ▼
Iron
```

The ontology defines *what is possible*; the graph records *what exists*.

---

# 13.13 Integration with Symbolic Reasoning

The inference engine consumes ontology predicates directly.

Example:

```prolog
contains(spinach,iron).

iron_deficiency(User)
    :-
low_haemoglobin(User).

recommend(User,spinach)
    :-
iron_deficiency(User),
contains(spinach,iron).
```

This demonstrates how semantic relationships become executable reasoning.

---

# 13.14 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-011: Ontology-Driven Symbolic Implementation

### Status

Accepted

### Decision

Implement a formal ontology package that defines canonical entities, relationships, hierarchies, constraints, and metadata used across the entire NutriLogic platform.

### Rationale

* Ensures semantic consistency.
* Reduces duplication.
* Simplifies rule development.
* Enables future interoperability.
* Supports knowledge graph evolution.

---

# 13.15 Chapter Summary

This chapter specifies the implementation of the Nutrition Ontology within the symbolic reasoning environment. By defining canonical entities, relationships, hierarchies, namespaces, metadata, and validation mechanisms, the ontology becomes the authoritative semantic model for every component of NutriLogic.

It establishes a stable vocabulary upon which the Knowledge Graph, inference engine, database schema, APIs, and user interface can reliably build.

# Chapter 14: Nutrition Knowledge Graph Implementation

## 14.1 Introduction

The Nutrition Knowledge Graph (NKG) is the executable semantic representation of nutrition knowledge within NutriLogic. While the ontology defines the vocabulary and conceptual relationships of the domain, the Knowledge Graph instantiates these concepts as interconnected entities that support symbolic reasoning, explanation generation, semantic querying, and future neuro-symbolic learning.

Unlike traditional expert systems that store knowledge exclusively as isolated rules, NutriLogic models nutrition knowledge as a graph of clinically meaningful entities and relationships. The SWI-Prolog inference engine reasons over this semantic structure to generate deterministic and explainable recommendations.

The Knowledge Graph therefore acts as the semantic backbone connecting the ontology, symbolic reasoning engine, application services, and future neural components.

---

# 14.2 Objectives

The implementation of the Nutrition Knowledge Graph aims to:

* Represent nutrition knowledge as interconnected entities.
* Centralize semantic relationships.
* Support graph traversal during inference.
* Improve explainability.
* Reduce duplication of symbolic facts.
* Enable graph analytics.
* Support interoperability with external healthcare knowledge bases.
* Facilitate future machine learning integration.

---

# 14.3 Architectural Position

The Knowledge Graph occupies the semantic layer between the ontology and symbolic inference.

```text id="kg-position"
Clinical Guidelines
        │
        ▼
Knowledge Engineering
        │
        ▼
Nutrition Ontology
        │
        ▼
Nutrition Knowledge Graph
        │
        ▼
Inference Service
        │
        ▼
SWI-Prolog Engine
        │
        ▼
Clinical Recommendations
```

The graph does not replace symbolic reasoning; it enriches and organizes it.

---

# 14.4 Graph Implementation Architecture

The graph is implemented as an independent subsystem.

```text id="kg-package"
backend/
└── knowledge_graph/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── entities.py
    ├── relationships.py
    ├── graph.py
    ├── graph_loader.py
    ├── graph_queries.py
    ├── graph_validator.py
    ├── graph_export.py
    ├── graph_cache.py
    └── serializers.py
```

Responsibilities are clearly separated to support testing, maintenance, and future extensions.

---

# 14.5 Graph Data Model

The graph consists of three primary elements.

### Nodes

Nodes represent domain entities.

Examples include:

* Person
* Food
* Nutrient
* Disease
* Diagnosis
* Intervention
* Meal
* Goal
* Biomarker
* County
* Food Group
* Physical Activity

Every node possesses:

* Unique identifier.
* Ontology class.
* Attributes.
* Metadata.
* Provenance.

---

### Edges

Edges represent semantic relationships.

Examples include:

```text id="edge-types"
CONTAINS

BELONGS_TO

RECOMMENDED_FOR

CONTRAINDICATED_FOR

HAS_CONDITION

REQUIRES

SUPPORTS

MEASURED_BY

AVAILABLE_IN

SUBSTITUTES

CAUSES

TARGETS
```

Edges are directional and semantically typed.

---

### Properties

Both nodes and edges contain metadata.

Examples include:

* Evidence source.
* Confidence level.
* Effective date.
* Guideline version.
* Reviewer.
* Last modified.
* Clinical priority.

---

# 14.6 Entity Lifecycle

Every graph entity follows a controlled lifecycle.

```text id="entity-lifecycle"
Extract

↓

Validate

↓

Normalize

↓

Assign Ontology Class

↓

Generate Identifier

↓

Insert Into Graph

↓

Link Relationships

↓

Validate Graph

↓

Publish
```

This lifecycle ensures semantic consistency.

---

# 14.7 Graph Construction Pipeline

The graph is constructed automatically from authoritative knowledge sources.

```text id="construction-pipeline"
Clinical Documents

↓

Knowledge Extraction

↓

Ontology Mapping

↓

Entity Resolution

↓

Relationship Extraction

↓

Validation

↓

Graph Construction

↓

Graph Publication
```

This process minimizes manual duplication and improves maintainability.

---

# 14.8 Semantic Validation

Before publication, the graph undergoes validation.

Validation checks include:

* Duplicate entities.
* Missing ontology classes.
* Invalid relationships.
* Circular dependencies.
* Orphan nodes.
* Invalid metadata.
* Broken references.

Validation failures prevent deployment of inconsistent knowledge.

---

# 14.9 Graph Query Layer

The graph exposes reusable semantic queries.

Examples include:

```text id="graph-query-examples"
Find all foods rich in iron.

Find interventions for hypertension.

Find foods contraindicated during pregnancy.

Find alternative foods available in Kisumu County.

Find nutrients supporting immune function.
```

These semantic queries support both inference and user-facing features.

---

# 14.10 Integration with Prolog

The graph supplies structured facts to the inference engine.

Illustrative mapping:

```prolog id="graph-prolog"
food(spinach).

contains(spinach, iron).

recommended_for(spinach, anaemia).

available_in(spinach, kisumu).
```

The inference engine reasons over these facts to produce recommendations.

---

# 14.11 Integration with Django

The Django backend interacts with the graph through a dedicated service layer.

```text id="django-graph"
REST Request

↓

Application Service

↓

Knowledge Graph Service

↓

Inference Service

↓

SWI-Prolog

↓

Structured Response
```

The application never manipulates graph internals directly.

---

# 14.12 Integration with Explainability

The graph provides explicit reasoning paths.

Example:

```text id="graph-explain"
Spinach

↓

Contains Iron

↓

Supports Haemoglobin

↓

Low Haemoglobin Detected

↓

Iron Deficiency Risk

↓

Recommendation Generated
```

Each edge becomes part of the explanation delivered to clinicians and users.

---

# 14.13 Export and Interoperability

The graph supports export to interoperable formats.

Potential export targets include:

* RDF
* OWL
* GraphML
* JSON-LD

These exports enable future collaboration with semantic web tools, graph databases, visualization software, and healthcare interoperability initiatives.

---

# 14.14 Graph Governance

Every graph modification must record:

* Author.
* Reviewer.
* Source document.
* Guideline section.
* Timestamp.
* Version.
* Validation status.

Governance ensures traceability and supports clinical auditing.

---

# 14.15 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-012: Knowledge Graph as an Executable Semantic Subsystem

### Status

Accepted

### Context

As NutriLogic expands, semantic relationships become too numerous and interconnected to manage effectively through isolated symbolic predicates alone.

### Decision

Implement the Nutrition Knowledge Graph as a dedicated software subsystem responsible for semantic representation, validation, querying, and interoperability. The graph supplies structured knowledge to the SWI-Prolog inference engine while remaining independent of application logic.

### Rationale

* Centralizes semantic knowledge.
* Improves maintainability.
* Enables graph-based explanation.
* Supports interoperability.
* Prepares the platform for future neuro-symbolic learning.

### Consequences

All new domain knowledge must be introduced through the ontology and graph construction pipeline before becoming available to the symbolic inference engine.

---

# 14.16 Chapter Summary

This chapter specifies the implementation of the Nutrition Knowledge Graph as the semantic core of NutriLogic. By organizing nutrition knowledge into validated entities and relationships, the graph provides a scalable foundation for symbolic reasoning, explainability, interoperability, and future machine learning integration while preserving deterministic inference through SWI-Prolog.

# Chapter 15: Prolog Rule Module Architecture

## 15.1 Introduction

The symbolic reasoning capabilities of NutriLogic are implemented through a modular collection of SWI-Prolog rule modules. Rather than storing all rules within a single knowledge base, the system organizes reasoning logic according to the stages of the Nutrition Care Process and the domain model defined by the Nutrition Ontology and Knowledge Graph.

This modular architecture promotes scalability, maintainability, independent testing, and explainability while allowing the knowledge base to evolve alongside national nutrition guidelines and clinical practice.

---

# 15.2 Objectives

The Prolog Rule Module Architecture is designed to:

* Organize rules into cohesive domains.
* Separate knowledge from reasoning infrastructure.
* Improve maintainability.
* Support incremental expansion.
* Enable independent module testing.
* Facilitate rule traceability.
* Support explanation generation.
* Minimize coupling between rule sets.

---

# 15.3 Directory Structure

```text id="rule-structure"
prolog/
├── load.pl
├── inference/
│   ├── engine.pl
│   ├── working_memory.pl
│   ├── forward_chain.pl
│   ├── backward_chain.pl
│   ├── conflict_resolution.pl
│   ├── explanation_engine.pl
│   ├── priorities.pl
│   └── trace.pl
│
└── knowledge/
    ├── ontology/
    ├── assessment/
    ├── diagnosis/
    ├── interventions/
    ├── diseases/
    ├── monitoring/
    ├── explanations/
    ├── validation/
    └── utilities/
```

The inference layer contains execution logic, while the knowledge layer contains domain expertise.

---

# 15.4 Assessment Modules

Assessment modules transform observations into structured clinical findings.

Examples:

```text id="assessment-modules"
anthropometry.pl

dietary_assessment.pl

lifestyle_assessment.pl

clinical_assessment.pl

biochemical_assessment.pl
```

Typical predicates include:

```prolog id="assessment-preds"
calculate_bmi/3.

dietary_diversity_score/2.

physical_activity_level/2.

nutrition_risk/2.
```

Outputs become assessment facts stored in working memory.

---

# 15.5 Diagnosis Modules

Diagnosis modules interpret assessment findings.

Examples:

```text id="diagnosis-modules"
nutrition_diagnosis.pl

obesity.pl

malnutrition.pl

micronutrient_deficiency.pl

cardiometabolic_risk.pl
```

Representative predicates:

```prolog id="diagnosis-preds"
nutrition_diagnosis/2.

diagnosis_supported_by/2.

risk_level/2.
```

Each diagnosis must be traceable to supporting evidence.

---

# 15.6 Intervention Modules

Intervention modules generate evidence-based care plans.

Examples:

```text id="intervention-modules"
meal_planning.pl

therapeutic_diets.pl

food_substitution.pl

physical_activity.pl

supplementation.pl

behaviour_change.pl
```

Representative predicates:

```prolog id="intervention-preds"
recommend_meal_plan/2.

recommend_activity/2.

recommend_food/2.

recommend_supplement/2.
```

---

# 15.7 Disease Modules

Each disease has its own dedicated knowledge module.

Example:

```text id="disease-tree"
diseases/

diabetes.pl

hypertension.pl

obesity.pl

anaemia.pl

pregnancy.pl

hiv.pl

kidney_disease.pl
```

Each disease module encapsulates:

* diagnostic criteria
* contraindications
* nutrition interventions
* monitoring requirements
* explanation metadata

---

# 15.8 Validation Modules

Validation modules enforce clinical safety.

Responsibilities include:

* allergy detection
* contraindications
* medication-food interactions
* pregnancy restrictions
* age restrictions
* nutrient upper limits

Safety rules execute before recommendation rules.

---

# 15.9 Monitoring Modules

Monitoring modules support longitudinal nutrition care.

Responsibilities include:

* adherence evaluation
* goal tracking
* trend analysis
* follow-up scheduling
* alert generation

Monitoring uses historical observations to inform future recommendations.

---

# 15.10 Explanation Modules

Explanation modules reconstruct the reasoning process.

Outputs include:

* triggered rules
* supporting evidence
* intermediate conclusions
* recommendation rationale
* guideline references

The explanation engine operates independently from the inference engine to preserve modularity.

---

# 15.11 Rule Metadata

Each rule should include structured metadata.

Suggested attributes:

* Rule ID
* Module
* Source document
* Guideline section
* Priority
* Author
* Reviewer
* Version
* Effective date
* Safety classification

Illustrative structure:

```prolog id="rule-metadata"
rule_metadata(
    rule_htn_001,
    hypertension,
    moh2025,
    section("4.3"),
    priority(critical),
    version("1.0")
).
```

Metadata supports governance, auditing, and explainability.

---

# 15.12 Rule Execution Flow

```text id="execution-flow"
Load Modules

↓

Validate Facts

↓

Populate Working Memory

↓

Run Assessment Rules

↓

Run Diagnosis Rules

↓

Run Intervention Rules

↓

Apply Validation Rules

↓

Resolve Conflicts

↓

Generate Explanations

↓

Return Recommendations
```

This staged execution aligns with the Clinical Decision Pipeline.

---

# 15.13 Module Communication

Modules communicate through public predicates only.

Example interfaces:

```prolog id="module-interfaces"
run_assessment/1.

generate_diagnosis/2.

generate_interventions/2.

validate_recommendations/2.

build_explanations/2.
```

Internal predicates remain encapsulated within their respective modules.

---

# 15.14 Testing Strategy

Every rule module must include:

* syntax tests
* predicate tests
* integration tests
* regression tests
* clinical validation scenarios

Representative patient profiles should be used to verify expected diagnoses and recommendations.

---

# 15.15 Performance Considerations

To support efficient inference:

* load only required modules
* index frequently used predicates
* cache immutable ontology facts
* avoid redundant rule evaluation
* separate static facts from session facts

Performance optimizations must preserve deterministic reasoning.

---

# 15.16 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-013: Modular Rule-Based Reasoning

### Status

Accepted

### Context

A monolithic rule base becomes difficult to maintain as the number of clinical domains and recommendations grows.

### Decision

Organize symbolic reasoning into modular Prolog packages aligned with the Nutrition Care Process and domain model. Separate inference infrastructure from domain knowledge.

### Rationale

* Improves maintainability.
* Simplifies testing.
* Supports collaborative development.
* Enhances explainability.
* Enables incremental expansion.

### Consequences

All new reasoning capabilities must be implemented within dedicated modules that expose stable public predicates and include metadata for traceability.

---

# 15.17 Chapter Summary

This chapter defines the implementation architecture of NutriLogic's Prolog reasoning modules. By separating assessment, diagnosis, intervention, disease, validation, monitoring, and explanation logic into cohesive components supported by a dedicated inference layer, the symbolic reasoning engine becomes scalable, testable, and aligned with the platform's ontology-driven architecture.

# Chapter 16: Governance, Security, Privacy, and Clinical Safety Architecture

## 16.1 Introduction

Healthcare software operates within a highly regulated and safety-critical environment. Unlike conventional information systems, Clinical Decision Support Systems (CDSS) must ensure that recommendations are clinically appropriate, explainable, secure, and respectful of patient privacy. Consequently, governance is not an auxiliary concern but an architectural requirement.

This chapter defines the governance architecture of NutriLogic, establishing cross-cutting mechanisms for knowledge governance, clinical safety, privacy, security, auditability, quality assurance, and operational oversight. These mechanisms apply across every layer of the platform and provide the foundation for trustworthy and responsible AI-assisted nutrition decision support.

---

# 16.2 Objectives

The Governance Architecture aims to:

* Promote trustworthy AI-assisted decision support.
* Protect sensitive health information.
* Support clinical safety.
* Enable accountability and traceability.
* Facilitate future regulatory alignment.
* Ensure knowledge quality and integrity.
* Establish secure software engineering practices.
* Support continuous quality improvement.

---

# 16.3 Cross-Cutting Governance Model

Governance spans every architectural layer.

```text id="governance-stack"
Clinical Governance
Knowledge Governance
Security Governance
Privacy Governance
Quality Assurance
Risk Management
Audit & Traceability
Compliance Readiness
```

Each governance concern operates independently while interacting with application, semantic, reasoning, and knowledge layers.

---

# 16.4 Clinical Governance

Clinical Governance ensures that recommendations remain evidence-based and clinically appropriate.

Key principles include:

* Guideline-based recommendations.
* Transparent reasoning.
* Human interpretability.
* Evidence traceability.
* Clinical review of knowledge updates.
* Periodic validation against current nutrition guidance.

Clinical reasoning modules should reference authoritative nutrition sources and maintain provenance metadata.

---

# 16.5 Knowledge Governance

The symbolic knowledge base is treated as a managed clinical asset.

Knowledge governance includes:

* Rule versioning.
* Ontology versioning.
* Knowledge review workflows.
* Source attribution.
* Deprecation policies.
* Change approval.
* Regression validation before publication.

Each knowledge modification should be linked to supporting documentation and review records.

---

# 16.6 Security Architecture

Security is implemented according to defense-in-depth principles.

Key controls include:

* JWT authentication.
* Role-based authorization.
* Secure password hashing.
* HTTPS communication.
* Input validation.
* Output encoding.
* Protection against injection attacks.
* Secure session management.
* Secret management.
* Dependency vulnerability monitoring.

Security mechanisms are implemented independently of clinical reasoning.

---

# 16.7 Privacy Architecture

Privacy considerations are incorporated throughout the platform.

Design principles include:

* Data minimization.
* Purpose limitation.
* Explicit consent where applicable.
* User transparency.
* Secure storage.
* Controlled access.
* Data retention policies.
* Data deletion workflows.

Personally identifiable information is separated from symbolic reasoning wherever practical.

---

# 16.8 Clinical Safety

Clinical safety is a primary design objective.

Safety mechanisms include:

* Contraindication checking.
* Allergy validation.
* Medication interaction checks.
* Nutrient upper-limit validation.
* Rule conflict detection.
* Mandatory evidence requirements.
* Recommendation confidence classification.
* Safe fallback behaviour when evidence is insufficient.

The platform is intended to support—not replace—professional clinical judgement.

---

# 16.9 Explainability and Accountability

Every recommendation must be explainable.

Each recommendation records:

* Triggered assessment findings.
* Applied rules.
* Supporting evidence.
* Knowledge source.
* Generated interventions.
* Explanation chain.
* Timestamp.
* Knowledge version.

These records support transparency, user trust, and future clinical audits.

---

# 16.10 Audit Architecture

The platform maintains immutable audit records for significant events.

Illustrative audit events include:

* User authentication.
* Assessment submission.
* Recommendation generation.
* Knowledge base updates.
* Rule modifications.
* Administrative actions.
* API access.
* Data export.

Audit records support operational oversight and post-incident investigation.

---

# 16.11 Risk Management

Potential risks are identified and mitigated through architectural controls.

Representative risks include:

| Risk                     | Mitigation                                             |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| Incorrect recommendation | Rule validation, expert review, explanation generation |
| Incomplete assessment    | Missing-data detection, follow-up questions            |
| Conflicting rules        | Conflict resolution engine                             |
| Unauthorized access      | Authentication and authorization                       |
| Data leakage             | Encryption and access controls                         |
| Outdated knowledge       | Versioning and review workflows                        |

Risk management is integrated into system design rather than treated as a separate process.

---

# 16.12 Quality Assurance

Quality assurance encompasses both software and knowledge assets.

Activities include:

* Automated unit testing.
* Integration testing.
* Knowledge validation.
* Clinical scenario testing.
* Regression testing.
* Performance testing.
* Security testing.
* Usability evaluation.

Knowledge quality is evaluated alongside software quality.

---

# 16.13 Compliance Readiness

The architecture is designed to support future alignment with applicable legal, ethical, and professional frameworks.

Relevant considerations include:

* Kenyan health sector policies.
* Kenya Data Protection Act.
* AI governance principles.
* Clinical documentation practices.
* Software engineering standards.
* Healthcare interoperability initiatives.

The project does not claim certification or regulatory compliance but is architected to facilitate future alignment.

---

# 16.14 Governance Responsibilities

Governance responsibilities are distributed across stakeholder roles.

| Role                 | Primary Responsibilities                                       |
| -------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Knowledge Engineer   | Knowledge acquisition, ontology maintenance, rule validation   |
| Clinical Expert      | Clinical review, evidence validation, guideline interpretation |
| Software Engineer    | Implementation, testing, deployment, security                  |
| System Administrator | Infrastructure, monitoring, backups                            |
| Research Team        | Evaluation, documentation, governance oversight                |

Clear responsibility allocation supports accountability and sustainable maintenance.

---

# 16.15 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-014: Governance as a Cross-Cutting Architectural Concern

### Status

Accepted

### Context

Clinical decision support systems require governance mechanisms that extend beyond application logic to encompass knowledge management, security, privacy, safety, auditability, and quality assurance.

### Decision

Introduce a dedicated Governance Layer that spans every architectural component and defines policies for clinical safety, knowledge governance, privacy, security, auditing, and quality management.

### Rationale

* Improves trustworthiness.
* Supports responsible AI.
* Enhances maintainability.
* Facilitates future regulatory alignment.
* Promotes accountability.

### Consequences

All architectural components must expose sufficient metadata, audit information, and governance controls to support oversight throughout the platform lifecycle.

---

# 16.16 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes the Governance Architecture of NutriLogic. By treating governance as a foundational architectural layer rather than an afterthought, the platform integrates clinical safety, knowledge stewardship, security, privacy, auditability, risk management, and quality assurance into every aspect of its design. This approach strengthens the platform's trustworthiness and prepares it for future evolution within healthcare and research environments.

# Chapter 17: System Integration Architecture

## 17.1 Introduction

The NutriLogic platform consists of multiple architectural layers that collectively implement a knowledge-centric neuro-symbolic clinical decision support system. While previous chapters defined these layers independently, this chapter specifies how they interact to form a cohesive software platform.

The System Integration Architecture establishes communication pathways, service boundaries, dependency rules, data flow, execution lifecycle, and integration contracts among all major subsystems. It ensures that application logic, symbolic reasoning, semantic knowledge, governance mechanisms, and infrastructure remain loosely coupled while operating as a unified system.

---

# 17.2 Objectives

The System Integration Architecture aims to:

* Define communication between architectural layers.
* Minimize coupling.
* Maximize cohesion.
* Establish service contracts.
* Enable independent subsystem evolution.
* Support testing through abstraction.
* Simplify future AI integration.
* Improve maintainability.

---

# 17.4 Layer Responsibilities

## Presentation Layer

Responsible for:

* User interaction
* Visualization
* Authentication flow
* Recommendation presentation
* Explanation display

No clinical reasoning occurs here.

---

## Application Layer

Coordinates business workflows.

Responsibilities include:

* Validation
* Session management
* Authorization
* Orchestration
* Persistence
* API responses

The Application Layer never contains symbolic reasoning logic.

---

## Integration Layer

Provides stable interfaces between services and reasoning components.

Interfaces include:

* Inference Port
* Knowledge Port
* Explanation Port
* Analytics Port

Adapters implement these interfaces.

---

## Semantic Layer

Maintains domain knowledge.

Components include:

* Ontology
* Knowledge Graph
* Working Memory
* Terminology services

---

## Reasoning Layer

Responsible for deterministic inference.

Includes:

* Rule execution
* Forward chaining
* Backward chaining
* Conflict resolution
* Explanation generation

---

## Knowledge Layer

Stores symbolic clinical expertise.

Contains:

* assessment rules
* diagnosis rules
* intervention rules
* disease modules
* monitoring rules

---

## Infrastructure Layer

Provides technical services.

Includes:

* database
* logging
* storage
* deployment
* networking

The layered architecture minimizes changes required for future scaling.

---

# 17.14 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-015: Layered Integration Architecture

### Status

Accepted

### Context

The growing complexity of NutriLogic requires explicit integration mechanisms that separate application workflows from semantic reasoning while maintaining modularity and testability.

### Decision

Adopt a layered integration architecture in which application services communicate with semantic and reasoning components through stable interfaces and adapters.

### Rationale

* Reduces coupling.
* Improves maintainability.
* Simplifies testing.
* Supports future neuro-symbolic extensions.
* Enables independent subsystem evolution.

### Consequences

Direct communication between application services and Prolog modules is prohibited. All interactions must occur through defined integration interfaces.

---

This chapter defines the System Integration Architecture that binds together every major subsystem within NutriLogic. By establishing explicit communication pathways, service contracts, dependency rules, and integration principles, the architecture enables the platform to operate as a cohesive, modular, and extensible neuro-symbolic clinical decision support system. It provides the structural blueprint upon which the remaining implementation chapters are built.
