# 19.47 Cross-Cutting Architectural Concerns Demonstrated

The running scenario demonstrates how several architectural principles are exercised simultaneously:

| Concern                | Demonstration in Scenario                                                                           |
| ---------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Separation of Concerns | Each bounded context performs a distinct responsibility                                             |
| Domain Integrity       | Assessment validation occurs before persistence or inference                                        |
| Loose Coupling         | Contexts communicate through domain events                                                          |
| Explainability         | Every recommendation is accompanied by a reasoning trace                                            |
| Knowledge Governance   | Recommendations are derived from version-controlled rules and ontology concepts                     |
| Clinical Safety        | Validation and contraindication checks precede recommendation publication                           |
| Auditability           | Events, rule activations, and explanations are persistently recorded                                |
| Extensibility          | Additional reasoning engines or ML components can be introduced via the existing ports and adapters |

---

# 19.48 Architectural Insights from the Case Study

The walkthrough highlights several characteristics that distinguish NutriLogic from conventional expert systems:

1. **Knowledge-Centric Design:** Clinical knowledge is represented explicitly through ontologies, knowledge graphs, and symbolic rules rather than embedded within procedural code.
2. **Explainable Decision-Making:** Every recommendation is traceable to the rules, evidence, and guideline references that produced it.
3. **Bounded Context Collaboration:** Independent domains coordinate through events while remaining loosely coupled.
4. **Technology Independence:** The Domain Layer is insulated from Django, PostgreSQL, and SWI-Prolog through ports, adapters, repositories, and the Anti-Corruption Layer.
5. **Clinical Governance:** Recommendations are generated within a framework of version-controlled knowledge, auditability, and guideline compliance.

---

# Part VIII — UML Architectural Models

---

# 19.49 Package Architecture

## 19.49.1 Purpose

While the previous sections introduced the logical organization of bounded contexts and implementation layers, a UML Package Model provides a higher-level architectural view of dependencies between software packages. This representation demonstrates that the application follows the principles of Domain-Driven Design by grouping software according to business capabilities rather than technical frameworks.

The package organization also reflects the dependency inversion strategy established earlier in this chapter, ensuring that higher-level business concepts remain independent of infrastructure technologies.

---

## 19.49.2 Package Diagram

```text
                                   +-----------------------+
                                   |     Presentation      |
                                   |-----------------------|
                                   | REST API             |
                                   | React UI             |
                                   | Authentication       |
                                   +----------+------------+
                                              |
                                              v
                                   +-----------------------+
                                   |     Application       |
                                   |-----------------------|
                                   | Commands             |
                                   | Queries              |
                                   | Event Handlers       |
                                   | Workflow Services    |
                                   +----------+------------+
                                              |
             +--------------------------------+--------------------------------+
             |                                |                                |
             v                                v                                v
+------------------------+     +------------------------+     +------------------------+
|     Assessment         |     |      Knowledge         |     |     Monitoring         |
|------------------------|     |------------------------|     |------------------------|
| Assessment Aggregate   |     | Ontology              |     | Follow-up              |
| Assessment Services    |     | Knowledge Graph       |     | Outcomes               |
| Value Objects          |     | Rule Modules          |     | Behaviour Goals        |
+-----------+------------+     +-----------+------------+     +-----------+------------+
            \                          |                          /
             \                         |                         /
              \                        |                        /
               +-----------------------+-----------------------+
                                       |
                                       v
                           +---------------------------+
                           |        Shared Kernel      |
                           |---------------------------|
                           | Common Value Objects      |
                           | Domain Events             |
                           | Interfaces               |
                           +-------------+-------------+
                                         |
                                         v
                           +---------------------------+
                           |     Infrastructure        |
                           |---------------------------|
                           | PostgreSQL               |
                           | SWI-Prolog              |
                           | Redis                  |
                           | External APIs          |
                           +-------------------------+
```

---

## 19.49.3 Architectural Interpretation

Several important architectural characteristics are evident from the package model:

* Business capabilities are organized as bounded contexts.
* Dependencies converge toward the shared kernel rather than directly between contexts.
* Infrastructure remains at the outermost layer, preserving domain independence.
* Shared abstractions prevent cyclic dependencies and reduce coupling.
* The package structure mirrors the logical decomposition introduced throughout Chapters 6–18.

---

# 19.50 Component Architecture

## 19.50.1 Overview

The Component Model identifies the deployable and executable software units that collectively implement the NutriLogic platform. Unlike the Package Diagram, which emphasizes software organization, the Component Diagram illustrates runtime collaboration among system components.

---

---

## 19.50.3 Discussion

The component architecture reinforces one of the central architectural decisions of NutriLogic: symbolic reasoning is not embedded within the web application but encapsulated as an independent reasoning subsystem accessed exclusively through the Anti-Corruption Layer.

This separation enables future replacement or augmentation of the inference engine without affecting the remainder of the application.

---

# 19.51 Aggregate Design Example

## 19.51.1 Aggregate Root

To illustrate tactical Domain-Driven Design, this section presents the **NutritionAssessment Aggregate**, which encapsulates all information collected during the Assessment phase of the Nutrition Care Process.

The aggregate root is responsible for preserving consistency across all related entities and value objects.

---

## 19.51.2 Aggregate Structure

```text
NutritionAssessment (Aggregate Root)
│
├── PatientInformation
│
├── AnthropometricObservation
│     ├── Height
│     ├── Weight
│     ├── BMI
│     └── WaistCircumference
│
├── ClinicalObservation
│     ├── BloodPressure
│     ├── Diagnoses
│     ├── Medications
│     └── Symptoms
│
├── DietaryObservation
│     ├── FoodFrequency
│     ├── MealPattern
│     └── DietaryDiversity
│
├── LifestyleObservation
│     ├── PhysicalActivity
│     └── SleepQuality
│
├── LaboratoryObservation
│     └── LaboratoryResults
│
└── AssessmentFact
      ├── FactIdentifier
      ├── SymbolicRepresentation
      └── OntologyReference
```

---

## 19.51.3 Aggregate Responsibilities

The NutritionAssessment Aggregate is responsible for:

* Maintaining assessment consistency.
* Validating mandatory clinical observations.
* Computing derived clinical indicators (e.g., BMI, waist-to-height ratio).
* Preventing invalid assessment states.
* Acting as the sole entry point for modifications to assessment data.

This design ensures that all assessment-related invariants are enforced within a single aggregate boundary.

# 19.53 Architectural Conformance Rules

To ensure that the implemented system remains faithful to its intended architecture, NutriLogic adopts explicit architectural conformance rules.

| Rule ID | Constraint                                                                                 |
| ------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| AR-01   | Domain entities shall not import Django, DRF, or PySWIP packages.                          |
| AR-02   | Infrastructure components shall implement domain interfaces rather than the reverse.       |
| AR-03   | Controllers shall delegate all business operations to Application Services.                |
| AR-04   | All symbolic reasoning requests shall pass exclusively through the Anti-Corruption Layer.  |
| AR-05   | Aggregate invariants shall be enforced before persistence.                                 |
| AR-06   | Knowledge modules shall be version-controlled and clinically reviewed prior to deployment. |
| AR-07   | Cross-context communication shall occur through Domain Events wherever practical.          |
| AR-08   | Architecture Decision Records (ADRs) shall document all significant architectural changes. |

These rules provide measurable criteria for architectural reviews and continuous integration pipelines.

# 1. Purpose

The preceding nineteen chapters have progressively transformed NutriLogic from an initial concept into a comprehensive architectural specification for a Knowledge-Centric Neuro-Symbolic Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System (CNDSS). The architecture now encompasses requirements engineering, domain modelling, ontology engineering, symbolic reasoning, explainable artificial intelligence, knowledge governance, system integration, and domain-driven software architecture.

The purpose of this roadmap is to establish a unified implementation blueprint that links architectural decisions to their realization in the remaining chapters. It provides traceability across requirements, bounded contexts, knowledge models, software components, implementation artefacts, and evaluation activities, ensuring that the remainder of the dissertation proceeds in a coherent, systematic, and verifiable manner.

# CHAPTER 20

# Database and Persistence Architecture

---

# 20.1 Introduction

## 20.1.1 Overview

The preceding chapters established the conceptual, logical, and application architecture of NutriLogic. The domain model, bounded contexts, ontology, knowledge graph, symbolic reasoning engine, and application services collectively define *what* the system must accomplish and *how* clinical workflows are orchestrated. The purpose of this chapter is to describe how these architectural constructs are realized through a coherent persistence strategy.

Unlike conventional web applications, NutriLogic manages multiple categories of information with distinct structural and semantic characteristics. These include transactional clinical data, longitudinal patient records, symbolic rules, ontology definitions, semantic relationships, reasoning traces, governance artefacts, and audit logs. Each category has different requirements regarding consistency, versioning, explainability, and retrieval.

Consequently, the persistence layer is designed not merely as a relational database but as an integrated data management architecture capable of supporting both conventional application data and knowledge-centric artefacts while maintaining clinical integrity and traceability.

---

## 20.1.2 Position within the Overall Architecture

Within the overall software architecture, the persistence layer occupies the Infrastructure Layer while exposing repository interfaces to the Application Layer defined in Chapter 19.

```text
                React Frontend
                       │
                REST Controllers
                       │
              Application Services
                       │
              Repository Interfaces
                       │
        ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
        │              │              │
        ▼              ▼              ▼
 PostgreSQL     Knowledge Store   Audit Store
        │              │              │
        └──────────────┼──────────────┘
                       ▼
              Symbolic Knowledge
             Ontology + Rule Base
```

The Domain Layer remains independent of persistence technologies, interacting exclusively through repository abstractions in accordance with Domain-Driven Design and Hexagonal Architecture.

---

## 20.1.3 Design Objectives

The persistence architecture has been designed to achieve the following objectives:

1. Preserve the integrity of longitudinal clinical data.
2. Support efficient retrieval of patient histories and recommendations.
3. Maintain explicit separation between transactional data and symbolic knowledge.
4. Enable version-controlled evolution of ontologies and rule bases.
5. Record complete reasoning traces for explainability and auditability.
6. Support future interoperability through standardized data representations.
7. Ensure compliance with clinical governance and data protection requirements.
8. Facilitate maintainability through repository abstractions.
9. Provide scalability for increasing patient populations and knowledge resources.
10. Preserve architectural independence from specific database technologies.

These objectives directly support the functional and non-functional requirements established in Chapter 5 and the governance framework introduced in Chapter 16.

---

# 20.2 Persistence Philosophy

## 20.2.1 From Database-Centric to Knowledge-Centric Persistence

Traditional information systems treat relational databases as the sole persistent store. Clinical decision support systems, however, must preserve not only patient records but also the knowledge structures that generate clinical recommendations.

NutriLogic therefore adopts a **knowledge-centric persistence philosophy** in which persistence encompasses both operational data and explicit clinical knowledge.

The persistence architecture distinguishes four complementary categories of information:

1. **Transactional Data** – user accounts, assessments, recommendations, and monitoring records.
2. **Knowledge Artefacts** – ontologies, knowledge graphs, symbolic rules, and clinical guidelines.
3. **Governance Metadata** – version histories, approval records, provenance, and audit information.
4. **Operational Metadata** – logs, system events, caching, and performance metrics.

Each category has distinct lifecycle, consistency, and access requirements, and is therefore managed using specialized persistence mechanisms while remaining unified through the repository layer.

---

## 20.2.2 Polyglot Persistence Strategy

Although the current implementation uses PostgreSQL as the primary database, the architectural design embraces the principle of **polyglot persistence**, recognizing that different data types are optimally managed by different storage technologies.

The architecture therefore distinguishes between the logical persistence model and the physical implementation.

### Logical Data Stores

| Logical Store       | Managed Information                                        |
| ------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------- |
| Clinical Data Store | Patients, assessments, recommendations, monitoring records |
| Knowledge Store     | Ontology concepts, symbolic rules, clinical guidelines     |
| Semantic Store      | Knowledge graph entities and relationships                 |
| Governance Store    | Version histories, approvals, provenance                   |
| Audit Store         | Clinical reasoning traces and system events                |

### Physical Implementation (Current)

| Logical Store       | Physical Technology                                                                    |
| ------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Clinical Data Store | PostgreSQL                                                                             |
| Knowledge Store     | Version-controlled Prolog modules + OWL files                                          |
| Semantic Store      | RDF serialization persisted with project resources (future graph database integration) |
| Governance Store    | PostgreSQL                                                                             |
| Audit Store         | PostgreSQL                                                                             |

This separation ensures that future adoption of graph databases, RDF triple stores, or dedicated ontology repositories can occur without requiring changes to the Domain Layer.

---

# 20.3 Persistence Principles

The persistence architecture adheres to the following principles:

### Principle 1 — Domain Independence

Domain entities shall not contain database-specific annotations or persistence logic.

---

### Principle 2 — Repository Abstraction

All persistence operations shall occur through repository interfaces defined by the Domain Layer.

---

### Principle 3 — Aggregate Consistency

Each aggregate root shall be persisted atomically to preserve domain invariants.

---

### Principle 4 — Immutable Clinical History

Clinical assessments shall never be overwritten; instead, new records shall represent successive observations, preserving longitudinal history.

---

### Principle 5 — Versioned Knowledge

Symbolic rules, ontologies, and guideline mappings shall be version-controlled to ensure reproducibility of clinical recommendations.

---

### Principle 6 — Explainability Preservation

Reasoning traces, activated rules, and evidence chains shall be persisted alongside generated recommendations to support transparency and clinical audit.

---

### Principle 7 — Security by Design

Sensitive health information shall be protected through encryption, role-based access control, audit logging, and secure persistence practices consistent with applicable Kenyan health data governance and privacy requirements.

---

# 20.4 Architectural Context

The persistence subsystem occupies a central role within the overall architecture.

```text
                        Presentation Layer
                                │
                                ▼
                       Application Layer
                                │
                                ▼
                     Repository Interfaces
                                │
              ┌─────────────────┼──────────────────┐
              │                 │                  │
              ▼                 ▼                  ▼
      Clinical Database   Knowledge Store    Audit Repository
              │                 │                  │
              └─────────────────┼──────────────────┘
                                ▼
                       Infrastructure Layer
```

Repositories insulate the application from physical storage technologies, enabling future migration to alternative persistence mechanisms while preserving the integrity of the Domain Layer.

---

# 20.5 Quality Attribute Requirements

The persistence architecture is evaluated against the quality attributes defined throughout the dissertation.

| Quality Attribute | Persistence Strategy                                                |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Maintainability   | Repository pattern, bounded contexts, modular schemas               |
| Scalability       | Indexed tables, partition-ready design, future polyglot persistence |
| Reliability       | ACID transactions, backups, audit trails                            |
| Explainability    | Persistent reasoning traces and evidence chains                     |
| Clinical Safety   | Immutable assessment history and versioned knowledge                |
| Performance       | Query optimization, caching strategy, indexing                      |
| Security          | RBAC, encryption, audit logging, secure persistence                 |
| Evolvability      | Separation of logical and physical persistence models               |

---

# 20.6 Transition to Detailed Database Design

Having established the persistence philosophy, architectural objectives, guiding principles, and quality requirements, the remainder of this chapter progressively refines the persistence architecture from conceptual data models to physical implementation. The following sections will define the domain aggregates, relational schema, semantic persistence mechanisms, repository mappings, indexing strategies, transaction management, audit infrastructure, and governance processes that collectively realize the persistence layer of NutriLogic.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.7 Conceptual Data Model

---

## 20.7.1 Introduction

The conceptual data model defines the fundamental information structures required to support the clinical, semantic, and operational functions of NutriLogic. Unlike the physical database schema, which focuses on implementation technologies, the conceptual model captures the core business entities, their relationships, and the constraints imposed by the Nutrition Care Process (NCP), the Ministry of Health Kenya Nutrient Profile Model, and the Domain-Driven Application Architecture established in Chapter 19.

The conceptual data model serves as the authoritative representation of the application's information domain. It provides the foundation for:

* the logical relational database schema,
* the Django ORM persistence layer,
* repository implementations,
* REST API resource design,
* frontend data models,
* symbolic fact generation,
* ontology mappings, and
* knowledge graph construction.

By establishing a single conceptual representation, consistency is maintained across every implementation layer of the platform.

---

# 20.7.2 Conceptual Modelling Principles

The conceptual model was developed according to the following principles:

### Clinical First

The model reflects clinical workflows rather than database normalization alone. Entities correspond to real-world concepts within the Nutrition Care Process.

---

### Aggregate-Oriented Design

Conceptual entities are grouped according to Domain-Driven Design aggregates rather than individual database tables.

---

### Semantic Compatibility

Every major clinical concept is designed to be linkable to ontology concepts and knowledge graph nodes.

---

### Longitudinal Care

The model preserves historical observations rather than replacing previous records.

---

### Explainability

Every recommendation can be traced to:

* assessments,
* activated rules,
* ontology concepts,
* evidence sources,
* reasoning traces.

---

### Extensibility

Future neuro-symbolic modules, machine learning models, wearable devices, and FHIR resources can be incorporated without restructuring the conceptual model.

---

# 20.7.3 Major Domain Aggregates

The persistence model is organized around the aggregate roots introduced in Chapter 19.

The following aggregates constitute the primary business entities of NutriLogic.

| Aggregate             | Purpose                                       |
| --------------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| User                  | Identity, authentication, and health profile  |
| Nutrition Assessment  | Complete patient assessment                   |
| Recommendation        | Generated interventions                       |
| Monitoring Record     | Longitudinal outcome tracking                 |
| Knowledge Module      | Clinical knowledge assets and symbolic rules  |
| Explanation Session   | Reasoning traces and evidence                 |
| Audit Record          | Clinical governance and accountability        |

Each aggregate encapsulates its associated entities and value objects, preserving consistency boundaries within the domain model.

---

# 20.7.4 Master Conceptual Domain Model

The following diagram summarizes the principal conceptual relationships among the core aggregates.

```text
                              User
                               │
                               │ 1
                               │
                               ▼
                      Health Profile
                               │
                               │ 1
                               │
                     ┌─────────┴─────────┐
                     │                   │
                     ▼                   ▼
          Nutrition Assessment      Monitoring Record
                     │                   │
                     │                   │
                     ▼                   ▼
              Recommendation      Behaviour Goal
                     │
                     │
         ┌───────────┼────────────┐
         │           │            │
         ▼           ▼            ▼
   Reasoning Trace  Meal Plan  Explanation
         │
         │
         ▼
     Activated Rules
         │
         ▼
    Knowledge Module
         │
         ▼
      Nutrition Ontology
         │
         ▼
     Knowledge Graph
```

This model illustrates that clinical recommendations emerge from interactions between patient data and explicit knowledge assets rather than from isolated algorithmic computations.

---

# 20.7.5 Aggregate Descriptions

## User Aggregate

The User aggregate represents the authenticated system actor.

Responsibilities include:

* authentication,
* authorization,
* identity management,
* account lifecycle.

Primary relationships:

```text
User
 ├── Health Profile
 ├── Recommendations
 ├── Monitoring Records
 └── Audit Records
```

---

## Health Profile Aggregate

The Health Profile stores relatively stable patient characteristics.

Typical attributes include:

* demographic information,
* county,
* sex,
* birth date,
* baseline anthropometric measurements,
* medical history,
* allergies,
* lifestyle characteristics.

Unlike assessments, the profile evolves gradually throughout the patient's care journey.

---

## Nutrition Assessment Aggregate

The Nutrition Assessment represents a complete assessment episode.

Subcomponents include:

```text
Nutrition Assessment

├── Anthropometric Observation
├── Clinical Observation
├── Dietary Observation
├── Lifestyle Observation
├── Laboratory Observation
└── Assessment Fact
```

The aggregate root ensures that all assessment data satisfy clinical validation rules before persistence.

---

## Recommendation Aggregate

Recommendations represent structured interventions produced by symbolic reasoning.

Each recommendation references:

* originating assessment,
* activated rules,
* clinical guideline,
* ontology concepts,
* evidence,
* explanation,
* implementation priority.

Recommendations are immutable once issued, with subsequent changes represented by new recommendation versions to preserve clinical history.

---

## Monitoring Aggregate

Monitoring records support longitudinal care.

Each monitoring episode contains:

* progress measurements,
* behavioural adherence,
* intervention outcomes,
* follow-up schedules,
* clinician observations.

This enables evaluation of intervention effectiveness over time.

---

## Knowledge Aggregate

Knowledge assets include:

* ontology concepts,
* food composition knowledge,
* nutrient guidelines,
* dietary recommendations,
* symbolic rules,
* knowledge graph relationships.

Unlike patient data, knowledge assets are version-controlled and governed through formal review processes.

---

# 20.7.6 Entity Relationships

The principal cardinalities within the conceptual model are summarized below.

| Relationship                             | Cardinality |
| ---------------------------------------- | ----------- |
| User → Health Profile                    | 1 : 1       |
| User → Nutrition Assessments             | 1 : Many    |
| Nutrition Assessment → Recommendations   | 1 : Many    |
| Recommendation → Explanation             | 1 : 1       |
| Recommendation → Activated Rules         | Many : Many |
| Activated Rule → Knowledge Module        | Many : 1    |
| Knowledge Module → Ontology Concepts     | 1 : Many    |
| Ontology Concept → Knowledge Graph Nodes | 1 : Many    |
| User → Monitoring Records                | 1 : Many    |

These relationships will later be transformed into relational associations within the PostgreSQL schema.

---

# 20.7.7 Value Objects

Several concepts exist solely to describe the state of larger entities.

Typical value objects include:

| Value Object            | Description                      |
| ----------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| BMI                     | Calculated body mass index       |
| Waist Circumference     | Anthropometric measurement       |
| Blood Pressure          | Clinical observation             |
| Physical Activity Level | Lifestyle classification         |
| Dietary Diversity Score | Nutritional assessment indicator |
| Portion Size            | Meal planning quantity           |
| Nutrient Intake         | Calculated nutrient values       |
| Recommendation Priority | Clinical urgency                 |

Value objects possess no independent identity and are always owned by an aggregate.

---

# 20.7.8 Domain Events and Persistence

Several domain events require persistent recording to support auditability and workflow coordination.

Examples include:

| Domain Event            | Purpose                  |
| ----------------------- | ------------------------ |
| UserRegistered          | Account creation         |
| ProfileUpdated          | Demographic changes      |
| AssessmentSubmitted     | Completion of assessment |
| RecommendationGenerated | Completion of inference  |
| RecommendationAccepted  | Patient acknowledgement  |
| FollowUpScheduled       | Monitoring workflow      |
| KnowledgeModuleApproved | Governance               |
| RuleVersionPublished    | Knowledge evolution      |

Persisting these events enables complete reconstruction of the patient's clinical journey and supports governance requirements.

---

# 20.7.9 Integration with the Knowledge Model

The conceptual data model is tightly integrated with the semantic architecture established in earlier chapters.

The relationship can be summarized as follows:

```text
Patient Assessment
          │
          ▼
Domain Entity
          │
          ▼
Ontology Concept
          │
          ▼
Knowledge Graph Node
          │
          ▼
Symbolic Rule
          │
          ▼
Inference Result
          │
          ▼
Recommendation Entity
```

This integration demonstrates that the persistence architecture is fundamentally knowledge-centric rather than purely transactional.

---

# 20.7.10 Conceptual Integrity

The conceptual model satisfies several important architectural goals:

* **Consistency:** All implementation artefacts derive from a unified domain representation.
* **Clinical Traceability:** Every recommendation is linked to its originating assessment and supporting knowledge.
* **Knowledge Traceability:** Rules, ontology concepts, and graph relationships remain explicitly connected.
* **Longitudinal Support:** Historical assessments and recommendations are preserved.
* **Explainability:** Evidence and reasoning traces are modeled as first-class entities.
* **Future Extensibility:** The model accommodates machine learning modules, FHIR interoperability, wearable integrations, and advanced analytics without restructuring the core domain.

---

# 20.7.11 Transition to the Logical Data Model

The conceptual model presented in this section is intentionally technology-independent. It captures the business semantics and clinical relationships that define the NutriLogic domain without prescribing their physical realization. In the following section, these conceptual aggregates and relationships will be transformed into a logical relational data model suitable for implementation in PostgreSQL and the Django ORM, while preserving the aggregate boundaries, domain invariants, and semantic traceability established throughout the architecture.

---

# 20.8 Persistence Mapping Strategy

---

## 20.8.1 Introduction

The conceptual data model presented in the previous section defines the clinical and business semantics of the NutriLogic domain independently of any persistence technology. The role of the persistence mapping strategy is to systematically transform these conceptual models into physical storage structures while preserving the architectural principles established throughout the dissertation.

Unlike conventional information systems, NutriLogic persists heterogeneous information, including transactional clinical records, semantic knowledge assets, symbolic reasoning artefacts, governance metadata, and explainability traces. Consequently, persistence cannot be achieved through direct object-relational mapping alone. Instead, specialized mapping strategies are employed for each category of information while maintaining a unified repository abstraction.

This section defines the mapping principles that guide the implementation of the persistence layer and ensure consistency across PostgreSQL, the symbolic knowledge base, and semantic resources.

---

# 20.8.2 Mapping Philosophy

Persistence within NutriLogic follows the principle that **the domain model is authoritative**, while storage technologies are implementation details.

Accordingly:

* Domain entities are not designed around relational tables.
* Aggregate boundaries determine transaction boundaries.
* Repositories isolate business logic from storage mechanisms.
* Symbolic knowledge remains external to relational persistence.
* Semantic artefacts retain explicit mappings to domain entities.

The persistence strategy therefore preserves the integrity of the domain model while allowing storage technologies to evolve independently.

---

# 20.8.3 Mapping Layers

Persistence occurs across multiple complementary layers.

```text
                 Domain Layer
                       │
            Domain Entities
            Value Objects
            Aggregates
                       │
                       ▼
          Repository Interfaces
                       │
                       ▼
        Persistence Mapping Layer
                       │
      ┌────────┬──────────┬───────────┐
      │        │          │           │
      ▼        ▼          ▼           ▼
 PostgreSQL  Ontology   RDF Graph   Prolog KB
```

The mapping layer functions as the translation boundary between the technology-independent domain model and the physical persistence technologies.

---

# 20.8.4 Aggregate Mapping Strategy

Each aggregate root introduced in Chapter 19 maps to a corresponding persistence boundary.

| Aggregate Root        | Persistence Strategy                       |
| --------------------- | ------------------------------------------ |
| User                  | Primary relational table                   |
| Nutrition Assessment  | Aggregate root with dependent tables       |
| Recommendation        | Aggregate root with explanation references |
| Monitoring Record     | Longitudinal relational records            |
| Knowledge Module      | Version-controlled metadata                |
| Knowledge Module      | External Prolog modules with metadata      |
| ExplanationSession    | Audit-oriented relational storage          |

Each aggregate is persisted atomically to preserve domain consistency and prevent partial updates.

---

# 20.8.5 Entity Mapping

Entities possessing independent identity are mapped to dedicated relational tables.

Examples include:

| Domain Entity       | Database Representation |
| ------------------- | ----------------------- |
| User                | `users`                 |
| HealthProfile       | `health_profiles`       |
| NutritionAssessment | `nutrition_assessments` |
| Recommendation      | `recommendations`       |
| MonitoringRecord    | `monitoring_records`    |
| AuditRecord         | `audit_records`         |

Each entity receives a stable primary key and maintains referential integrity through foreign key relationships.

---

# 20.8.6 Value Object Mapping

Unlike entities, value objects possess no independent identity.

Consequently, they are persisted as attributes of their owning aggregates rather than separate tables unless specific reuse or historical tracking requirements justify normalization.

Examples include:

| Value Object           | Mapping Strategy                        |
| ---------------------- | --------------------------------------- |
| BMI                    | Embedded numeric columns                |
| BloodPressure          | Embedded systolic and diastolic columns |
| PhysicalActivityLevel  | Enumeration                             |
| RecommendationPriority | Enumeration                             |
| DietaryDiversityScore  | Numeric attribute                       |
| PortionSize            | Decimal attribute                       |
| NutrientIntake         | Embedded nutritional fields             |

This approach reduces unnecessary joins while preserving aggregate cohesion.

---

# 20.8.7 Aggregate Boundary Mapping

Aggregate boundaries determine transactional consistency.

For example:

```text
NutritionAssessment

├── AnthropometricAssessment
├── ClinicalAssessment
├── DietaryAssessment
├── LifestyleAssessment
├── LaboratoryAssessment
└── SymptomAssessment
```

Although these components may occupy multiple relational tables, they are persisted within a single transactional boundary.

This guarantees that incomplete assessments cannot exist within the database.

---

# 20.8.8 Repository Mapping

Repositories provide the only persistence interface visible to the Application Layer.

```text
Application Service
        │
        ▼
Assessment Repository
        │
        ▼
Django ORM
        │
        ▼
PostgreSQL
```

The repository is responsible for:

* loading aggregates,
* reconstructing value objects,
* enforcing optimistic consistency,
* persisting transactional changes,
* hiding ORM implementation details.

Business logic therefore remains independent of database technology.

---

# 20.8.9 Domain Event Mapping

Domain events are persisted separately from transactional entities.

Examples include:

| Event                   | Persistence Purpose     |
| ----------------------- | ----------------------- |
| AssessmentSubmitted     | Workflow reconstruction |
| RecommendationGenerated | Clinical audit          |
| KnowledgeModuleApproved | Governance              |
| RuleVersionPublished    | Version history         |
| FollowUpScheduled       | Monitoring              |

Persisting domain events supports explainability, auditing, and future event-driven extensions.

---

# 20.8.10 Ontology Mapping

Ontology concepts are **not** stored as conventional relational entities.

Instead, relational records maintain references to ontology identifiers.

Example:

```text
Recommendation

↓

ontology_concept_id

↓

OWL Concept

↓

Nutrition Ontology
```

This separation preserves semantic interoperability while preventing duplication of knowledge definitions within operational tables.

---

# 20.8.11 Knowledge Graph Mapping

Knowledge graph entities represent semantic relationships rather than transactional records.

Accordingly:

```text
Clinical Observation

↓

Ontology Concept

↓

Knowledge Graph Node

↓

Semantic Relationship

↓

Inference
```

Operational data reference graph concepts without embedding graph structures directly into relational tables.

This enables future migration to dedicated graph databases such as Neo4j or RDF triple stores without modifying the Domain Layer.

---

# 20.8.12 Symbolic Rule Mapping

Clinical rules remain external to the relational database.

Instead:

```text
Knowledge Module

↓

Rule Metadata

↓

Prolog Module

↓

SWI-Prolog
```

Only governance information—including version, approval status, effective date, author, and provenance—is persisted relationally.

The executable rules remain version-controlled Prolog source files, preserving readability, maintainability, and compatibility with the symbolic inference engine.

---

# 20.8.13 Explainability Mapping

Explainability is treated as a first-class persistence concern.

Each recommendation stores references to:

* originating assessment,
* activated rules,
* ontology concepts,
* evidence chain,
* reasoning trace,
* guideline version,
* explanation narrative.

This enables complete reconstruction of every clinical recommendation for audit, review, and user-facing explanations.

---

# 20.8.14 Mapping Summary

The persistence mapping strategy can be summarized as follows.

| Domain Concept       | Persistence Representation |
| -------------------- | -------------------------- |
| Entity               | Relational Table           |
| Value Object         | Embedded Attributes        |
| Aggregate            | Transaction Boundary       |
| Repository           | ORM Adapter                |
| Domain Event         | Event Store / Audit Tables |
| Ontology Concept     | Semantic Identifier        |
| Knowledge Graph Node | Graph Representation       |
| Symbolic Rule        | External Prolog Module     |
| Reasoning Trace      | Relational Audit Record    |

---

# 20.8.15 Architectural Rationale

This persistence mapping strategy realizes several architectural objectives established throughout the dissertation:

* **Domain integrity** by ensuring that storage structures reflect aggregate boundaries.
* **Technology independence** by isolating persistence concerns behind repositories.
* **Semantic consistency** by linking operational data to ontology concepts rather than duplicating knowledge.
* **Explainability** by preserving reasoning traces and evidence chains.
* **Knowledge governance** by separating executable symbolic rules from transactional data while maintaining explicit metadata.
* **Future extensibility** through compatibility with graph databases, ontology repositories, FHIR resources, and additional neuro-symbolic components.

---

# 20.8.16 Transition to the Logical Relational Model

With the persistence mapping strategy established, the architecture is now ready to transition from conceptual domain aggregates to a concrete logical relational model. The next section will derive the normalized relational schema from the aggregate structures defined in Sections 20.7 and 20.8, specifying entities, relationships, cardinalities, keys, and constraints that will subsequently be implemented using PostgreSQL and the Django ORM.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.9 Data Classification and Storage Architecture

---

## 20.9.1 Introduction

An effective persistence architecture begins by understanding the nature of the information being managed. Although all data contribute to the operation of NutriLogic, they differ significantly in their purpose, lifecycle, ownership, consistency requirements, and governance obligations. Treating all information as homogeneous relational data would obscure these differences and increase system complexity.

Accordingly, NutriLogic adopts a **data classification and storage architecture** that organizes information into distinct categories according to its semantic role within the system. Each category is associated with an appropriate persistence strategy while remaining accessible through the unified repository abstractions established in Chapter 19.

This classification provides the conceptual bridge between the persistence mapping strategy (Section 20.8) and the logical database design presented in the subsequent sections.

---

# 20.9.2 Data Classification Principles

The classification strategy is guided by six principles:

1. **Clinical relevance:** Data are organized according to their role in the Nutrition Care Process.
2. **Knowledge separation:** Operational data are distinguished from reusable clinical knowledge.
3. **Lifecycle awareness:** Data with different retention and update patterns are managed independently.
4. **Governance alignment:** Storage strategies support auditability, provenance, and regulatory compliance.
5. **Explainability preservation:** Information required to reconstruct clinical reasoning is retained.
6. **Future extensibility:** New data sources (e.g., wearables, FHIR resources, ML outputs) can be incorporated without redesigning the architecture.

---

# 20.9.3 Primary Data Domains

The persistence architecture recognizes six principal data domains.

```text id="2o8m9k"
                    NutriLogic Data

        ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
        │              │              │
        ▼              ▼              ▼
 Clinical Data   Knowledge Assets   Semantic Data
        │              │              │
        ├──────────────┼──────────────┤
        ▼              ▼              ▼
 Governance     Explainability   Operational Data
```

Each domain exhibits unique characteristics and therefore requires an appropriate persistence strategy.

---

# 20.9.4 Clinical Data

Clinical data describe the health status and care activities of individual patients. These records are transactional, longitudinal, and subject to strict confidentiality requirements.

Typical examples include:

* user accounts,
* demographic profiles,
* anthropometric measurements,
* dietary assessments,
* lifestyle observations,
* laboratory values,
* nutrition diagnoses,
* recommendations,
* follow-up records.

### Characteristics

| Property              | Description      |
| --------------------- | ---------------- |
| Ownership             | Patient-specific |
| Update Frequency      | High             |
| Persistence           | Long-term        |
| Integrity Requirement | Very High        |
| Confidentiality       | Very High        |
| Primary Storage       | PostgreSQL       |

Clinical data form the operational core of the Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System.

---

# 20.9.5 Knowledge Assets

Knowledge assets represent reusable clinical expertise rather than patient-specific information. They provide the evidence base from which recommendations are generated.

Examples include:

* Ministry of Health nutrition guidelines,
* Kenya Nutrient Profile Model,
* therapeutic diet definitions,
* nutrient reference values,
* food composition knowledge,
* clinical protocols,
* rule metadata.

Unlike clinical records, these assets evolve through controlled governance processes rather than routine patient interactions.

### Characteristics

| Property         | Description                                                        |
| ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Ownership        | System-wide                                                        |
| Update Frequency | Low                                                                |
| Governance       | Formal review and approval                                         |
| Versioning       | Mandatory                                                          |
| Primary Storage  | Version-controlled resources with metadata persisted in PostgreSQL |

---

# 20.9.6 Semantic Data

Semantic data capture the relationships among clinical concepts that support interoperability and reasoning.

This category includes:

* ontology classes,
* concept hierarchies,
* RDF entities,
* knowledge graph nodes,
* semantic relationships,
* controlled vocabularies.

Semantic information enables consistent interpretation of clinical concepts across the symbolic reasoning engine and future interoperable systems.

### Characteristics

| Property         | Description                                                              |
| ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Structure        | Graph-oriented                                                           |
| Update Frequency | Low                                                                      |
| Versioning       | Mandatory                                                                |
| Interoperability | High                                                                     |
| Current Storage  | Ontology files, RDF serializations, and metadata persisted in PostgreSQL |

---

# 20.9.7 Explainability Data

Explainability data provide the evidence necessary to understand, validate, and audit the recommendations generated by the symbolic reasoning engine.

Examples include:

* activated rules,
* reasoning traces,
* evidence chains,
* ontology concepts referenced,
* confidence annotations (where applicable),
* recommendation justifications.

These data support clinician trust and facilitate retrospective review of decision-making processes.

### Characteristics

| Property            | Description                                           |
| ------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- |
| Ownership           | Recommendation-specific                               |
| Lifecycle           | Longitudinal                                          |
| Clinical Importance | High                                                  |
| Storage             | PostgreSQL audit structures linked to recommendations |

---

# 20.9.8 Governance Data

Governance data ensure accountability, traceability, and controlled evolution of the knowledge base.

Examples include:

* rule versions,
* ontology versions,
* approval records,
* reviewer identities,
* publication dates,
* provenance metadata,
* change history.

These records are essential for demonstrating that recommendations are generated from approved and traceable knowledge sources.

### Characteristics

| Property         | Description                  |
| ---------------- | ---------------------------- |
| Purpose          | Governance and compliance    |
| Update Frequency | Low                          |
| Retention        | Long-term                    |
| Storage          | PostgreSQL governance tables |

---

# 20.9.9 Operational Data

Operational data describe the functioning of the software platform rather than the clinical domain.

Examples include:

* authentication logs,
* API usage,
* background job status,
* performance metrics,
* cache statistics,
* scheduled task execution,
* error logs,
* deployment metadata.

Although these data do not directly influence clinical recommendations, they are essential for monitoring, troubleshooting, and maintaining the system.

### Characteristics

| Property         | Description                                       |
| ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------- |
| Purpose          | System operations                                 |
| Update Frequency | High                                              |
| Retention        | Configurable                                      |
| Storage          | PostgreSQL and application logging infrastructure |

---

# 20.9.10 Data Lifecycle Classification

Each category of information progresses through a characteristic lifecycle.

```text id="8jv3wp"
Creation
    │
    ▼
Validation
    │
    ▼
Storage
    │
    ▼
Usage
    │
    ▼
Versioning
    │
    ▼
Archiving
    │
    ▼
Retention / Disposal
```

While this lifecycle applies broadly, individual stages differ between clinical records, knowledge assets, and operational data.

---

# 20.9.11 Data Ownership Matrix

The following matrix summarizes ownership and stewardship responsibilities across the major data domains.

| Data Domain         | Primary Owner                                                | Stewardship                                |
| ------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------ |
| Clinical Data       | Patient (subject to applicable law and institutional policy) | Healthcare provider / System administrator |
| Knowledge Assets    | Knowledge engineering team                                   | Clinical governance committee              |
| Semantic Data       | Knowledge engineering team                                   | Ontology governance team                   |
| Explainability Data | System                                                       | Clinical governance committee              |
| Governance Data     | System administrators                                        | Clinical governance committee              |
| Operational Data    | System                                                       | Platform administrators                    |

This separation of ownership supports accountability and controlled access.

---

# 20.9.12 Storage Strategy Matrix

The relationship between data categories and persistence technologies is summarized below.

| Data Category          | Logical Store             | Current Physical Storage                           | Future Evolution                                 |
| ---------------------- | ------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |
| Clinical Records       | Clinical Repository       | PostgreSQL                                         | PostgreSQL cluster / partitioning                |
| Knowledge Assets       | Knowledge Repository      | Prolog modules, OWL/RDF files, PostgreSQL metadata | Dedicated knowledge repository                   |
| Semantic Relationships | Semantic Repository       | RDF serialization                                  | Graph database (e.g., Neo4j or RDF triple store) |
| Explainability         | Explainability Repository | PostgreSQL                                         | Dedicated reasoning store if required            |
| Governance             | Governance Repository     | PostgreSQL                                         | Integrated governance platform                   |
| Operational Data       | Operational Repository    | PostgreSQL + logs                                  | Centralized observability stack                  |

This matrix emphasizes the separation between the **logical architecture** and the **physical implementation**, allowing future evolution without disrupting the domain model.

---

# 20.9.13 Security Classification

Different categories of information require different levels of protection.

| Data Category       | Classification   | Protection Requirements             |
| ------------------- | ---------------- | ----------------------------------- |
| Clinical Data       | Highly Sensitive | Encryption, RBAC, audit logging     |
| Knowledge Assets    | Restricted       | Version control, approval workflow  |
| Semantic Data       | Internal         | Integrity protection and provenance |
| Explainability Data | Sensitive        | Controlled access and auditability  |
| Governance Data     | Restricted       | Immutable audit history             |
| Operational Data    | Internal         | Access control and monitoring       |

This classification supports the security architecture presented in Chapter 16.

---

# 20.9.14 Architectural Rationale

The proposed data classification architecture offers several advantages:

* It distinguishes transactional information from reusable knowledge.
* It enables governance processes to evolve independently of clinical workflows.
* It preserves semantic interoperability by separating ontology and graph resources from patient records.
* It supports explainable AI by treating reasoning artefacts as first-class persistent data.
* It prepares the platform for future technologies without requiring changes to the domain model or application services.

---

# 20.9.15 Transition to the Logical Relational Model

Having classified the information managed by NutriLogic according to its clinical, semantic, governance, and operational roles, the persistence architecture now has a clear basis for determining how each category will be represented within the relational database. The next section introduces the **Logical Relational Data Model**, where the conceptual aggregates and classified data domains are translated into normalized entities, relationships, keys, and integrity constraints suitable for implementation in PostgreSQL and the Django ORM.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.10 Logical Relational Data Model

---

## 20.10.1 Introduction

The conceptual data model presented in Section 20.7 captures the clinical semantics of the NutriLogic domain independently of implementation technologies. Sections 20.8 and 20.9 subsequently established the persistence mapping strategy and classified the information managed by the platform according to its clinical, semantic, governance, and operational roles. This section translates those conceptual constructs into a logical relational model suitable for implementation using PostgreSQL and the Django ORM.

The logical relational model defines the persistent entities, their attributes, keys, relationships, integrity constraints, and normalization strategy while preserving the aggregate boundaries established by the Domain-Driven Design (DDD) architecture. The objective is not merely to design database tables but to ensure that the persistence layer faithfully realizes the domain model, supports the Nutrition Care Process (NCP), maintains semantic traceability, and enables explainable neuro-symbolic reasoning.

The logical model therefore serves as the definitive blueprint from which the physical database schema, Django ORM models, repository implementations, REST API resources, and data migration scripts are derived.

---

# 20.10.2 Design Objectives

The logical relational model has been designed to satisfy the following objectives:

1. Preserve domain aggregate boundaries while supporting relational storage.
2. Achieve Third Normal Form (3NF) for operational data, minimizing redundancy and update anomalies.
3. Maintain referential integrity through explicit primary and foreign key relationships.
4. Support longitudinal clinical records without destructive updates.
5. Enable traceability between patient data, symbolic reasoning, ontology concepts, and recommendations.
6. Facilitate efficient querying for clinical workflows and reporting.
7. Provide a stable foundation for future interoperability (e.g., HL7 FHIR mappings) and analytical extensions.

These objectives ensure that the logical schema supports both operational requirements and the research goals of the dissertation.

---

# 20.10.3 Normalization Strategy

The schema adopts a hybrid normalization strategy.

* **Transactional entities** (e.g., users, assessments, recommendations) are normalized to Third Normal Form (3NF) to eliminate redundancy and maintain consistency.
* **Reference data** (e.g., counties, food categories, nutrient definitions) are stored in dedicated lookup tables.
* **Value objects** that are tightly coupled to their owning aggregate (e.g., BMI, blood pressure) are embedded within the parent entity unless historical tracking or independent querying is required.
* **Semantic artefacts** (ontology identifiers, graph references, rule identifiers) are referenced rather than duplicated.

This approach balances data integrity with implementation simplicity and query performance.

---

# 20.10.4 Core Entity Groups

The logical schema is organized into eight principal entity groups that correspond directly to the bounded contexts defined in Chapter 19.

| Entity Group              | Purpose                                              |
| ------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- |
| Identity & Access         | User authentication and authorization                |
| Patient Profile           | Longitudinal demographic and health information      |
| Clinical Assessment       | Nutrition assessment episodes and observations       |
| Clinical Decision Support | Diagnoses, recommendations, meal plans, explanations |
| Monitoring & Follow-up    | Progress tracking and outcome evaluation             |
| Knowledge Management      | Knowledge assets, ontology metadata, rule metadata   |
| Governance & Audit        | Versioning, approvals, reasoning traces, audit logs  |
| Platform Operations       | System configuration, jobs, notifications, logs      |

This grouping improves modularity and supports future schema evolution.

---

# 20.10.5 High-Level Logical Entity Model

The major entities and their relationships are illustrated conceptually below.

```text
                           USERS
                             │
                    1 ───────┴────── 1
                             │
                      HEALTH_PROFILES
                             │
                    1 ───────┴────── N
                             │
                NUTRITION_ASSESSMENTS
                             │
          ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐
          │                  │                  │
          ▼                  ▼                  ▼
 CLINICAL_OBSERVATIONS  DIETARY_LOGS   LAB_RESULTS
                             │
                             ▼
                  RECOMMENDATIONS
                             │
          ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐
          │                  │                  │
          ▼                  ▼                  ▼
      MEAL_PLANS     EXPLANATIONS     REASONING_TRACES
                             │
                             ▼
                    ACTIVATED_RULES
                             │
                             ▼
                  KNOWLEDGE_MODULES
                             │
                             ▼
                 ONTOLOGY_CONCEPTS
                             │
                             ▼
                  KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH
```

This logical model demonstrates that clinical recommendations are derived through explicit links between patient assessments, symbolic reasoning, and semantic knowledge assets.

---

# 20.10.6 Identity and Access Model

The identity subsystem manages authentication, authorization, and user lifecycle.

### Primary Entities

| Entity        | Description             |
| ------------- | ----------------------- |
| `users`       | Registered system users |
| `roles`       | Role definitions        |
| `permissions` | Access permissions      |
| `user_roles`  | User-role assignments   |

Relationships:

```text
USER

│ 1

├────────────── N USER_ROLES N ────────────── 1 ROLE

│

└────────────── N AUDIT_LOG
```

The separation of users, roles, and permissions enables flexible role-based access control (RBAC), supporting clinicians, administrators, nutritionists, and patients.

---

# 20.10.7 Patient Profile Model

Each registered user has a single longitudinal health profile containing relatively stable information.

| Entity               | Description                                            |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| `health_profiles`    | Core patient demographics and baseline characteristics |
| `medical_histories`  | Historical conditions and comorbidities                |
| `allergies`          | Known food or medication allergies                     |
| `lifestyle_profiles` | Smoking, alcohol use, physical activity, occupation    |

The one-to-one relationship between `users` and `health_profiles` separates authentication concerns from clinical information while supporting future integration with external identity providers.

---

# 20.10.8 Clinical Assessment Model

The Nutrition Assessment aggregate is represented using a parent-child structure.

```text
NUTRITION_ASSESSMENT

├── Anthropometric Assessment

├── Clinical Assessment

├── Dietary Assessment

├── Lifestyle Assessment

├── Laboratory Assessment

└── Symptom Assessment
```

Each child entity is linked to a single assessment record, ensuring that the complete assessment can be managed as a cohesive aggregate while allowing detailed storage of specialized observations.

---

# 20.10.9 Clinical Decision Support Model

The decision support subsystem persists the outputs of the symbolic reasoning engine.

Primary entities include:

| Entity                | Description                        |
| --------------------- | ---------------------------------- |
| `recommendations`     | Clinical nutrition recommendations |
| `meal_plans`          | Structured dietary interventions   |
| `nutrition_diagnoses` | Nutrition problem statements       |
| `interventions`       | Recommended clinical actions       |
| `explanations`        | Human-readable reasoning summaries |

Every recommendation references:

* the originating assessment,
* the activated symbolic rules,
* the supporting ontology concepts,
* the knowledge module version used during inference.

This design ensures full traceability and reproducibility.

---

# 20.10.10 Knowledge Management Model

Knowledge assets are represented through metadata rather than executable content.

| Entity                      | Purpose                                   |
| --------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
| `knowledge_modules`         | Logical collections of clinical knowledge |
| `rule_metadata`             | Metadata describing Prolog rule modules   |
| `ontology_metadata`         | Ontology versions and provenance          |
| `guideline_versions`        | Ministry of Health guideline versions     |
| `food_composition_metadata` | Food database versions                    |

The executable Prolog files and OWL ontologies remain external resources, referenced through these metadata entities.

---

# 20.10.11 Governance and Audit Model

Clinical governance requires comprehensive recording of system activity.

Key entities include:

| Entity                | Purpose                              |
| --------------------- | ------------------------------------ |
| `audit_logs`          | User and system actions              |
| `reasoning_traces`    | Symbolic inference execution records |
| `knowledge_approvals` | Governance workflow                  |
| `rule_versions`       | Historical rule publications         |
| `ontology_versions`   | Ontology lifecycle management        |

These entities enable retrospective analysis, regulatory reporting, and quality assurance.

---

# 20.10.12 Integrity Constraints

The logical model enforces several categories of integrity constraints.

### Entity Integrity

* Every table contains a unique primary key.
* Primary keys are immutable.

### Referential Integrity

* Foreign keys ensure valid relationships between entities.
* Cascading deletes are restricted for clinical records to preserve historical information.

### Domain Integrity

* Clinical measurements must fall within medically plausible ranges.
* Enumerated fields enforce controlled vocabularies.
* Mandatory relationships prevent incomplete assessment records.

### Business Integrity

* Recommendations cannot exist without an originating assessment.
* Assessments cannot exist without an associated patient.
* Rule metadata must reference an approved knowledge module.

---

# 20.10.13 Traceability Relationships

A defining feature of the logical model is explicit traceability.

```text
Patient

↓

Assessment

↓

Recommendation

↓

Reasoning Trace

↓

Activated Rules

↓

Knowledge Module

↓

Guideline Version

↓

Ontology Concept
```

This chain ensures that every recommendation can be traced back to both the patient data that triggered it and the knowledge sources that justified it.

---

# 20.10.14 Relationship Summary

| Parent Entity     | Child Entity      | Cardinality |
| ----------------- | ----------------- | ----------- |
| User              | Health Profile    | 1 : 1       |
| User              | Assessment        | 1 : N       |
| Assessment        | Recommendation    | 1 : N       |
| Recommendation    | Explanation       | 1 : 1       |
| Recommendation    | Meal Plan         | 1 : N       |
| Recommendation    | Reasoning Trace   | 1 : 1       |
| Reasoning Trace   | Activated Rule    | N : N       |
| Rule Metadata     | Knowledge Module  | N : 1       |
| Knowledge Module  | Guideline Version | N : 1       |
| Guideline Version | Ontology Metadata | N : 1       |

---

# 20.10.15 Architectural Significance

The logical relational model is more than a normalized database schema; it is the persistent realization of the Domain-Driven Architecture developed throughout this dissertation. By preserving aggregate boundaries, explicit traceability, and semantic references, the model supports clinical safety, explainability, governance, and future interoperability while remaining implementation-independent.

---

# 20.10.16 Transition to the Entity–Relationship Model

The logical relational model defines the entities and relationships that constitute the persistence architecture of NutriLogic. The next section presents the **Entity–Relationship Diagram (ERD)**, providing a visual representation of these structures and serving as the immediate precursor to the physical PostgreSQL schema and Django ORM implementation.

---

# CHAPTER 20

# 20.11 Information Architecture

---

## 20.11.1 Introduction

Information is the primary asset managed by NutriLogic. While the preceding sections defined the conceptual domain model, persistence philosophy, and logical relational structure, this section focuses on the organization, ownership, flow, governance, and lifecycle of information across the platform.

The Information Architecture establishes a coherent framework for managing clinical observations, nutritional knowledge, semantic resources, reasoning artefacts, governance metadata, and operational information. It ensures that information remains consistent, traceable, secure, and reusable throughout the Nutrition Care Process (NCP).

Unlike a database schema, which specifies how data are stored, the Information Architecture specifies **what information exists, who owns it, how it moves through the system, and how it is transformed into clinical knowledge and actionable recommendations**.

---

# 20.11.2 Information Architecture Objectives

The Information Architecture has the following objectives:

1. Organize information according to business meaning rather than implementation technology.
2. Establish authoritative information domains and ownership responsibilities.
3. Enable traceability from patient observations to generated recommendations.
4. Support semantic interoperability through explicit ontology mappings.
5. Preserve explainability by maintaining reasoning provenance.
6. Facilitate longitudinal clinical care through structured information lifecycles.
7. Provide a stable foundation for future interoperability standards and advanced analytics.

These objectives complement the persistence architecture by ensuring that stored information retains its semantic integrity and clinical value.

---

# 20.11.3 Information Domains

The information managed by NutriLogic is organized into seven major domains.

```text
                    NutriLogic Information

        ┌──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐
        │              │              │
        ▼              ▼              ▼
 Patient        Clinical Care      Knowledge
 Information      Information        Assets
        │              │              │
        ├──────────────┼──────────────┤
        ▼              ▼              ▼
 Semantic      Explainability    Governance
 Information      Information     Information
                     │
                     ▼
             Operational Information
```

Each domain has distinct responsibilities, ownership, and governance requirements.

---

# 20.11.4 Patient Information Domain

This domain contains information that uniquely identifies or describes an individual receiving nutritional care.

Typical information includes:

* user identity,
* demographic profile,
* anthropometric measurements,
* medical history,
* allergies,
* lifestyle characteristics,
* contact information.

### Characteristics

| Property         | Description                   |
| ---------------- | ----------------------------- |
| Business Owner   | Patient / Healthcare Provider |
| Confidentiality  | Very High                     |
| Availability     | High                          |
| Retention        | Longitudinal                  |
| Update Frequency | Moderate                      |

This information forms the foundation for individualized nutritional assessment and personalized recommendations.

---

# 20.11.5 Clinical Care Information Domain

The Clinical Care Information Domain captures information generated during the Nutrition Care Process.

Examples include:

* nutrition assessments,
* nutrition diagnoses,
* interventions,
* meal plans,
* follow-up records,
* monitoring outcomes.

### Information Flow

```text
Assessment

↓

Diagnosis

↓

Recommendation

↓

Meal Plan

↓

Monitoring

↓

Reassessment
```

This reflects the cyclical nature of evidence-based nutritional care.

---

# 20.11.6 Knowledge Domain

The Knowledge Domain stores reusable clinical expertise rather than patient-specific data.

Knowledge assets include:

* Kenyan Ministry of Health nutrition guidelines,
* Kenya Nutrient Profile Model,
* nutrient reference values,
* food composition information,
* dietary intervention protocols,
* symbolic rule metadata.

Unlike clinical information, these assets evolve through structured governance and version control processes.

---

# 20.11.7 Semantic Information Domain

Semantic information provides explicit meaning to clinical concepts and enables interoperability.

This domain includes:

* ontology concepts,
* concept hierarchies,
* RDF resources,
* knowledge graph nodes,
* semantic relationships,
* controlled vocabularies.

Semantic information links operational data to formal knowledge representations, enabling consistent interpretation by both humans and reasoning systems.

---

# 20.11.8 Explainability Information Domain

Explainability information records the reasoning process underlying each recommendation.

Examples include:

* activated symbolic rules,
* reasoning traces,
* evidence chains,
* supporting ontology concepts,
* guideline references,
* explanation narratives.

By treating explainability as a first-class information domain, NutriLogic ensures that recommendations remain transparent and auditable.

---

# 20.11.9 Governance Information Domain

Governance information supports the controlled evolution of the system's knowledge resources and clinical policies.

Typical records include:

* knowledge module approvals,
* ontology releases,
* rule publication histories,
* provenance metadata,
* reviewer comments,
* change logs.

These records ensure that recommendations can always be traced to approved and versioned knowledge sources.

---

# 20.11.10 Operational Information Domain

Operational information describes the functioning of the platform itself.

Examples include:

* authentication events,
* API requests,
* background processing,
* job queues,
* deployment metadata,
* system health metrics,
* application logs.

Although not directly involved in clinical reasoning, these records support monitoring, maintenance, and reliability.

---

# 20.11.11 Information Lifecycle

Information progresses through a managed lifecycle.

```text
Capture

↓

Validation

↓

Transformation

↓

Reasoning

↓

Recommendation

↓

Monitoring

↓

Archiving

↓

Retention
```

Each stage incorporates validation and governance controls to preserve information quality and clinical safety.

---

# 20.11.12 Information Flow Across the Architecture

The interaction of the information domains is illustrated below.

```text
Patient Information
        │
        ▼
Clinical Assessment
        │
        ▼
Application Services
        │
        ▼
Knowledge Layer
        │
        ▼
SWI-Prolog Reasoning
        │
        ▼
Recommendation
        │
        ▼
Explainability Layer
        │
        ▼
Monitoring & Follow-up
```

This end-to-end flow demonstrates how raw observations are transformed into explainable clinical recommendations and subsequently incorporated into longitudinal patient care.

---

# 20.11.13 Information Governance Principles

The Information Architecture is governed by the following principles:

* **Authoritative Source:** Every information element has a defined system of record.
* **Single Responsibility:** Information is stored once and referenced where needed.
* **Traceability:** Every recommendation can be traced to its originating observations and knowledge sources.
* **Integrity:** Validation rules prevent inconsistent or incomplete information.
* **Confidentiality:** Access is restricted according to user roles and organizational responsibilities.
* **Lifecycle Management:** Information is retained, archived, and disposed of according to governance policies and applicable legal requirements.
* **Semantic Consistency:** Clinical concepts maintain stable identifiers across the ontology, knowledge graph, and symbolic rule base.

---

# 20.11.14 Information Architecture Matrix

| Information Domain   | Primary Producer              | Primary Consumer            | Storage                                            |
| -------------------- | ----------------------------- | --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| Patient Information  | Patient / Clinician           | Assessment Services         | PostgreSQL                                         |
| Clinical Care        | Assessment Services           | Inference Engine            | PostgreSQL                                         |
| Knowledge Assets     | Knowledge Engineers           | SWI-Prolog                  | Prolog modules, OWL/RDF files, PostgreSQL metadata |
| Semantic Information | Ontology Team                 | Knowledge Graph & Inference | OWL/RDF files, metadata                            |
| Explainability       | Inference Engine              | Clinicians & Patients       | PostgreSQL                                         |
| Governance           | Clinical Governance Committee | Administrators              | PostgreSQL                                         |
| Operational          | Platform Services             | Administrators              | PostgreSQL & logging infrastructure                |

This matrix clarifies how information moves between stakeholders and system components while maintaining clear ownership.

---
