# 20.11.15 Transition to the Entity–Relationship Architecture

With the Information Architecture defined, the dissertation now has a complete understanding of the information assets managed by NutriLogic, their ownership, governance, and lifecycle. The following sections translate these information domains into a series of layered Entity–Relationship Diagrams (ERDs) that progressively refine the persistence architecture from enterprise-level information groups to detailed relational structures.

---

# Chapter 20

# 20.12 Enterprise Entity–Relationship Architecture

---

## 20.12.1 Introduction

The Enterprise Entity–Relationship Architecture (Enterprise ERD) provides a high-level view of the persistent information landscape of NutriLogic. Unlike the detailed ERDs presented in later sections, which focus on specific bounded contexts, the Enterprise ERD identifies the major business entities and illustrates how they interact across the entire Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System (CNDSS).

The Enterprise ERD serves as the conceptual blueprint for the physical PostgreSQL schema and ensures that all persistence components remain aligned with the Domain-Driven Design (DDD) architecture established in Chapter 19.

Its primary objective is to demonstrate how patient information, clinical assessments, decision support, knowledge management, governance, and monitoring integrate into a coherent enterprise-wide data model.

---

# 20.12.2 Scope

The Enterprise ERD encompasses the following bounded contexts:

| Bounded Context           | Primary Entities                                   |
| ------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| Identity & Access         | User, Role, Permission                             |
| Patient Management        | Health Profile                                     |
| Nutrition Assessment      | Assessment, Observation, Dietary Log               |
| Clinical Decision Support | Diagnosis, Recommendation, Meal Plan, Explanation  |
| Monitoring                | Monitoring Record, Behaviour Goal                  |
| Knowledge Management      | Knowledge Module, Rule Metadata, Ontology Metadata |
| Governance                | Audit Log, Rule Version, Ontology Version          |
| Explainability            | Reasoning Trace, Activated Rule                    |

Each context owns its entities while interacting with other contexts through explicit foreign key relationships and repository interfaces.

---

# 20.12.3 Enterprise ERD

```text
                                        USER
                                         │
                                  1──────┴──────1
                                         │
                                 HEALTH_PROFILE
                                         │
                                  1──────┴──────N
                                         │
                              NUTRITION_ASSESSMENT
                                         │
                 ┌───────────────────────┼────────────────────────┐
                 │                       │                        │
                 ▼                       ▼                        ▼
        CLINICAL_OBSERVATION      DIETARY_LOG            LAB_RESULT
                 │
                 ▼
          NUTRITION_DIAGNOSIS
                 │
                 ▼
          RECOMMENDATION
                 │
      ┌──────────┼────────────────┬───────────────┐
      │          │                │               │
          ▼                ▼                          ▼                        ▼
 MEAL_PLAN  EXPLANATION_CHAIN  REASONING_TRACE  INTERVENTION
                               │
                                                   ▼
                        ACTIVATED_RULE
                               │
                                                   ▼
                        RULE_METADATA
                               │
                                                   ▼
                     KNOWLEDGE_MODULE
                               │
               ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
                         ▼                        ▼                          ▼
        GUIDELINE_VERSION  ONTOLOGY_CONCEPTS FOOD_DATABASE
                               │
                                                   ▼
                       KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH
                               │
                                                   ▼
                      MONITORING_RECORD
                               │
                                                   ▼
                      BEHAVIOURAL_GOAL

USER ─────────────── AUDIT_LOG
RULE_METADATA ────── RULE_VERSION
ONTOLOGY ─────────── ONTOLOGY_VERSION
```

This diagram illustrates the major enterprise entities and the primary flow of information from patient assessment through symbolic reasoning to recommendation generation and longitudinal monitoring.

---

# 20.12.4 Enterprise Entity Catalogue

The following catalogue summarizes the primary responsibilities of each enterprise entity.

| Entity                   | Responsibility                                                |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **User**                 | Authentication, authorization, and identity management        |
| **Health Profile**       | Longitudinal demographic and baseline health information      |
| **Nutrition Assessment** | Aggregate root for assessment episodes                        |
| **Clinical Observation** | Anthropometric, clinical, dietary, and lifestyle measurements |
| **Dietary Log**          | Detailed dietary intake records                               |
| **Lab Result**           | Laboratory observations relevant to nutrition                 |
| **Nutrition Diagnosis**  | Structured nutrition problem statements                       |
| **Recommendation**       | Personalized nutrition recommendations                        |
| **Meal Plan**            | Structured dietary interventions                              |
| **Intervention**         | Clinical actions recommended by the system                    |
| **Explanation**          | Human-readable explanation of recommendations                 |
| **Reasoning Trace**      | Complete symbolic inference execution history                 |
| **Activated Rule**       | Rules fired during reasoning                                  |
| **Knowledge Module**     | Logical grouping of nutrition knowledge                       |
| **Rule Metadata**        | Metadata describing executable Prolog rule modules            |
| **Guideline Version**    | Versioned Ministry of Health clinical guidance                |
| **Ontology Metadata**    | Nutrition ontology lifecycle information                      |
| **Knowledge Graph**      | Semantic relationships among nutrition concepts               |
| **Monitoring Record**    | Follow-up assessments and outcome tracking                    |
| **Behavioural Goal**     | Patient-specific nutrition and lifestyle objectives           |
| **Audit Log**            | Security, governance, and operational audit events            |

---

# 20.12.5 Relationship Analysis

The Enterprise ERD is governed by the following principal relationships.

## Identity Relationships

* One User owns one Health Profile.
* One User may complete many Nutrition Assessments.
* One User may possess multiple Monitoring Records.
* One User may generate multiple Audit Log entries.

---

## Clinical Relationships

* One Nutrition Assessment may produce multiple Nutrition Diagnoses.
* One Diagnosis may generate multiple Recommendations.
* One Recommendation may contain multiple Meal Plans and Interventions.
* Every Recommendation possesses exactly one Explanation and one Reasoning Trace.

---

## Knowledge Relationships

* Multiple Activated Rules may reference a single Rule Metadata record.
* Multiple Rule Metadata records may belong to one Knowledge Module.
* Every Knowledge Module references one Guideline Version.
* Every Guideline Version is associated with one Ontology Metadata record.
* Ontology Metadata links semantic concepts represented within the Knowledge Graph.

---

## Monitoring Relationships

* One User may have many Monitoring Records.
* One Monitoring Record may contain multiple Behavioural Goals.
* Monitoring Records reference Recommendations to evaluate adherence and outcomes over time.

---

# 20.12.6 Aggregate Boundaries

The Enterprise ERD preserves the aggregate boundaries established in Chapter 19.

| Aggregate Root       | Owned Entities                                        |
| -------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- |
| User                 | Health Profile                                        |
| Nutrition Assessment | Clinical Observation, Dietary Log, Lab Result         |
| Recommendation       | Meal Plan, Intervention, Explanation, Reasoning Trace |
| Monitoring Record    | Behavioural Goal                                      |
| Knowledge Module     | Rule Metadata                                         |
| Governance           | Audit Log, Rule Version, Ontology Version             |

Each aggregate is designed to enforce domain invariants and transactional consistency while minimizing coupling between bounded contexts.

---

# 20.12.7 Traceability Across the Enterprise Model

One of the defining characteristics of the Enterprise ERD is its support for end-to-end traceability.

```text
Patient
    │
    ▼
Health Profile
    │
    ▼
Nutrition Assessment
    │
    ▼
Nutrition Diagnosis
    │
    ▼
Recommendation
    │
    ▼
Reasoning Trace
    │
    ▼
Activated Rule
    │
    ▼
Knowledge Module
    │
    ▼
Guideline Version
    │
    ▼
Ontology Metadata
```

This chain ensures that every recommendation can be traced from the originating patient observations to the specific knowledge assets that justified the recommendation.

---

# 20.12.8 Architectural Quality Considerations

The Enterprise ERD contributes directly to the quality attributes identified throughout the dissertation.

| Quality Attribute    | Architectural Support                                               |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Maintainability**  | Modular bounded contexts reduce coupling.                           |
| **Scalability**      | Domain-oriented entities support incremental expansion.             |
| **Clinical Safety**  | Assessment and recommendation traceability preserve accountability. |
| **Explainability**   | Explicit reasoning entities enable transparent recommendations.     |
| **Security**         | Identity and audit entities support RBAC and accountability.        |
| **Interoperability** | Semantic entities facilitate future FHIR and ontology integration.  |
| **Governance**       | Version-controlled knowledge entities support reproducibility.      |

---

# 20.12.9 Architectural Rationale

The Enterprise ERD intentionally models **business capabilities** rather than merely database tables. By organizing persistent entities around clinical workflows and bounded contexts, the architecture aligns the data model with the Nutrition Care Process and the Domain-Driven Design established earlier in the dissertation. This approach minimizes accidental coupling, improves maintainability, and provides a stable foundation for future extensions, including additional clinical domains, interoperability standards, and advanced neuro-symbolic reasoning capabilities.

---

# 20.12.10 Transition to the Clinical Assessment ERD

The Enterprise ERD provides a holistic view of the persistence architecture but intentionally omits detailed attribute-level relationships to maintain readability. The following section focuses on the **Clinical Assessment Entity–Relationship Diagram**, expanding the Nutrition Assessment aggregate into its constituent entities and relationships. This detailed model will define the persistent representation of patient observations, dietary assessments, laboratory findings, and clinical measurements that initiate the Nutrition Care Process and serve as the primary inputs to the symbolic reasoning engine.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.13 Clinical Assessment Entity–Relationship Architecture

---

## 20.13.1 Introduction

The Clinical Assessment Entity–Relationship Architecture defines the persistent representation of the Nutrition Assessment aggregate, which constitutes the primary source of clinical information for NutriLogic's Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System (CNDSS). This aggregate captures the multidimensional observations required by the Nutrition Care Process (NCP), including anthropometric measurements, biochemical indicators, clinical observations, dietary intake, lifestyle factors, and nutrition-focused physical findings.

Within the overall enterprise architecture, the Clinical Assessment aggregate serves as the gateway between patient-specific clinical data and the neuro-symbolic reasoning engine. Every recommendation generated by the system originates from one or more assessment records. Consequently, the integrity, completeness, and traceability of this aggregate are fundamental to ensuring clinically valid, explainable, and reproducible recommendations.

Unlike conventional health information systems that often store assessments as loosely structured forms, NutriLogic models the assessment as a structured aggregate with clearly defined subdomains. This design preserves transactional consistency while supporting extensibility and interoperability.

---

# 20.13.2 Objectives

The Clinical Assessment ERD has the following objectives:

1. Represent the Nutrition Assessment as the principal aggregate root of the Nutrition Care Process.
2. Capture comprehensive clinical, dietary, behavioural, anthropometric, and laboratory observations.
3. Preserve longitudinal patient histories through immutable assessment episodes.
4. Support symbolic reasoning by structuring observations into machine-interpretable facts.
5. Enable semantic linkage between assessment data and ontology concepts.
6. Facilitate explainability through traceable assessment provenance.
7. Provide a normalized relational model suitable for PostgreSQL implementation.

---

# 20.13.3 Scope

This ERD encompasses the following bounded contexts:

| Domain                | Included Entities                                  |
| --------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| Patient Management    | User, Health Profile                               |
| Nutrition Assessment  | Nutrition Assessment                               |
| Anthropometry         | Height, Weight, BMI, MUAC, Waist Circumference     |
| Clinical Assessment   | Medical Conditions, Symptoms, Clinical Observations|
| Dietary Assessment    | Dietary Intake, Meal Frequency, Food Consumption   |
| Lifestyle Assessment  | Physical Activity, Smoking, Alcohol, Sleep         |
| Laboratory Assessment | Laboratory Observations                            |
| Metadata              | Assessment Status, Assessment Source               |

The output of this aggregate serves as the primary input to the symbolic reasoning engine described in Chapters 10–15.

---

# 20.13.4 Clinical Assessment ERD

```text
                                  USER
                                   │
                          1────────┴────────1
                                   │
                           HEALTH_PROFILE
                                   │
                          1────────┴────────N
                                   │
                          NUTRITION_ASSESSMENT
                                   │
         ┌─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┐
         │                         │                        │
         ▼                         ▼                        ▼
    OBSERVATION              ASSESSMENT_FACT       ONTOLOGY_REFERENCE
         │                         │                        │
  ┌──────┼──────┐                  ▼                        │
  │      │      │          PROLOG_FACT_EXPORT               │
  ▼      ▼      ▼                                           │
ANTHRO CLINICAL DIETARY (etc.)                              │
  │      │      │                                           │
  └──────┴──────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

This architecture models the assessment as a cohesive aggregate while preserving the distinct semantic roles of its constituent observations.

---

# 20.13.5 Aggregate Root

The aggregate root is **NutritionAssessment**, representing a single clinical encounter or assessment episode.

### Core Attributes

| Attribute         | Description                          |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------------ |
| Assessment ID     | Unique identifier                    |
| Patient ID        | Reference to Health Profile          |
| Assessment Date   | Date and time of assessment          |
| Assessor          | Clinician or self-assessment         |
| Assessment Type   | Initial, Follow-up, Screening        |
| Status            | Draft, Completed, Validated          |
| Source            | Web, Mobile, Community Health Worker |
| Guideline Version | Active guideline at assessment time  |

Each NutritionAssessment defines the transactional boundary for all associated observations.

---

# 20.13.6 Anthropometric Assessment

Anthropometric data describe body composition and growth indicators relevant to nutritional assessment.

### Entity: Anthropometry

Representative attributes include:

| Attribute           | Description                 |
| ------------------- | --------------------------- |
| Height              | cm                          |
| Weight              | kg                          |
| BMI                 | Calculated value            |
| MUAC                | Mid-upper arm circumference |
| Waist Circumference | cm                          |
| Hip Circumference   | cm                          |
| Body Fat Percentage | Optional                    |
| Growth Z-score      | Paediatric assessments      |

Derived values (e.g., BMI) should be calculated within the Domain Layer and persisted only when necessary for auditing or reporting.

---

# 20.13.7 Clinical Observations

Clinical observations capture the raw, uninterpreted clinical inputs required for nutritional assessment.

Examples include:

* pre-existing diagnosed conditions,
* patient-reported symptoms,
* presence of oedema,
* reported appetite changes,
* gastrointestinal symptoms,
* medication history,
* pregnancy status,
* comorbidities.

Each observation references controlled terminology and, where appropriate, ontology concepts before being interpreted by the inference engine.

---

# 20.13.8 Dietary Assessment

Dietary assessment captures habitual food intake and nutritional behaviours.

Representative entities include:

| Entity          | Description                    |
| --------------- | ------------------------------ |
| Dietary Log     | Daily intake records           |
| Meal Pattern    | Meal frequency and timing      |
| Food Intake     | Foods consumed                 |
| Portion Record  | Portion size estimation        |
| Nutrient Intake | Estimated nutrient consumption |

Dietary records are normalized to support longitudinal analysis and food composition integration.

---

# 20.13.9 Lifestyle Assessment

Lifestyle observations provide contextual information influencing nutritional status.

Typical attributes include:

* physical activity level,
* occupation,
* smoking status,
* alcohol consumption,
* sleep duration,
* stress indicators,
* household food security (if collected),
* socioeconomic context.

These variables enrich personalization while remaining distinct from clinical diagnoses.

---

# 20.13.10 Laboratory Assessment

Laboratory observations support evidence-based nutritional diagnosis.

Representative attributes include:

| Attribute      | Description             |
| -------------- | ----------------------- |
| Haemoglobin    | g/dL                    |
| Blood Glucose  | mmol/L                  |
| Serum Ferritin | µg/L                    |
| Lipid Profile  | Composite result        |
| Vitamin Levels | Structured measurements |
| Electrolytes   | Clinical chemistry      |

Each laboratory result records:

* measurement value,
* unit,
* reference range,
* observation date,
* performing laboratory (optional),
* provenance metadata.

---

# 20.13.11 Assessment Facts

The **AssessmentFact** entity is a distinctive and critical feature of the NutriLogic architecture. 

It serves as the explicit bridge between the **Clinical World** (raw inputs and measurements) and the **Symbolic World** (computable logic). Rather than coupling SWI-Prolog reasoning directly to relational observation tables, clinically relevant observations are interpreted and transformed into normalized `AssessmentFact` entities *before* Prolog execution begins.

Examples:

```prolog
age_group(adult).
bmi(overweight).
condition(hypertension).
physical_activity(low).
fruit_intake(inadequate).
```

These facts provide the abstraction layer consumed by the SWI-Prolog inference engine.

By persisting generated facts (or regenerating them deterministically from the assessment), the system supports reproducibility, auditing, and debugging of inference outcomes.

---

# 20.13.12 Ontology References

Every clinically significant observation may reference one or more ontology concepts.

Example:

```text
BMI Category
      │
      ▼
Nutrition Ontology Concept
      │
      ▼
Knowledge Graph Node
```

This mapping enables semantic interoperability and consistent interpretation across reasoning modules.

---

# 20.13.13 Relationship Analysis

The principal relationships are summarized below.

| Parent               | Child                | Cardinality |
| -------------------- | -------------------- | ----------- |
| User                 | Health Profile       | 1 : 1       |
| Health Profile       | Nutrition Assessment | 1 : N       |
| Nutrition Assessment | Observation          | 1 : N       |
| Observation          | Anthropometric Obs.  | 1 : 1       |
| Observation          | Clinical Obs.        | 1 : 1       |
| Observation          | Dietary Obs.         | 1 : 1       |
| Observation          | Lifestyle Obs.       | 1 : 1       |
| Observation          | Laboratory Obs.      | 1 : 1       |
| Nutrition Assessment | Assessment Fact      | 1 : N       |
| Assessment Fact      | Ontology Reference   | N : 1       |

---

# 20.13.14 Integrity Constraints

The Clinical Assessment model enforces:

* Mandatory association between an assessment and a patient profile.
* Immutable assessment episodes after clinical validation.
* Referential integrity across all child entities.
* Controlled vocabularies for categorical observations.
* Unit validation for quantitative measurements.
* Provenance recording for all externally sourced observations.

These constraints ensure data quality before information is translated into symbolic facts.

---

# 20.13.15 Architectural Significance

The Clinical Assessment ERD operationalizes the **Assessment** phase of the Nutrition Care Process and establishes the canonical clinical dataset from which all downstream reasoning is derived. By separating raw observations from symbolic facts, the architecture preserves both the fidelity of clinical data and the flexibility of the reasoning engine. It also enables future support for interoperability standards such as HL7 FHIR, where many of these entities naturally correspond to resources such as *Patient*, *Observation*, *Condition*, *QuestionnaireResponse*, and *NutritionIntake*.

---

# 20.13.16 Transition to the Clinical Decision Support ERD

The Clinical Assessment ERD defines how patient observations are captured, validated, and structured for reasoning. The next section, **20.14 Clinical Decision Support Entity–Relationship Architecture**, models how these assessment facts are transformed into nutrition diagnoses, personalized recommendations, meal plans, explanation chains, and reasoning traces, completing the transition from data acquisition to evidence-based decision support.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.14 Clinical Decision Support and Explainability Entity–Relationship Architecture

---

## 20.14.1 Introduction

The Clinical Decision Support and Explainability Entity–Relationship Architecture defines the persistent representation of the decision-making processes within NutriLogic. Building upon the Clinical Assessment Architecture presented in Section 20.13, this model captures how validated patient observations are transformed into nutrition diagnoses, personalized recommendations, intervention plans, and transparent explanations through the neuro-symbolic reasoning engine.

Unlike conventional recommendation systems that store only final outputs, NutriLogic persists the complete reasoning lifecycle. This includes symbolic facts, activated inference rules, ontology references, reasoning traces, explanation chains, and recommendation provenance. Such an approach supports explainability, reproducibility, governance, and clinical auditability, ensuring that every recommendation can be justified using explicit evidence rather than opaque computational processes.

The architecture therefore operationalizes the **Diagnosis** and **Intervention** phases of the Nutrition Care Process (NCP) while providing the foundation for monitoring and continuous evaluation.

---

# 20.14.2 Objectives

The Clinical Decision Support and Explainability Architecture is designed to:

1. Persist nutrition diagnoses generated by the symbolic reasoning engine.
2. Represent personalized recommendations and intervention plans as first-class domain entities.
3. Capture reasoning traces and activated symbolic rules for explainability.
4. Maintain explicit links between recommendations, ontology concepts, and guideline versions.
5. Preserve reproducibility by recording the knowledge assets and rule versions used during inference.
6. Support future integration of probabilistic or machine learning components while maintaining symbolic transparency.
7. Enable longitudinal analysis of recommendations and patient outcomes.

---

# 20.14.3 Scope

This architecture encompasses the following domains:

| Domain                    | Included Entities                                     |
| ------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- |
| Clinical Assessment       | Assessment Fact                                       |
| Symbolic Reasoning        | Inference Session, Activated Rule                     |
| Clinical Decision Support | Nutrition Diagnosis, Recommendation, Intervention     |
| Explainability            | Explanation Chain, Evidence Item, Reasoning Trace     |
| Knowledge Management      | Knowledge Module, Guideline Version, Ontology Concept |
| Monitoring                | Follow-up Reference                                   |

---

# 20.14.4 Clinical Decision Support ERD

```text
                           NUTRITION_ASSESSMENT
                                      │
                                      ▼
                              ASSESSMENT_FACT
                                      │
                                      ▼
                             INFERENCE_SESSION
                                      │
                ┌─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┐
                │                     │                      │
                ▼                     ▼                      ▼
        ACTIVATED_RULE      NUTRITION_DIAGNOSIS     REASONING_TRACE
                │                     │                      │
                └──────────────┬──────┴──────────────┐
                               ▼                     ▼
                       RECOMMENDATION        EXPLANATION_CHAIN
                               │                     │
                  ┌────────────┼─────────────┐       │
                  │            │             │       │
                  ▼            ▼             ▼       ▼
             INTERVENTION  MEAL_PLAN   FOLLOW_UP  EVIDENCE_ITEM
                               │                     │
                               └─────────────┬───────┘
                                             ▼
                                     ONTOLOGY_CONCEPTS
                                             │
                                             ▼
                                     KNOWLEDGE_MODULE
                                             │
                                             ▼
                                     GUIDELINE_VERSION
```

This model illustrates the complete transformation of structured assessment information into explainable clinical recommendations.

---

# 20.14.5 Inference Session

The **InferenceSession** entity represents a single execution of the symbolic reasoning engine.

Each session records:

| Attribute                | Description                 |
| ------------------------ | --------------------------- |
| Session ID               | Unique execution identifier |
| Assessment ID            | Source assessment           |
| Knowledge Module Version | Rule set used               |
| Ontology Version         | Semantic model used         |
| Guideline Version        | Clinical guideline version  |
| Execution Time           | Timestamp                   |
| Duration                 | Reasoning duration          |
| Status                   | Successful, Partial, Failed |

The Inference Session acts as the parent entity for all generated reasoning artefacts.

---

# 20.14.6 Nutrition Diagnosis

Nutrition diagnoses represent the formal clinical interpretation of assessment findings.

Representative attributes include:

| Attribute         | Description                      |
| ----------------- | -------------------------------- |
| Diagnosis Code    | Internal identifier              |
| Problem Statement | Standardized nutrition diagnosis |
| Severity          | Mild, Moderate, Severe           |
| Priority          | Clinical urgency                 |
| Evidence Summary  | Supporting observations          |
| Diagnosis Date    | Timestamp                        |

Each diagnosis references the originating inference session and may contribute to multiple recommendations.

---

# 20.14.7 Recommendations

Recommendations constitute the primary clinical outputs delivered to clinicians and patients.

Representative attributes include:

| Attribute             | Description                            |
| --------------------- | -------------------------------------- |
| Recommendation Type   | Dietary, Behavioural, Referral         |
| Recommendation Text   | Human-readable recommendation          |
| Clinical Priority     | Urgency                                |
| Personalization Level | General, Tailored, Highly Personalized |
| Effective Date        | Recommendation validity                |
| Review Date           | Planned reassessment                   |

Recommendations maintain references to:

* originating diagnosis,
* activated rules,
* evidence chain,
* explanation,
* knowledge module,
* ontology concepts.

---

# 20.14.8 Intervention Plans

Recommendations may consist of one or more intervention plans.

Examples include:

* therapeutic diets,
* food substitutions,
* meal schedules,
* nutrient supplementation,
* behaviour change interventions,
* referrals to nutritionists or clinicians.

Separating interventions from recommendations allows a single recommendation to generate multiple coordinated actions.

---

# 20.14.9 Meal Plans

Meal plans are structured nutritional prescriptions associated with a recommendation.

Typical attributes include:

* meal schedule,
* food items,
* portion sizes,
* nutrient targets,
* cultural adaptations,
* affordability considerations,
* regional food availability.

This design supports the project's objective of culturally appropriate and context-aware nutrition guidance for Kenya.

---

# 20.14.10 Explainability Model

Explainability is represented explicitly through three complementary entities.

### Reasoning Trace

Captures the complete execution path of symbolic inference.

### Explanation Chain

Transforms symbolic reasoning into clinician- and patient-readable narratives.

### Evidence Item

Represents individual pieces of evidence supporting a recommendation.

Examples include:

* elevated BMI,
* low fruit intake,
* hypertension diagnosis,
* low physical activity,
* anaemia indicators.

Together, these entities enable transparent reconstruction of every recommendation.

---

# 20.14.11 Activated Rules

Each inference session records the symbolic rules that contributed to the final recommendation.

Representative attributes include:

| Attribute             | Description               |
| --------------------- | ------------------------- |
| Rule Identifier       | Prolog rule ID            |
| Rule Version          | Version identifier        |
| Rule Outcome          | Satisfied / Not satisfied |
| Confidence Annotation | Optional                  |
| Execution Order       | Rule firing sequence      |

Persisting activated rules facilitates governance, debugging, and validation of the reasoning engine.

---

# 20.14.12 Ontology References

Every recommendation and explanation may reference one or more ontology concepts.

Example:

```text
Recommendation
       │
       ▼
Ontology Concept
       │
       ▼
Knowledge Graph Node
       │
       ▼
Clinical Guideline
```

This architecture links clinical recommendations to semantic knowledge assets while avoiding duplication.

---

# 20.14.13 Relationship Analysis

| Parent               | Child               | Cardinality |
| -------------------- | ------------------- | ----------- |
| Nutrition Assessment | Assessment Fact     | 1 : N       |
| Assessment Fact      | Inference Session   | N : 1       |
| Inference Session    | Nutrition Diagnosis | 1 : N       |
| Inference Session    | Activated Rule      | 1 : N       |
| Inference Session    | Reasoning Trace     | 1 : 1       |
| Nutrition Diagnosis  | Recommendation      | 1 : N       |
| Recommendation       | Intervention        | 1 : N       |
| Recommendation       | Meal Plan           | 1 : N       |
| Recommendation       | Explanation Chain   | 1 : 1       |
| Explanation Chain    | Evidence Item       | 1 : N       |
| Recommendation       | Ontology Concept    | N : N       |
| Activated Rule       | Knowledge Module    | N : 1       |
| Knowledge Module     | Guideline Version   | N : 1       |

---

# 20.14.14 Traceability Chain

A defining characteristic of this architecture is complete traceability from assessment to recommendation.

```text
Patient
    │
Assessment
    │
Assessment Facts
    │
Inference Session
    │
Activated Rules
    │
Nutrition Diagnosis
    │
Recommendation
    │
Explanation Chain
    │
Evidence Items
    │
Knowledge Module
    │
Guideline Version
```

This chain ensures that every recommendation can be reconstructed and audited using the original patient observations and the exact version of the knowledge base employed during reasoning.

---

# 20.14.15 Architectural Significance

The Clinical Decision Support and Explainability Architecture transforms NutriLogic from a recommendation engine into an accountable clinical decision support system. By modelling inference sessions, reasoning traces, activated rules, and explanation chains as persistent entities, the system ensures that decision-making is transparent, reproducible, and clinically defensible. This architecture also positions NutriLogic for future hybrid reasoning, where symbolic logic can be complemented by machine learning models without sacrificing explainability or governance.

---

# 20.14.16 Transition to the Knowledge Management ERD

Having modelled the generation of diagnoses, recommendations, and explanations, the next section focuses on the persistent management of the knowledge resources that underpin these decisions. **Section 20.15 – Knowledge Management Entity–Relationship Architecture** will describe the entities responsible for organizing ontology metadata, symbolic rule modules, guideline versions, food composition data, and knowledge governance, providing the foundation for maintaining, evolving, and validating the neuro-symbolic knowledge base independently of patient data.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.15 Knowledge Engineering and Governance Entity–Relationship Architecture

---

## 20.15.1 Introduction

The effectiveness, safety, and explainability of a Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System depend not only on the quality of patient data but also on the integrity of the knowledge from which clinical recommendations are derived. Unlike conventional information systems where business rules are embedded within application code, NutriLogic externalizes clinical expertise into modular symbolic knowledge assets governed through explicit knowledge engineering processes.

This section defines the persistent architecture responsible for managing clinical knowledge independently of patient records. It models the lifecycle of ontology concepts, symbolic rule modules, food composition knowledge, Ministry of Health nutrition guidelines, semantic relationships, and governance artefacts that collectively constitute the neuro-symbolic knowledge base.

The architecture establishes the separation between operational clinical data and reusable knowledge assets, thereby enabling continuous evolution of the reasoning engine without compromising patient information or application logic.

---

# 20.15.2 Objectives

The Knowledge Engineering and Governance Architecture aims to:

1. Represent reusable clinical knowledge independently of patient records.
2. Support modular organization of symbolic rule bases.
3. Maintain explicit version control for knowledge assets.
4. Preserve provenance of all clinical knowledge.
5. Enable peer review and approval workflows.
6. Support ontology evolution while maintaining semantic consistency.
7. Facilitate explainable reasoning through explicit traceability to knowledge sources.
8. Enable future integration of external biomedical terminologies and knowledge repositories.

---

# 20.15.3 Scope

The architecture encompasses the following domains.

| Domain              | Included Entities                             |
| ------------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| Symbolic Reasoning  | Rule Module, Rule Metadata                    |
| Ontology            | Ontology Concept, Ontology Version            |
| Knowledge Graph     | Semantic Node, Semantic Relationship          |
| Clinical Guidelines | Guideline, Guideline Version                  |
| Food Knowledge      | Food Composition Dataset, Nutrient Definition |
| Governance          | Knowledge Review, Approval, Publication       |
| Provenance          | Knowledge Source, Citation                    |

---

# 20.15.4 Knowledge Engineering ERD

```text
                      KNOWLEDGE_SOURCE
                              │
                              ▼
                        GUIDELINE
                              │
                              ▼
                    GUIDELINE_VERSION
                              │
         ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐
         ▼                    ▼                    ▼
 KNOWLEDGE_MODULE      ONTOLOGY_VERSION   FOOD_DATASET
         │                    │                    │
         ▼                    ▼                    ▼
 RULE_METADATA       ONTOLOGY_CONCEPTS     NUTRIENT_REFERENCE
         │                    │                    │
         └──────────────┬─────┴────────────┬───────┘
                        ▼                  ▼
                KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH     SEMANTIC_RELATIONSHIP
                        │
                        ▼
                  PROLOG_MODULE
                        │
                        ▼
                 RULE_PUBLICATION
                        │
         ┌──────────────┼───────────────┐
         ▼              ▼               ▼
 KNOWLEDGE_REVIEW   APPROVAL      CHANGE_LOG
```

This model separates executable knowledge, semantic resources, governance metadata, and provenance while preserving explicit traceability among them.

---

# 20.15.5 Knowledge Modules

A **Knowledge Module** is the principal organizational unit of reusable clinical expertise.

Each module groups a coherent set of symbolic rules addressing a particular clinical topic.

Examples include:

* Adult Nutrition Assessment
* Child Growth Assessment
* Hypertension Nutrition Therapy
* Diabetes Nutrition Therapy
* Maternal Nutrition
* Micronutrient Deficiency
* Obesity Management
* Healthy Diet Guidance

Representative attributes:

| Attribute        | Description                 |
| ---------------- | --------------------------- |
| Module ID        | Unique identifier           |
| Module Name      | Descriptive title           |
| Clinical Domain  | Area of practice            |
| Status           | Draft, Approved, Deprecated |
| Current Version  | Active version              |
| Responsible Team | Knowledge owner             |

Modules enable independent maintenance and deployment of clinical knowledge.

---

# 20.15.6 Rule Metadata

Executable Prolog rules remain external source files; however, their descriptive metadata are persisted to support governance and traceability.

Representative attributes include:

| Attribute         | Description                |
| ----------------- | -------------------------- |
| Rule Identifier   | Unique symbolic rule ID    |
| Rule Name         | Human-readable description |
| Module ID         | Parent knowledge module    |
| Version           | Rule version               |
| Clinical Priority | Execution priority         |
| Effective Date    | Activation date            |
| Retirement Date   | Deprecation date           |
| Validation Status | Review outcome             |

The rule metadata repository enables auditing without storing executable logic within relational tables.

---

# 20.15.7 Ontology Management

The ontology subsystem manages semantic concepts independently of operational data.

Core entities include:

| Entity               | Purpose                                           |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- |
| Ontology Version     | Versioned ontology release                        |
| Ontology Concept     | Clinical concept                                  |
| Concept Mapping      | Links to external vocabularies (future extension) |
| Concept Relationship | Hierarchical and associative relationships        |

Each ontology concept is identified by a stable identifier referenced throughout the system.

---

# 20.15.8 Knowledge Graph

The knowledge graph represents semantic relationships among nutrition concepts.

Representative node categories include:

* food,
* nutrient,
* disease,
* symptom,
* dietary pattern,
* intervention,
* lifestyle factor,
* demographic factor.

Representative relationships include:

* *contains nutrient*
* *recommended for*
* *contraindicated for*
* *associated with*
* *part of guideline*
* *causes*
* *improves*
* *requires monitoring*

The graph supports semantic navigation and enriches symbolic reasoning without duplicating operational data.

---

# 20.15.9 Clinical Guideline Management

Clinical recommendations must be traceable to authoritative guidance.

The guideline subsystem therefore records:

| Entity               | Purpose              |
| -------------------- | -------------------- |
| Guideline            | Clinical publication |
| Guideline Version    | Released edition     |
| Issuing Organization | Publishing authority |
| Publication Date     | Official publication |
| Effective Period     | Validity interval    |

For NutriLogic, the initial authoritative source is the **Kenya Ministry of Health Nutrient Profile Model (2025)**, while the architecture allows incorporation of additional evidence-based nutrition guidelines in future releases.

---

# 20.15.10 Food Composition Knowledge

Food composition knowledge is maintained separately from patient dietary records.

Representative entities include:

* Food Item
* Food Category
* Nutrient Reference
* Portion Definition
* Food Composition Dataset
* Regional Food Availability

This separation supports dataset updates without affecting historical patient records.

---

# 20.15.11 Knowledge Governance

Knowledge governance manages the lifecycle of clinical knowledge.

```text
Knowledge Proposal
        │
        ▼
Expert Review
        │
        ▼
Clinical Validation
        │
        ▼
Approval
        │
        ▼
Publication
        │
        ▼
Deployment
        │
        ▼
Monitoring
        │
        ▼
Revision
```

Each stage is recorded to ensure accountability and reproducibility.

---

# 20.15.12 Provenance Architecture

Every knowledge asset records its origin.

Representative provenance attributes include:

* originating publication,
* author,
* reviewer,
* publication date,
* approval committee,
* evidence level,
* citation,
* source URL or document reference,
* change rationale.

This provenance enables clinicians and researchers to verify the authority of every knowledge asset used during reasoning.

---

# 20.15.13 Relationship Analysis

| Parent            | Child                 | Cardinality |
| ----------------- | --------------------- | ----------- |
| Guideline         | Guideline Version     | 1 : N       |
| Guideline Version | Knowledge Module      | 1 : N       |
| Knowledge Module  | Rule Metadata         | 1 : N       |
| Knowledge Module  | Ontology Version      | N : 1       |
| Ontology Version  | Ontology Concept      | 1 : N       |
| Ontology Concept  | Semantic Relationship | 1 : N       |
| Knowledge Module  | Food Dataset          | N : N       |
| Rule Metadata     | Rule Publication      | 1 : N       |
| Rule Publication  | Knowledge Review      | 1 : N       |
| Knowledge Review  | Approval              | 1 : 1       |
| Knowledge Module  | Change Log            | 1 : N       |

---

# 20.15.14 Architectural Significance

This architecture elevates knowledge from a collection of executable rules to a governed organizational asset. By explicitly modelling modules, ontology versions, provenance, approvals, and publication workflows, NutriLogic ensures that every recommendation can be traced not only to patient observations but also to the authoritative clinical knowledge from which it was derived. This separation of concerns enables independent evolution of clinical knowledge while preserving the stability of the operational system.

---

# 20.15.15 Transition to Governance and Audit Architecture

The Knowledge Engineering and Governance ERD defines how reusable clinical knowledge is organized, versioned, and maintained. However, trustworthy clinical decision support also requires comprehensive oversight of operational activities, user actions, inference executions, and system changes. The next section, **20.16 Governance, Audit, and Compliance Entity–Relationship Architecture**, extends the persistence architecture to model audit trails, security events, decision provenance, approvals, and regulatory compliance, ensuring that NutriLogic remains accountable, transparent, and suitable for deployment in regulated healthcare environments.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.16 Governance, Audit, Compliance, and Decision Provenance Entity–Relationship Architecture

---

## 20.16.1 Introduction

Clinical decision support systems operate in environments where recommendations may influence patient care, clinical workflows, and public health outcomes. Consequently, every recommendation must be accountable, explainable, and traceable throughout its lifecycle. Governance extends beyond system administration to encompass the management of knowledge evolution, clinical validation, regulatory compliance, operational oversight, and decision accountability.

This section defines the persistent architecture responsible for recording governance metadata, audit events, compliance artefacts, security events, and decision provenance. Together, these entities ensure that NutriLogic maintains a complete record of **who performed an action, when it occurred, what knowledge was used, why a recommendation was generated, and how that recommendation can be reproduced**.

The architecture complements the Knowledge Engineering Architecture (Section 20.15) by focusing on operational accountability rather than knowledge creation.

---

# 20.16.2 Objectives

The Governance, Audit, Compliance, and Decision Provenance Architecture aims to:

1. Maintain complete audit trails for clinical and administrative actions.
2. Record the provenance of every recommendation produced by the symbolic reasoning engine.
3. Support clinical governance through approval and review workflows.
4. Enable compliance with applicable health information governance principles and regulatory requirements.
5. Preserve reproducibility by recording software, ontology, guideline, and rule versions.
6. Support security monitoring and incident investigation.
7. Provide a trustworthy foundation for explainable and accountable AI-assisted clinical decision support.

---

# 20.16.3 Scope

This architecture includes the following domains:

| Domain                 | Included Entities                                 |
| ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- |
| Audit                  | Audit Log, Audit Event                            |
| Governance             | Approval Record, Review Decision                  |
| Decision Provenance    | Provenance Record, Decision Context               |
| Security               | Authentication Event, Authorization Event         |
| Compliance             | Consent Record, Policy Version                    |
| Deployment             | System Version, Configuration Snapshot            |
| Knowledge Traceability | Rule Version, Guideline Version, Ontology Version |

---

# 20.16.4 Governance and Provenance ERD

```text
                              USER
                               │
               ┌───────────────┴───────────────┐
               ▼                               ▼
         AUTH_EVENT                     AUDIT_EVENT
               │                               │
               └───────────────┬───────────────┘
                               ▼
                         AUDIT_LOG
                               │
                               ▼
                     DECISION_PROVENANCE
                               │
        ┌──────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────┐
        ▼              ▼               ▼              ▼
 INFERENCE_SESSION RULE_VERSION GUIDELINE_VERSION ONTOLOGY_VERSION
        │
        ▼
 CONFIGURATION_SNAPSHOT
        │
        ▼
 SYSTEM_VERSION

RECOMMENDATION ───────────────► DECISION_PROVENANCE

KNOWLEDGE_MODULE ─────────────► DECISION_PROVENANCE

ASSESSMENT ───────────────────► DECISION_PROVENANCE

POLICY_VERSION ───────────────► COMPLIANCE_RECORD

CONSENT_RECORD ───────────────► USER
```

The ERD demonstrates that governance information spans both operational activities and clinical reasoning, providing a unified framework for accountability.

---

# 20.16.5 Audit Architecture

The audit subsystem records significant actions performed by users and system components.

Representative entities include:

| Entity               | Description                            |
| -------------------- | -------------------------------------- |
| Audit Log            | Immutable record of system activities  |
| Audit Event          | Individual user or system action       |
| Authentication Event | Login, logout, token refresh           |
| Authorization Event  | Permission checks and access decisions |

Representative audit attributes include:

* event identifier,
* user identifier,
* timestamp,
* affected entity,
* action performed,
* originating IP address (where appropriate),
* application component,
* outcome (success/failure).

Audit records are append-only and should not be modified after creation.

---

# 20.16.6 Decision Provenance

The **DecisionProvenance** entity is a central contribution of the NutriLogic architecture.

Rather than recording only the final recommendation, Decision Provenance captures the complete context in which that recommendation was generated.

Representative attributes include:

| Attribute                | Description              |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------ |
| Provenance ID            | Unique identifier        |
| Recommendation ID        | Linked recommendation    |
| Assessment ID            | Source assessment        |
| Inference Session ID     | Reasoning execution      |
| Knowledge Module Version | Knowledge used           |
| Rule Version             | Symbolic rule set        |
| Guideline Version        | Clinical guideline       |
| Ontology Version         | Semantic model           |
| Configuration Snapshot   | Runtime configuration    |
| Software Version         | Application release      |
| Timestamp                | Decision generation time |

This entity enables deterministic reconstruction of clinical decisions for auditing, research, and quality assurance.

---

# 20.16.7 Governance Workflow

Knowledge and system changes follow a controlled governance lifecycle.

```text
Knowledge Proposal
        │
        ▼
Clinical Review
        │
        ▼
Technical Validation
        │
        ▼
Approval
        │
        ▼
Publication
        │
        ▼
Deployment
        │
        ▼
Monitoring
        │
        ▼
Retirement
```

Each stage generates governance records that are persisted independently of patient data.

---

# 20.16.8 Compliance Architecture

Compliance information records the policies and permissions governing the use of patient information.

Representative entities include:

| Entity             | Purpose                            |
| ------------------ | ---------------------------------- |
| Consent Record     | Patient consent status             |
| Policy Version     | Applicable organizational policies |
| Compliance Record  | Compliance checks and outcomes     |
| Retention Schedule | Data retention requirements        |

The architecture supports policy evolution without altering historical clinical records.

> **Implementation Note:** The specific consent model, retention periods, and legal obligations should be configured to align with the applicable regulatory framework of the deployment environment (e.g., Kenya's Data Protection Act, 2019, institutional policies, and any future Ministry of Health digital health guidance). These implementation details belong in the governance configuration rather than being hard-coded into the domain model.

---

# 20.16.9 Security Events

Operational security events are persisted separately from clinical records.

Representative events include:

* authentication attempts,
* failed logins,
* permission violations,
* administrative actions,
* API access events,
* suspicious activity,
* configuration changes.

Separating operational security data from clinical information simplifies monitoring while preserving confidentiality.

---

# 20.16.10 Relationship Analysis

| Parent            | Child               | Cardinality |
| ----------------- | ------------------- | ----------- |
| User              | Audit Event         | 1 : N       |
| Audit Event       | Audit Log           | N : 1       |
| Recommendation    | Decision Provenance | 1 : 1       |
| Assessment        | Decision Provenance | 1 : N       |
| Inference Session | Decision Provenance | 1 : N       |
| Knowledge Module  | Decision Provenance | 1 : N       |
| Rule Version      | Decision Provenance | 1 : N       |
| Guideline Version | Decision Provenance | 1 : N       |
| Ontology Version  | Decision Provenance | 1 : N       |
| Policy Version    | Compliance Record   | 1 : N       |
| User              | Consent Record      | 1 : N       |

---

# 20.16.11 End-to-End Accountability Chain

A defining feature of the architecture is the preservation of complete accountability.

```text
Patient
    │
Health Profile
    │
Assessment
    │
Inference Session
    │
Activated Rules
    │
Recommendation
    │
Decision Provenance
    │
Knowledge Module
    │
Guideline Version
    │
Ontology Version
    │
Software Version
    │
Audit Log
```

This chain ensures that every recommendation can be traced not only to the originating patient observations but also to the exact knowledge, software configuration, and governance context in effect at the time of inference.

---

# 20.16.12 Architectural Significance

The Governance, Audit, Compliance, and Decision Provenance Architecture transforms NutriLogic into a platform capable of supporting trustworthy clinical decision support. By modelling governance artefacts as first-class entities rather than ancillary logs, the architecture strengthens transparency, accountability, reproducibility, and quality assurance. Decision provenance bridges the gap between explainability and operational governance, enabling independent verification of recommendations and supporting future certification, regulatory review, and scientific reproducibility.

---

# 20.16.13 Transition to the Physical Database Design

Sections 20.12 through 20.16 have established the enterprise information model, clinical assessment architecture, decision support architecture, knowledge engineering architecture, and governance architecture. Collectively, these define the logical persistence model of NutriLogic. The following sections transition from logical design to implementation by specifying the **physical PostgreSQL schema**, **Django ORM mappings**, **repository implementations**, **indexing strategies**, and **transaction management** required to realize this architecture in software.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.17 Physical PostgreSQL Schema Design

---

## 20.17.1 Introduction

The logical persistence architecture presented in Sections 20.12–20.16 defines the conceptual organization of clinical data, knowledge assets, governance artefacts, and reasoning metadata. This section translates those conceptual models into a physical PostgreSQL schema suitable for implementation within the NutriLogic platform.

The physical schema is designed to support the Domain-Driven Design (DDD) architecture introduced in Chapter 19. Rather than organizing tables according to generic technical concerns, the schema reflects the bounded contexts of the application. This alignment improves maintainability, simplifies repository implementation, and establishes a clear migration path should individual bounded contexts later be extracted into independent services.

Although the current implementation uses Django's default database schema, the architecture is intentionally documented using logical PostgreSQL namespaces to encourage modularity and future scalability.

---

# 20.17.2 Design Objectives

The physical schema has the following objectives:

1. Preserve the aggregate boundaries defined by the domain model.
2. Enforce referential integrity through primary and foreign key constraints.
3. Support efficient execution of clinical queries.
4. Enable versioning of knowledge assets without modifying historical patient data.
5. Facilitate longitudinal storage of assessments and monitoring records.
6. Support future horizontal scalability while remaining compatible with Django ORM.
7. Minimize redundancy through normalization while allowing carefully controlled denormalization where justified by performance.

---

# 20.17.3 Logical PostgreSQL Namespaces

The persistence layer is organized into logical schemas corresponding to the bounded contexts defined in Chapter 19.

| PostgreSQL Schema | Purpose                                                      |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **identity**      | Users, authentication, authorization, roles, permissions     |
| **clinical**      | Assessments, observations, diagnoses, interventions          |
| **reasoning**     | Inference sessions, activated rules, explanation traces      |
| **knowledge**     | Ontologies, rule metadata, food composition, knowledge graph |
| **governance**    | Audit logs, approvals, provenance, compliance                |
| **monitoring**    | Follow-up assessments, behavioural goals, outcomes           |
| **shared**        | Common reference tables, enumerations, configuration         |

Although Django initially stores all tables within the default `public` schema, these logical namespaces define the intended modular organization of the persistence layer.

---

# 20.17.4 Clinical Schema

The **clinical** schema contains patient-specific nutritional information.

Representative tables include:

| Table                  | Description                                  |
| ---------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| `health_profile`       | Patient demographic and baseline information |
| `nutrition_assessment` | Assessment aggregate root                    |
| `anthropometry`        | Anthropometric measurements                  |
| `clinical_finding`     | Clinical observations                        |
| `dietary_log`          | Dietary intake records                       |
| `laboratory_result`    | Laboratory measurements                      |
| `nutrition_diagnosis`  | Nutrition diagnoses                          |
| `recommendation`       | Clinical recommendations                     |
| `meal_plan`            | Structured dietary plans                     |
| `intervention`         | Recommended interventions                    |

---

# 20.17.5 Knowledge Schema

The **knowledge** schema stores reusable knowledge assets independent of patient records.

Representative tables include:

| Table                  | Description                    |
| ---------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| `knowledge_module`     | Modular knowledge organization |
| `rule_metadata`        | Prolog rule metadata           |
| `ontology_concept`     | Semantic concepts              |
| `ontology_version`     | Version-controlled ontology    |
| `knowledge_graph_node` | Graph nodes                    |
| `knowledge_graph_edge` | Semantic relationships         |
| `food_dataset`         | Food composition data          |
| `nutrient_reference`   | Nutrient definitions           |

Separating knowledge assets from clinical data simplifies independent maintenance and versioning.

---

# 20.17.6 Reasoning Schema

The **reasoning** schema stores symbolic inference artefacts.

Representative tables include:

| Table               | Description              |
| ------------------- | ------------------------ |
| `assessment_fact`   | Generated symbolic facts |
| `inference_session` | Reasoning execution      |
| `activated_rule`    | Fired Prolog rules       |
| `reasoning_trace`   | Inference trace          |
| `explanation_chain` | ExplanationSessions   |
| `evidence_item`     | Supporting evidence      |

This separation isolates transient reasoning artefacts from persistent clinical records while preserving complete traceability.

---

# 20.17.7 Governance Schema

Governance tables provide accountability and compliance.

Representative tables include:

| Table                    | Description                    |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------ |
| `audit_event`            | User and system events         |
| `audit_log`              | Immutable audit records        |
| `decision_provenance`    | Recommendation provenance      |
| `approval_record`        | Knowledge approvals            |
| `consent_record`         | Patient consent                |
| `policy_version`         | Applicable governance policies |
| `configuration_snapshot` | Runtime configuration          |
| `system_version`         | Software release metadata      |

---

# 20.17.8 Monitoring Schema

Monitoring tables support longitudinal nutrition care.

Representative tables include:

| Table                  | Description            |
| ---------------------- | ---------------------- |
| `monitoring_record`    | Follow-up encounters   |
| `behaviour_goal`       | Nutrition goals        |
| `progress_measurement` | Outcome metrics        |
| `adherence_record`     | Intervention adherence |

This schema enables long-term evaluation of nutritional interventions.

---

# 20.17.9 Primary Key Strategy

Each aggregate root uses a surrogate primary key implemented as a UUID.

| Entity           | Primary Key |
| ---------------- | ----------- |
| Health Profile   | UUID        |
| Assessment       | UUID        |
| Recommendation   | UUID        |
| Knowledge Module | UUID        |
| Rule Metadata    | UUID        |
| Audit Event      | UUID        |

UUIDs simplify synchronization, replication, and future distributed deployments.

---

# 20.17.10 Foreign Key Strategy

Relationships enforce aggregate consistency.

Representative examples include:

```text id="q4pjfx"
health_profile
      │
      ▼
nutrition_assessment
      │
      ▼
nutrition_diagnosis
      │
      ▼
recommendation
      │
      ▼
meal_plan
```

Cascading delete operations are intentionally limited to preserve historical clinical information and maintain auditability.

---

# 20.17.11 Constraints

The schema enforces integrity through database constraints.

Examples include:

* Primary Key constraints
* Foreign Key constraints
* Unique constraints
* Check constraints
* NOT NULL constraints
* Composite uniqueness where appropriate

Examples:

```sql
CHECK (height_cm > 0)

CHECK (weight_kg > 0)

CHECK (bmi >= 0)
```

Such constraints complement domain validation implemented within the application layer.

---

# 20.17.12 Indexing Strategy

Clinical systems require efficient retrieval of longitudinal patient information.

Representative indexes include:

| Table                  | Indexed Columns             |
| ---------------------- | --------------------------- |
| `nutrition_assessment` | Patient ID, Assessment Date |
| `recommendation`       | Assessment ID               |
| `activated_rule`       | Rule Identifier             |
| `audit_event`          | Timestamp                   |
| `knowledge_module`     | Module Name                 |

Composite indexes should be introduced for frequently executed query patterns identified through performance monitoring.

---

# 20.17.13 JSONB Usage

Although the schema is predominantly relational, PostgreSQL's `JSONB` type is used selectively for flexible, semi-structured data.

Suitable use cases include:

* dynamic questionnaire responses,
* configurable application settings,
* external metadata,
* imported wearable device payloads,
* integration metadata.

Clinical entities remain normalized to preserve integrity and facilitate querying.

---

# 20.17.14 Full-Text Search

PostgreSQL full-text indexing supports efficient searching of:

* recommendation narratives,
* clinical explanations,
* knowledge module documentation,
* ontology definitions.

This capability enhances clinician usability without requiring external search infrastructure.

---

# 20.17.15 Scalability Considerations

The schema supports future growth through:

* logical schema separation,
* UUID-based identifiers,
* append-only audit records,
* immutable assessment history,
* modular knowledge repositories,
* compatibility with table partitioning and read replicas.

These design choices reduce future migration effort as data volume and user numbers increase.

---

# 20.17.16 ORM Mapping Strategy

The physical schema maps directly to the Domain-Driven backend described in Chapter 19.

| Layer               | Responsibility           |
| ------------------- | ------------------------ |
| PostgreSQL          | Persistent storage       |
| Django ORM          | Persistence abstraction  |
| Repository          | Aggregate reconstruction |
| Domain Entity       | Business logic           |
| Application Service | Use-case orchestration   |

The Repository pattern ensures that the Domain Layer remains independent of the ORM implementation.

---

# 20.17.17 Architectural Significance

The physical schema realizes the conceptual persistence architecture while preserving the modularity, traceability, and maintainability established in earlier chapters. By structuring the database around bounded contexts rather than technical features, the design aligns persistence with the clinical workflows of the Nutrition Care Process and the principles of Domain-Driven Design. This provides a robust foundation for reliable data management, symbolic reasoning, and future system evolution.

---

# 20.17.18 Transition to ORM and Repository Implementation

Having defined the physical PostgreSQL schema, the next section specifies how this schema is implemented within the software architecture. **Section 20.18 – Django ORM and Repository Mapping Architecture** will describe the translation between relational tables, Django models, repositories, and pure domain entities, completing the bridge between the persistence layer and the application core.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.18 Domain Persistence Architecture: Django ORM, Repositories, and Aggregate Mapping

---

## 20.18.1 Introduction

The logical persistence architecture and physical PostgreSQL schema defined in the preceding sections establish how clinical, knowledge, reasoning, and governance information are stored within NutriLogic. However, direct interaction between business logic and relational tables would violate the Domain-Driven Design (DDD) principles established in Chapter 19 by coupling clinical workflows to the persistence technology.

This section defines the Domain Persistence Architecture, which bridges the relational database and the core domain model through Django ORM models, Repository implementations, and aggregate mapping. Rather than exposing database entities directly to application services, the persistence layer reconstructs rich domain entities from relational data and persists changes through well-defined repository interfaces.

This architecture ensures that the core clinical logic remains independent of Django, PostgreSQL, and any future persistence technology.

---

# 20.18.2 Objectives

The Domain Persistence Architecture is designed to:

1. Maintain persistence ignorance within the Domain Layer.
2. Separate relational models from domain entities.
3. Implement repositories as the sole gateway to persistent storage.
4. Preserve aggregate consistency during transactional operations.
5. Support dependency inversion between the application and infrastructure layers.
6. Enable unit testing of business logic without requiring a database.
7. Facilitate future migration to alternative persistence technologies.

---

# 20.18.3 Architectural Context

The persistence architecture occupies the Infrastructure Layer of the Hexagonal Architecture introduced in Chapter 19.

```text
                 React Frontend
                       │
                       ▼
              Django REST API
                       │
                       ▼
            Application Services
                       │
                       ▼
              Repository Interfaces
                       │
        ┌──────────────┴──────────────┐
        ▼                             ▼
 Django Repository             Prolog Repository
        │                             │
        ▼                             ▼
 Django ORM                    SWI-Prolog Engine
        │
        ▼
 PostgreSQL Database
```

Repositories isolate the Application Layer from infrastructure details while coordinating access to relational and symbolic persistence.

---

# 20.18.4 Persistence Architecture

The persistence subsystem is organized into four complementary layers.

| Layer                      | Responsibility                           |
| -------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- |
| Domain                     | Aggregate roots, entities, value objects |
| Repository Interfaces      | Contracts for persistence operations     |
| Repository Implementations | Django ORM mapping and reconstruction    |
| Database                   | PostgreSQL storage                       |

This separation prevents framework-specific concerns from contaminating the clinical domain model.

---

# 20.18.5 Aggregate Reconstruction

Repositories reconstruct complete aggregates rather than returning isolated database rows.

Example:

```text
NutritionAssessment
        │
        ├──────── Anthropometry
        ├──────── Clinical Findings
        ├──────── Dietary Records
        ├──────── Laboratory Results
        └──────── Lifestyle Assessment
```

A single repository operation returns the fully initialized aggregate required by the application service.

---

# 20.18.6 Repository Architecture

Each aggregate root owns a dedicated repository.

Representative repositories include:

| Repository               | Aggregate            |
| ------------------------ | -------------------- |
| HealthProfileRepository  | Health Profile       |
| AssessmentRepository     | Nutrition Assessment |
| RecommendationRepository | Recommendation       |
| KnowledgeRepository      | Knowledge Module     |
| MonitoringRepository     | Monitoring Record    |
| AuditRepository          | Audit Events         |

Repositories encapsulate query construction, aggregate reconstruction, persistence, and transaction management.

---

# 20.18.7 Repository Interfaces

The Domain Layer defines repository contracts independently of Django.

Illustrative example:

```python
class AssessmentRepository(ABC):

    @abstractmethod
    def save(self, assessment: NutritionAssessment):
        pass

    @abstractmethod
    def find_by_id(self, assessment_id):
        pass

    @abstractmethod
    def find_by_patient(self, patient_id):
        pass
```

Infrastructure implementations satisfy these contracts using Django ORM while remaining invisible to the domain model.

---

# 20.18.8 ORM Models

Django ORM models function exclusively as persistence representations.

Responsibilities include:

* table mapping,
* foreign key relationships,
* database constraints,
* migrations,
* indexing metadata.

They **do not** contain:

* nutrition logic,
* symbolic reasoning,
* recommendation algorithms,
* clinical validation,
* inference orchestration.

These responsibilities belong to the Domain and Application layers.

---

# 20.18.9 Mapping Strategy

Persistence requires bidirectional translation.

```text
Database Row
      │
      ▼
Django ORM Model
      │
      ▼
Repository Mapper
      │
      ▼
Domain Entity
```

Saving follows the reverse direction.

This mapping isolates the domain model from relational concerns.

---

# 20.18.10 Transaction Management

Repositories coordinate transactional consistency across aggregate boundaries.

Typical workflow:

```text
Start Transaction
        │
Save Assessment
        │
Generate Assessment Facts
        │
Create Inference Session
        │
Persist Recommendations
        │
Commit
```

Failures trigger rollback to maintain aggregate consistency.

---

# 20.18.11 Lazy vs Eager Loading

Loading strategies are selected according to aggregate boundaries.

| Strategy           | Appropriate Usage                 |
| ------------------ | --------------------------------- |
| Lazy Loading       | Historical records, audit history |
| Eager Loading      | Complete assessment aggregates    |
| Selective Prefetch | Recommendations and meal plans    |

This minimizes unnecessary database access while preserving domain consistency.

---

# 20.18.12 Query Optimization

Repository implementations should optimize common clinical workflows using:

* indexed foreign keys,
* `select_related`,
* `prefetch_related`,
* query batching,
* pagination for historical records.

Optimization decisions remain encapsulated within repository implementations rather than leaking into application services.

---

# 20.18.13 Domain Events

Repositories publish domain events following successful transactions.

Examples include:

* AssessmentCompleted
* RecommendationGenerated
* MonitoringRecorded
* KnowledgeModulePublished

Application services respond to these events without introducing tight coupling between bounded contexts.

---

# 20.18.14 Exception Translation

Infrastructure exceptions are translated into domain-specific exceptions.

Example:

```text
DatabaseError
        │
        ▼
AssessmentPersistenceException
```

This prevents infrastructure concerns from propagating into the application layer.

---

# 20.18.15 Testing Strategy

The separation between repositories and domain entities supports multiple testing levels.

| Test Type         | Scope                                                            |
| ----------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Unit Tests        | Domain entities and application services using mock repositories |
| Repository Tests  | ORM mapping and SQL behavior                                     |
| Integration Tests | End-to-end persistence with PostgreSQL                           |
| System Tests      | Complete clinical workflows                                      |

This layered testing strategy improves reliability while reducing the need for heavyweight integration environments during routine development.

---

# 20.18.16 Architectural Significance

The Domain Persistence Architecture preserves the integrity of the Domain-Driven Design adopted throughout NutriLogic. By ensuring that business logic depends only on repository interfaces rather than concrete persistence technologies, the architecture promotes maintainability, testability, and long-term adaptability. The persistence layer becomes an implementation detail rather than the foundation of the system, allowing the clinical domain model to evolve independently of database or framework changes.

---

# 20.18.17 Transition to Physical Database Optimization

Having defined how domain entities interact with the persistence layer, the remaining implementation details focus on database performance and operational concerns. The next section, **20.19 Database Optimization, Indexing, and Performance Engineering**, will specify indexing strategies, query optimization, caching approaches, transaction isolation, scalability considerations, backup strategies, and operational practices required to support efficient execution of the Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.19 Persistence Performance, Scalability, and Reliability Architecture

---

## 20.19.1 Introduction

A Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System (CNDSS) must provide timely, reliable, and consistent access to clinical data, symbolic reasoning artefacts, and knowledge resources. While the logical and physical persistence architectures define how information is organized and stored, operational success depends on how efficiently that information can be retrieved, processed, protected, and recovered.
