This section defines the persistence performance architecture for NutriLogic, addressing indexing strategies, query optimization, transaction management, caching, scalability, backup, recovery, monitoring, and operational reliability. These mechanisms ensure that the persistence layer can support both current implementation requirements and future growth without compromising data integrity or clinical safety.

---

# 20.19.2 Objectives

The Persistence Performance Architecture aims to:

1. Optimize database performance for clinical workflows.
2. Minimize query latency during nutrition assessments and recommendation generation.
3. Ensure transactional consistency and reliability.
4. Support horizontal and vertical scalability.
5. Protect patient information through robust backup and recovery mechanisms.
6. Improve operational observability.
7. Maintain high availability while preserving data integrity.

---

# 20.19.3 Performance Architecture Overview

```text id="w6m2ar"
                     React Frontend
                           │
                           ▼
                     Django REST API
                           │
                  Application Services
                           │
                  Repository Layer
                           │
             ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐
             ▼                           ▼
      PostgreSQL Database         SWI-Prolog Engine
             │                           │
             ▼                           ▼
      Query Optimizer          Knowledge Modules
             │
             ▼
     Indexes • Cache • WAL
             │
             ▼
 Backup • Monitoring • Recovery
```

The persistence architecture integrates storage optimization with reasoning infrastructure while maintaining clear separation between clinical data and symbolic knowledge.

---

# 20.19.4 Indexing Strategy

Efficient indexing is essential for reducing response times during patient assessments and longitudinal record retrieval.

### Primary Indexes

| Table                  | Indexed Attributes              |
| ---------------------- | ------------------------------- |
| `nutrition_assessment` | `patient_id`, `assessment_date` |
| `recommendation`       | `assessment_id`, `created_at`   |
| `monitoring_record`    | `patient_id`, `follow_up_date`  |
| `audit_event`          | `timestamp`, `user_id`          |
| `knowledge_module`     | `module_name`, `status`         |

### Composite Indexes

Composite indexes are recommended for frequently executed queries such as:

* patient assessment history,
* active recommendations,
* guideline version lookups,
* rule activation statistics.

Indexes should be reviewed periodically using PostgreSQL query analysis tools.

---

# 20.19.5 Query Optimization

Repository implementations should optimize database interaction by employing:

* `select_related()` for one-to-one and many-to-one relationships,
* `prefetch_related()` for one-to-many relationships,
* pagination for longitudinal histories,
* projection queries when full aggregates are unnecessary,
* batching to reduce repeated database access.

Business logic must never contain SQL optimization code; such concerns remain encapsulated within repository implementations.

---

# 20.19.6 Transaction Management

Clinical operations frequently involve multiple coordinated persistence actions.

A typical workflow includes:

```text id="g0v8ja"
Begin Transaction
        │
Persist Assessment
        │
Generate Assessment Facts
        │
Invoke Symbolic Reasoner
        │
Persist Diagnosis
        │
Persist Recommendation
        │
Persist Provenance
        │
Commit Transaction
```

If any operation fails, the transaction is rolled back to preserve consistency.

Transactions should be kept as short as practical to reduce lock contention and improve concurrency.

---

# 20.19.7 Caching Strategy

Caching improves responsiveness while reducing unnecessary database activity.

| Cache Target                   | Strategy                    |
| ------------------------------ | --------------------------- |
| Food composition data          | Long-lived cache            |
| Nutrition ontology             | In-memory cache             |
| Knowledge modules              | Read-through cache          |
| Guideline metadata             | Shared application cache    |
| Frequently used reference data | Application startup preload |

Patient-specific clinical data should not be cached beyond the lifetime necessary for request processing unless governed by explicit security and privacy controls.

---

# 20.19.8 Scalability Strategy

The persistence architecture supports progressive scaling.

### Vertical Scaling

* increased CPU resources,
* additional memory,
* SSD storage,
* PostgreSQL tuning.

### Horizontal Scaling

* read replicas,
* load-balanced application servers,
* asynchronous processing,
* modular deployment by bounded context.

The architecture deliberately avoids assumptions that require immediate microservice adoption while remaining compatible with future decomposition.

---

# 20.19.9 Backup and Recovery

Clinical systems require dependable data protection.

Recommended strategy:

| Backup Type              | Frequency         |
| ------------------------ | ----------------- |
| Incremental backup       | Daily             |
| Full backup              | Weekly            |
| Transaction log archival | Continuous        |
| Restore verification     | Scheduled testing |

Recovery procedures should be documented, rehearsed, and validated to ensure acceptable recovery objectives for the deployment environment.

---

# 20.19.10 Reliability and Fault Tolerance

Reliability mechanisms include:

* transactional integrity,
* retry strategies for transient failures,
* graceful degradation of non-critical services,
* immutable audit records,
* integrity verification of knowledge assets,
* controlled handling of inference engine failures.

If symbolic reasoning is temporarily unavailable, the system should fail safely by informing users that recommendations cannot be generated rather than producing incomplete or potentially misleading advice.

---

# 20.19.11 Monitoring and Observability

Operational observability enables proactive maintenance and troubleshooting.

Representative metrics include:

| Metric                    | Purpose                        |
| ------------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| Query latency             | Database performance           |
| Repository execution time | Application efficiency         |
| Transaction duration      | Lock analysis                  |
| Cache hit ratio           | Cache effectiveness            |
| Inference execution time  | Symbolic reasoning performance |
| Failed transactions       | Reliability monitoring         |
| Database connections      | Capacity planning              |

Monitoring data should support trend analysis and capacity planning.

---

# 20.19.12 Security Considerations

Performance optimizations must not compromise security.

Key considerations include:

* encryption of sensitive data at rest and in transit,
* least-privilege database accounts,
* parameterized queries via the ORM,
* secure backup storage,
* controlled access to audit data,
* periodic review of database permissions.

These measures complement the governance architecture described in Section 20.16.

---

# 20.19.13 Operational Maintenance

Routine operational activities include:

* index maintenance,
* database statistics updates,
* storage monitoring,
* migration verification,
* backup validation,
* integrity checking,
* archival of historical audit records.

Operational procedures should be documented and incorporated into deployment and maintenance workflows.

---

# 20.19.14 Quality Attribute Mapping

| Quality Attribute | Architectural Mechanism                                 |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- |
| Performance       | Indexes, optimized queries, caching                     |
| Scalability       | Modular schemas, read replicas, asynchronous processing |
| Reliability       | ACID transactions, retries, recovery procedures         |
| Availability      | Monitoring, backup, fail-safe behavior                  |
| Maintainability   | Repository abstraction, migration discipline            |
| Security          | Encryption, least privilege, audit trails               |
| Clinical Safety   | Transactional consistency, deterministic persistence    |

---

# 20.19.15 Architectural Significance

The Persistence Performance, Scalability, and Reliability Architecture ensures that NutriLogic's persistence layer is not only functionally correct but also operationally robust. By integrating optimization techniques with governance, transactional integrity, and monitoring, the architecture supports dependable clinical decision support while remaining adaptable to future increases in workload, knowledge complexity, and deployment scale.

---

# 20.19.16 Transition to Chapter Summary

Sections 20.12 through 20.19 have collectively established the conceptual, logical, physical, and operational dimensions of the NutriLogic persistence architecture. The final section of this chapter consolidates these contributions by mapping the persistence architecture to the system's bounded contexts, repositories, aggregates, database schemas, and functional requirements, providing an integrated view of how persistent storage supports the overall neuro-symbolic Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System.

---

# Chapter 20: Database and Persistence Architecture

# 20.20 Persistence Architecture Summary and Traceability Framework

---

## 20.20.1 Introduction

The preceding sections have progressively defined the persistence architecture of NutriLogic, beginning with the enterprise information model and culminating in the operational considerations for performance, scalability, and reliability. Together, these sections establish a comprehensive persistence architecture that supports the neuro-symbolic Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System (CNDSS).

Unlike conventional software systems, NutriLogic's persistence layer manages not only patient information but also symbolic knowledge assets, ontology metadata, reasoning artefacts, governance records, and decision provenance. Consequently, the persistence architecture forms the foundation upon which explainability, reproducibility, clinical safety, and long-term maintainability are built.

This concluding section synthesizes the chapter by demonstrating the traceability between system requirements, architectural decisions, persistence mechanisms, and implementation artefacts.

---

# 20.20.2 Persistence Architecture Overview

The persistence architecture is organized into four complementary layers.

```text id="a4u7zm"
                 Clinical Applications
                         │
                         ▼
              Application Services Layer
                         │
                         ▼
              Repository Abstraction Layer
                         │
         ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
         ▼               ▼                ▼
 Clinical Database   Knowledge Base   Governance Store
         │               │                │
         └───────────────┼────────────────┘
                         ▼
                  PostgreSQL Storage
```

Each layer has a clearly defined responsibility.

| Layer                | Responsibility                                       |
| -------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- |
| Application Services | Orchestrate persistence operations                   |
| Repository Layer     | Aggregate reconstruction and persistence abstraction |
| Persistence Stores   | Clinical, knowledge, governance, and reasoning data  |
| PostgreSQL           | Reliable transactional storage                       |

This layered organization aligns directly with the Hexagonal Architecture introduced in Chapter 19.

---

# 20.20.3 Bounded Context to Database Schema Mapping

The persistence architecture mirrors the bounded contexts of the domain model.

| Bounded Context           | PostgreSQL Schema | Principal Aggregate |
| ------------------------- | ----------------- | ------------------- |
| Identity & Access         | `identity`        | User                |
| Clinical Assessment       | `clinical`        | NutritionAssessment |
| Clinical Decision Support | `clinical`        | Recommendation      |
| Symbolic Reasoning        | `reasoning`       | InferenceSession    |
| Knowledge Engineering     | `knowledge`       | KnowledgeModule     |
| Governance                | `governance`      | DecisionProvenance  |
| Monitoring                | `monitoring`      | MonitoringRecord    |
| Shared Services           | `shared`          | ReferenceData       |

This mapping demonstrates architectural consistency across Chapters 19 and 20.

---

# 20.20.4 Aggregate–Repository Traceability

Each aggregate root is managed through a dedicated repository.

| Aggregate Root      | Repository               | Primary Persistence Store |
| ------------------- | ------------------------ | ------------------------- |
| User                | UserRepository           | PostgreSQL                |
| HealthProfile       | HealthProfileRepository  | PostgreSQL                |
| NutritionAssessment | AssessmentRepository     | PostgreSQL                |
| Recommendation      | RecommendationRepository | PostgreSQL                |
| InferenceSession    | InferenceRepository      | PostgreSQL                |
| KnowledgeModule     | KnowledgeRepository      | PostgreSQL                |
| MonitoringRecord    | MonitoringRepository     | PostgreSQL                |
| DecisionProvenance  | GovernanceRepository     | PostgreSQL                |

This mapping reinforces the persistence ignorance principle established in Chapter 19.

---
