# 20.20.10 End-to-End Persistence Lifecycle

The complete persistence lifecycle illustrates how information flows through the platform.

```text id="m8tye2"
Patient
    │
Assessment
    │
Clinical Database
    │
Assessment Facts
    │
Symbolic Reasoning
    │
Recommendation
    │
Explanation
    │
Decision Provenance
    │
Monitoring
    │
Knowledge Improvement
```

This lifecycle demonstrates the continuous interaction between patient information, symbolic reasoning, governance, and longitudinal monitoring.

---

# 20.20.11 Architecture Decision Record

## ADR-020: Polyglot Persistence and Knowledge-Centric Storage

### Context

NutriLogic manages heterogeneous information including:

* clinical data
* symbolic reasoning artefacts
* ontology concepts
* knowledge graphs
* governance records
* audit trails
* explainability metadata

A single persistence model is insufficient to represent these diverse concerns while preserving modularity and traceability.

### Decision

Adopt a **knowledge-centric persistence architecture** in which PostgreSQL serves as the primary transactional store while the persistence model is organized into bounded contexts that accommodate both relational and symbolic knowledge assets. The architecture separates clinical, reasoning, knowledge, governance, and monitoring concerns through logical schemas and repository abstractions rather than treating all information as homogeneous relational data.

### Rationale

* Preserves aggregate boundaries defined by Domain-Driven Design.
* Supports symbolic reasoning without coupling the domain to the inference engine.
* Enables independent evolution of clinical and knowledge assets.
* Improves maintainability, traceability, and explainability.
* Provides a migration path for introducing specialized storage technologies (e.g., graph databases or vector stores) if future requirements justify them, without requiring changes to the core domain model.

### Consequences

Positive:

* Strong separation of concerns.
* Clear repository boundaries.
* Improved extensibility.
* Better governance and provenance.

Trade-offs:

* Additional mapping between domain entities and persistence models.
* Slightly higher implementation complexity.
* Greater architectural discipline required during development.

---

# 20.20.12 Chapter Summary

This chapter has established the complete persistence architecture of the NutriLogic platform, progressing from conceptual information modelling to physical implementation and operational considerations. The architecture is organized around Domain-Driven Design principles, ensuring that persistence concerns remain aligned with the clinical workflows of the Nutrition Care Process and the modular organization of the neuro-symbolic reasoning engine.

By integrating enterprise information modelling, clinical assessment structures, explainable decision support, knowledge engineering, governance, physical database design, repository abstractions, and operational performance strategies, the persistence layer provides a robust foundation for a trustworthy Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System.

The traceability framework presented in this section demonstrates that every persistence mechanism is explicitly linked to system requirements, architectural decisions, and implementation artefacts. Consequently, the persistence architecture serves not merely as a storage solution but as an enabling infrastructure for explainable, reproducible, secure, and maintainable neuro-symbolic clinical decision support.

---

# Chapter 21: REST API and Application Service Architecture

I have aligned this chapter with the dissertation's existing roadmap rather than inventing a new structure. The roadmap explicitly defines **Chapter 21 as REST API Architecture**, with Application Services, REST APIs, and Authentication assigned to this chapter; Chapter 22 then takes over the React client and dashboard. 

One correction to our earlier planning is therefore important: **Chapter 21 should not become a generic "Django REST Framework" chapter.** Its purpose is to realize the application architecture established in Chapter 19 through secure API contracts and application-service orchestration.

---

# Chapter 21: REST API and Application Service Architecture

## 21.1 Introduction

The preceding chapters established the architectural foundations of NutriLogic as a knowledge-centric, neuro-symbolic Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System (CNDSS). Chapters 6–18 defined the system's logical, knowledge, reasoning, governance, and integration architectures, while Chapter 19 established the Domain-Driven Application Architecture and Chapter 20 defined the persistence architecture.

The next architectural boundary is the **application interface** through which external clients interact with these capabilities.

The REST API therefore serves as more than a collection of HTTP endpoints. It constitutes the controlled application boundary between the React presentation layer and the domain-oriented backend. It translates external requests into application commands and queries, invokes the appropriate Application Services, enforces authentication and authorization, validates request contracts, and transforms domain results into stable API representations.

The API must simultaneously preserve several architectural constraints:

1. Clinical business rules remain within the domain layer.
2. Application Services orchestrate use cases rather than implementing clinical rules.
3. REST controllers remain thin.
4. Persistence remains behind repository abstractions.
5. Symbolic reasoning remains behind the Anti-Corruption Layer.
6. Sensitive health information is protected through explicit security controls.
7. API responses expose sufficient information for explainability without exposing internal implementation details.

This chapter therefore realizes the **Application and Interface View** of NutriLogic.

---

# 21.2 Objectives

The REST API and Application Service Architecture aims to:

* expose NutriLogic capabilities through stable RESTful interfaces;
* translate HTTP requests into application commands and queries;
* isolate presentation concerns from domain logic;
* implement the Application Service layer defined in Chapter 19;
* provide secure authentication and authorization;
* establish consistent request and response contracts;
* support the Nutrition Care Process through API workflows;
* provide controlled access to recommendations and explanations;
* expose longitudinal monitoring and history;
* provide appropriate administrative interfaces for governed knowledge management;
* standardize validation and error handling;
* preserve clinical provenance across API operations;
* support future integration with healthcare information systems;
* provide a testable and versionable API boundary.

---

# 21.3 Architectural Context

The API occupies the boundary between the presentation environment and the application core.

```text
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                  React Presentation Layer                │
│                  Chapter 22                              │
└─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘
                              │ HTTPS / JSON
                              ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                    REST API Layer                        │
│              Django REST Framework                       │
│                                                         │
│ Authentication │ Authorization │ Validation │ Routing    │
└─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘
                              │ DTO / Command / Query
                              ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                  Application Layer                       │
│                                                         │
│ Assessment │ Inference │ Recommendation │ Explanation   │
│ Monitoring │ Knowledge │ Identity                         │
└─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘
                              │
                              ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                     Domain Layer                         │
│                                                         │
│ Aggregates │ Entities │ Value Objects │ Domain Services │
└─────────────┬───────────────────────────┬───────────────┘
              │                           │
              ▼                           ▼
       Repository Ports             Inference Port
              │                           │
              ▼                           ▼
        PostgreSQL                  Prolog ACL
                                          │
                                          ▼
                                    SWI-Prolog
```

This structure directly realizes the architectural principle that external technologies communicate with the application core through defined ports and adapters. Chapter 19 explicitly establishes this dependency direction and prohibits domain entities from importing Django, DRF, or PySWIP. 

---

# 21.4 API Architectural Principles

## 21.4.1 API as an Application Boundary

The REST API is an application boundary rather than the location of business logic.

A controller should therefore perform approximately four responsibilities:

```text
HTTP Request
     ↓
Authenticate
     ↓
Validate / Deserialize
     ↓
Invoke Application Service
     ↓
Serialize Result
     ↓
HTTP Response
```

It should **not**:

* calculate clinical diagnoses;
* construct Prolog queries;
* directly manipulate Django models;
* implement recommendation rules;
* bypass repositories;
* construct explanations independently.

This preserves the architectural conformance rule established in Chapter 19 that controllers delegate business operations to Application Services. 

---

# 21.5 Application Service Architecture

Application Services form the primary orchestration layer behind the API.

Chapter 19 already identifies the principal Application Services:

| Application Service                | Responsibility                   |
| ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| `AssessmentApplicationService`     | Coordinate nutrition assessments |
| `InferenceApplicationService`      | Execute symbolic reasoning       |
| `RecommendationApplicationService` | Generate interventions           |
| `ExplanationApplicationService`    | Produce explanations             |
| `MonitoringApplicationService`     | Manage longitudinal follow-up    |
| `KnowledgeApplicationService`      | Coordinate knowledge lifecycle   |

These services orchestrate workflows but do not themselves become repositories or HTTP controllers. 

---

# 21.6 Command and Query Model

Chapter 19 adopted **lightweight CQRS**, retaining a shared domain model and relational database rather than introducing full event sourcing. 

Chapter 21 therefore distinguishes between:

### Commands

Commands change state.

Examples:

```text
RegisterUser
UpdateHealthProfile
SubmitNutritionAssessment
GenerateRecommendation
CreateMonitoringGoal
RecordFollowUp
```

### Queries

Queries retrieve information without modifying domain state.

Examples:

```text
GetHealthProfile
GetAssessment
GetRecommendation
GetExplanation
GetAssessmentHistory
GetMonitoringHistory
GetKnowledgeMetadata
```

The API layer translates HTTP requests into these application operations.

---

# 21.7 Application Request Flow

A typical recommendation request follows:

```text
Client
  │
  │ POST /api/v1/assessments
  ▼
API Controller
  │
  ▼
Request DTO
  │
  ▼
Authentication / Authorization
  │
  ▼
AssessmentApplicationService
  │
  ▼
NutritionAssessment Aggregate
  │
  ▼
AssessmentRepository
  │
  ▼
AssessmentSubmitted
  │
  ▼
InferenceApplicationService
  │
  ▼
AssessmentFact Generation
  │
  ▼
Inference Port
  │
  ▼
Anti-Corruption Layer
  │
  ▼
SWI-Prolog
  │
  ▼
Inference Result
  │
  ▼
Recommendation / Explanation
  │
  ▼
Persistence
  │
  ▼
API Response
```

This is consistent with the runtime sequence already defined in Chapter 19, where the request moves from React through the Assessment API, Application Service, repository, domain event, inference service, Anti-Corruption Layer, SWI-Prolog, recommendation service, and explanation service before reaching the dashboard. 

---

# 21.8 API Resource Model

The API should expose **resources and capabilities**, rather than exposing internal database tables.

The principal resource model is:

```text
User
 │
 └── HealthProfile
       │
       └── NutritionAssessment
               │
               ├── ClinicalObservation
               ├── AssessmentFact
               │
               └── InferenceSession
                       │
                       ├── Recommendation
                       └── ExplanationSession
                               │
                               └── ExplanationChain
```

This preserves the domain model established in Chapters 19 and 20.

---

# 21.9 API Versioning

The API shall use explicit versioning.

Recommended base path:

```text
/api/v1/
```

Versioning protects the React application and future external clients from breaking changes.

Future evolution could introduce:

```text
/api/v2/
```

without immediately invalidating existing consumers.

API versions should represent **contract versions**, not internal software versions.

---

# 21.10 Authentication Architecture

Authentication establishes the identity of the API caller.

The system shall support token-based authentication suitable for the React SPA architecture.

```text
User
 │
 ▼
Login
 │
 ▼
Authentication Service
 │
 ▼
Access Token + Refresh Token
 │
 ▼
React Client
 │
 ▼
Authorization Header
 │
 ▼
Protected API
```

The architectural model should distinguish:

* authentication — *Who are you?*
* authorization — *What are you allowed to do?*
* clinical access control — *Which health information are you permitted to access?*

---

# 21.11 Authorization Architecture

Authorization should operate at multiple levels.

### Role level

Example roles:

| Role                    | Principal Capabilities                                |
| ----------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- |
| Patient                 | Own profile, assessments, recommendations, monitoring |
| Nutrition Professional  | Authorized patient records and clinical workflows     |
| Knowledge Administrator | Governed knowledge management                         |
| System Administrator    | System-level administration                           |

The exact production role set should remain configurable rather than hard-coded into the domain model.

---

# 21.12 Resource Ownership

Authentication alone is insufficient.

A patient authenticated as user `A` must not be able to retrieve user `B`'s assessment simply by changing an identifier.

Therefore authorization must enforce:

```text
Authenticated User
        │
        ▼
Resource Ownership / Permission Check
        │
        ▼
Authorized Application Operation
```

This is especially important because nutrition assessments and health profiles constitute sensitive health information.

---

# 21.13 API Endpoint Architecture

The API should be organized around bounded-context capabilities.

### Identity

```text
POST /api/v1/auth/register
POST /api/v1/auth/token
POST /api/v1/auth/token/refresh
POST /api/v1/auth/logout
```

### Profile

```text
GET   /api/v1/profile
PATCH /api/v1/profile
```

### Assessment

```text
POST /api/v1/assessments
GET  /api/v1/assessments/{id}
GET  /api/v1/assessments
```

### Recommendations

```text
POST /api/v1/recommendations
GET  /api/v1/recommendations/{id}
GET  /api/v1/recommendations
```

### Explanation

```text
GET /api/v1/explanations/{id}
GET /api/v1/inference-sessions/{id}/explanation
```

### Monitoring

```text
POST /api/v1/monitoring/goals
GET  /api/v1/monitoring
POST /api/v1/monitoring/follow-ups
```

### Knowledge

Administrative access only:

```text
GET /api/v1/knowledge/modules
GET /api/v1/knowledge/modules/{id}
GET /api/v1/knowledge/versions/{id}
```

### Health

```text
GET /api/v1/health
```

The exact URL structure should be finalized during implementation against the existing Django project. The architectural principle is more important than prematurely locking every route.

---

# 21.14 Assessment API

The assessment endpoint is one of the most important application interfaces.

Example request:

```json
{
  "assessment_date": "2026-08-08",
  "observations": [
    {
      "type": "anthropometric",
      "measurement": "weight",
      "value": 72.5,
      "unit": "kg"
    },
    {
      "type": "anthropometric",
      "measurement": "height",
      "value": 1.68,
      "unit": "m"
    }
  ],
  "dietary_observations": [],
  "clinical_observations": []
}
```

The API should not directly convert this JSON into Prolog facts.

Instead:

```text
JSON
 ↓
Assessment DTO
 ↓
AssessmentApplicationService
 ↓
NutritionAssessment
 ↓
AssessmentFactGenerator
 ↓
Inference Application Service
 ↓
Prolog ACL
```

This preserves the boundary between external representation and symbolic representation.

---

# 21.15 Recommendation API

Recommendation generation should expose the **decision result**, not the internal reasoning implementation.

A response should conceptually contain:

```json
{
  "recommendation_id": "...",
  "assessment_id": "...",
  "status": "generated",
  "recommendations": [],
  "explanation_session_id": "...",
  "knowledge_version": "...",
  "created_at": "..."
}
```

The important architectural point is the reference to the `ExplanationSession`.

A recommendation without its associated provenance and explanation should not be treated as a complete clinical decision artefact.

---

# 21.16 Explanation API

The Explanation API exposes the reasoning evidence necessary for transparency.

Conceptually:

```json
{
  "explanation_session_id": "...",
  "recommendation_id": "...",
  "knowledge_version": "...",
  "chains": [
    {
      "conclusion": "...",
      "supporting_facts": [],
      "activated_rules": [],
      "evidence": []
    }
  ]
}
```

This allows the frontend to construct an explanation interface without knowing anything about Prolog predicates or PySWIP internals.

That distinction is crucial:

> **The API exposes explanations; it does not expose the reasoning engine.**

---

# 21.17 Error Architecture

All API errors should follow a consistent structure.

Example:

```json
{
  "error": {
    "code": "INVALID_ASSESSMENT",
    "message": "The nutrition assessment could not be processed.",
    "details": [
      {
        "field": "weight",
        "reason": "Value must be greater than zero."
      }
    ],
    "request_id": "..."
  }
}
```

Error categories should distinguish:

* authentication failures;
* authorization failures;
* validation errors;
* resource-not-found errors;
* domain invariant violations;
* knowledge validation failures;
* inference failures;
* persistence failures;
* unexpected system failures.

Internal exceptions, stack traces, database errors, and Prolog implementation details must never be returned to clients.

---

# 21.18 Clinical Safety and Fail-Safe API Behaviour

The API represents a clinical decision-support boundary and therefore requires stronger failure semantics than a conventional CRUD application.

If inference fails:

```text
Inference Failure
      │
      ▼
No Recommendation
      │
      ▼
Controlled Error State
      │
      ▼
User Notification
```

The system must **not** return a partially generated recommendation as though inference succeeded.

Similarly:

* missing evidence should not be silently interpreted as negative evidence;
* invalid clinical states must be rejected;
* contraindication failures must block unsafe interventions;
* knowledge-version failures must prevent uncontrolled inference;
* unavailable reasoning infrastructure must produce a controlled failure.

This follows the project's explicit **Clinical Safety First** principle, which states that safety takes precedence over recommendation completeness, performance, and convenience. 

---

# 21.19 Idempotency and Duplicate Requests

Certain API operations may be retried by clients or intermediary infrastructure.

For state-changing clinical operations, the system should support idempotency where appropriate.

For example:

```text
Client
  │
  │ POST Assessment
  │ Idempotency-Key: abc123
  ▼
API
  │
  ▼
Application Service
```

If the same request is submitted again using the same idempotency key, the system should avoid creating duplicate clinical records.

This is particularly important for assessment submission and recommendation-generation workflows.

---

# 21.20 Pagination and Query Management

Longitudinal clinical data should never be returned as unbounded collections.

Endpoints such as:

```text
GET /api/v1/assessments
GET /api/v1/recommendations
GET /api/v1/monitoring
```

should support pagination.

Conceptually:

```text
?page=1&page_size=20
```

Filtering and sorting should be constrained to supported query fields.

This protects both API performance and frontend usability.

---

# 21.21 API Security Architecture

Security mechanisms should include:

* HTTPS in production;
* secure token handling;
* access-token expiration;
* refresh-token controls;
* role-based authorization;
* object-level authorization;
* request validation;
* rate limiting;
* secure CORS configuration;
* CSRF considerations where applicable;
* security headers;
* audit logging;
* sensitive-data minimization;
* controlled error disclosure.

The API must not rely on the frontend to enforce security.

A React client can be modified or bypassed.

**The backend must enforce every security boundary.**

---

# 21.22 Auditability

Security and clinical governance require audit records for significant operations.

Representative events include:

```text
USER_REGISTERED
PROFILE_UPDATED
ASSESSMENT_CREATED
INFERENCE_REQUESTED
INFERENCE_COMPLETED
RECOMMENDATION_GENERATED
EXPLANATION_ACCESSED
KNOWLEDGE_MODULE_UPDATED
KNOWLEDGE_VERSION_ACTIVATED
```

Audit records should capture appropriate metadata without unnecessarily duplicating sensitive clinical information.

---

# 21.23 API and Knowledge Versioning

Because recommendations depend on governed knowledge, API responses should make knowledge provenance available where clinically relevant.

A recommendation should be associated with:

```text
Recommendation
     │
     ├── KnowledgeModule
     ├── KnowledgeVersion
     ├── InferenceSession
     └── ExplanationSession
```

This permits later reconstruction of the knowledge environment that produced the recommendation.

This is particularly important for:

* reproducibility;
* clinical review;
* debugging;
* regulatory audit;
* research evaluation;
* knowledge evolution.

---

# 21.24 Interoperability Boundary

The REST API should be designed as an interoperability boundary rather than a permanently closed frontend API.

Future clients may include:

```text
React SPA
Mobile Application
Clinical Portal
Research Interface
External Healthcare System
```

The domain should remain independent of these clients.

The API therefore becomes an architectural adapter:

```text
External Client
      │
      ▼
REST Contract
      │
      ▼
Application Layer
      │
      ▼
Domain
```

Future FHIR interoperability may be introduced at the integration boundary without forcing FHIR representations into the core domain model.

This is consistent with the interoperability principle already established in the dissertation. 

---

# 21.25 API Documentation

The API should have machine-readable documentation.

The implementation should produce an OpenAPI specification describing:

* endpoints;
* methods;
* authentication requirements;
* request schemas;
* response schemas;
* validation constraints;
* error responses;
* pagination;
* versioning.

This specification becomes the formal contract between Chapter 21 and Chapter 22.

---

# 21.26 API Technology Mapping

| Architectural Responsibility | Technology                         |
| ---------------------------- | ---------------------------------- |
| HTTP/API framework           | Django REST Framework              |
| Authentication               | JWT-based authentication           |
| Serialization                | DRF Serializers / DTO mapping      |
| Routing                      | DRF Routers / URL configuration    |
| Application orchestration    | Python Application Services        |
| Domain model                 | Pure Python entities/value objects |
| Persistence                  | Repository interfaces              |
| Database adapter             | Django ORM / PostgreSQL            |
| Symbolic reasoning adapter   | PySWIP / Prolog ACL                |
| API documentation            | OpenAPI                            |
| Testing                      | Django/DRF test framework          |

The important distinction is that these technologies implement architectural roles; they do not define the domain itself.

---

# 21.27 Recommended Backend API Structure

The implementation should follow the boundaries established in Chapter 19.

```text
backend/
├── config/
│
├── domains/
│   ├── users/
│   ├── assessment/
│   ├── inference/
│   ├── intervention/
│   ├── explanation/
│   ├── monitoring/
│   └── knowledge/
│
├── application/
│   ├── commands/
│   ├── queries/
│   ├── services/
│   └── dto/
│
├── infrastructure/
│   ├── database/
│   ├── repositories/
│   ├── prolog/
│   └── security/
│
└── interfaces/
    └── api/
        ├── auth/
        ├── assessment/
        ├── recommendations/
        ├── explanations/
        ├── monitoring/
        ├── knowledge/
        └── health/
```

This is an architectural target, not a requirement that every directory become a separate Django application.

---

# 21.28 API Testing Architecture

Testing shall occur at multiple levels.

### Unit level

Test:

* DTO validation;
* Application Services;
* command handlers;
* query handlers;
* authorization policies.

### Integration level

Test:

```text
API
 ↓
Application Service
 ↓
Repository
 ↓
PostgreSQL
```

### Symbolic integration

Test:

```text
API
 ↓
Application Service
 ↓
Inference Port
 ↓
Prolog ACL
 ↓
SWI-Prolog
```

### End-to-end

Test:

```text
React
 ↓
REST API
 ↓
Application Layer
 ↓
Database
 ↓
Prolog
 ↓
Recommendation
 ↓
Explanation
```

The full verification strategy will be developed in Chapter 24, consistent with the dissertation roadmap, which assigns Chapter 24 to Testing and Validation. 

---

# 21.29 API Quality Attributes

| Quality Attribute | API Mechanism                                |
| ----------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| Security          | Authentication, authorization, HTTPS         |
| Maintainability   | Application Services, DTOs, bounded contexts |
| Testability       | Dependency inversion and service isolation   |
| Performance       | Pagination, caching, optimized queries       |
| Reliability       | Validation, idempotency, controlled failures |
| Explainability    | Explanation resources and provenance         |
| Interoperability  | Versioned REST contracts                     |
| Scalability       | Stateless API design                         |
| Clinical Safety   | Fail-safe inference handling                 |
| Auditability      | API and domain event logging                 |

---

# 21.30 Architectural Decision Record

## ADR-021: Adoption of a Versioned REST Application Boundary

### Status

**Accepted**

### Context

NutriLogic requires a stable interface between its React presentation layer and the Domain-Driven backend. Direct coupling between frontend components, Django ORM models, Prolog predicates, and internal domain objects would undermine maintainability and make future client or integration changes costly.

### Decision

NutriLogic shall expose application capabilities through a **versioned REST API**. HTTP controllers shall translate external representations into Application Service commands and queries, while Application Services remain responsible for use-case orchestration.

The API shall not directly expose database models or symbolic reasoning internals.

### Rationale

The decision:

* preserves the separation established by Hexagonal Architecture;
* provides a stable contract for the React client;
* supports future external clients;
* enables independent evolution of internal domain implementations;
* improves testability;
* supports controlled authentication and authorization;
* provides a natural interoperability boundary.

### Consequences

**Positive**

* Clear separation between presentation and application logic.
* Stable client contract.
* Improved testability.
* Easier future integration.
* Controlled security boundary.

**Trade-offs**

* DTO-to-domain mapping introduces additional code.
* API version management creates maintenance overhead.
* Application Services introduce another abstraction layer between controllers and domain objects.

The additional complexity is accepted because NutriLogic's clinical and neuro-symbolic architecture makes direct framework coupling undesirable.

---

# 21.31 API Traceability Matrix

| Requirement / Architectural Concern | API Component                  | Supporting Architecture  |
| ----------------------------------- | ------------------------------ | ------------------------ |
| User registration                   | Identity API                   | Users Context            |
| Authentication                      | Authentication Service         | Security Architecture    |
| Profile management                  | Profile API                    | User / HealthProfile     |
| Nutrition assessment                | Assessment API                 | NutritionAssessment      |
| Recommendation generation           | Recommendation API             | Intervention + Inference |
| Explanation                         | Explanation API                | ExplanationSession       |
| Monitoring                          | Monitoring API                 | Monitoring Context       |
| Knowledge administration            | Knowledge API                  | KnowledgeModule          |
| Auditability                        | Audit interface                | Governance Architecture  |
| Follow-up scheduling                | Monitoring API                 | FR-010                   |
| Frontend consumption                | Versioned REST contract        | Chapter 22               |
| Persistence                         | Repository interfaces          | Chapter 20               |
| Symbolic reasoning                  | Inference Application Service  | Chapters 11, 15          |
| Security                            | Authentication + authorization | Chapter 16               |

The dissertation already associates REST Interfaces with Chapter 21 in its expanded architectural traceability matrix. 

---

# 21.32 Cross-Chapter Traceability

Chapter 21 realizes architectural decisions established previously.

| Previous Chapter | Chapter 21 Realization                                                         |
| ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Chapter 5        | Functional and non-functional requirements become API contracts                |
| Chapter 6        | System architecture becomes an executable application boundary                 |
| Chapter 10       | Nutrition Care Process becomes API workflows                                   |
| Chapters 11–15   | Symbolic reasoning becomes an application capability accessed through services |
| Chapter 16       | Security, governance, explainability, and safety become API constraints        |
| Chapter 17       | Integration architecture becomes concrete service boundaries                   |
| Chapter 18       | Application/development viewpoints become implementation structure             |
| Chapter 19       | Application Services and DDD become API orchestration                          |
| Chapter 20       | Repository and persistence architecture become application infrastructure      |

---

# 21.33 Chapter Summary

This chapter has established the REST API and Application Service Architecture through which NutriLogic exposes its domain capabilities to external clients. The architecture deliberately separates HTTP concerns from application orchestration, domain behaviour, persistence, and symbolic reasoning.

Application Services provide the principal orchestration boundary for nutrition assessment, inference, recommendation generation, explanation, monitoring, and knowledge management. The REST layer translates external requests into commands and queries while enforcing authentication, authorization, validation, error handling, versioning, and auditability.

The architecture also establishes a controlled interface to the neuro-symbolic reasoning subsystem. REST clients never interact directly with SWI-Prolog, PySWIP, Prolog predicates, or persistence models. Instead, symbolic reasoning is invoked through the application and inference ports established by the Domain-Driven and Hexagonal Architectures.

Particular emphasis has been placed on clinical safety. Failed or incomplete reasoning operations must not be represented as successful clinical recommendations, and sensitive clinical information must be protected through backend-enforced authorization and governance controls.

The resulting API architecture provides the stable application boundary required for the React frontend, future healthcare integrations, and subsequent deployment and validation activities. It therefore forms the direct bridge between the architectural foundations established in Chapters 1–20 and the frontend realization presented in Chapter 22.

---

# Chapter 22: Frontend Architecture

## 22.1 Introduction

The preceding chapters established NutriLogic as a knowledge-centric, neuro-symbolic Clinical Nutrition Decision Support System (CNDSS). Chapters 6–18 defined the system, knowledge, reasoning, governance, and integration architectures; Chapter 19 established the Domain-Driven Application Architecture; Chapter 20 defined the persistence architecture; and Chapter 21 established the Application Service and REST API boundary.

Chapter 22 realizes the **Presentation View** of the NutriLogic architecture.

The frontend is implemented as a React-based Single Page Application (SPA) and provides the primary human interaction surface for patients, nutrition professionals, administrators, and other authorized users. Its responsibility is to present clinical workflows and system information in an accessible and understandable form while communicating with the backend through the versioned REST API established in Chapter 21.

The frontend deliberately does **not** contain clinical reasoning logic. Clinical decisions originate from the symbolic reasoning subsystem, while the frontend presents the resulting assessments, recommendations, explanations, monitoring information, and historical records. This separation is consistent with the architectural principle established in Chapter 17 that the presentation layer is responsible for user interaction and visualization while clinical reasoning remains outside the presentation layer.

The frontend therefore acts as a **presentation and interaction adapter**, translating domain capabilities exposed through the REST API into user-oriented workflows.

---

## 22.2 Objectives

The Frontend Architecture aims to:

1. Provide a coherent React-based presentation layer for NutriLogic.
2. Implement the user-facing workflows defined by the Nutrition Care Process.
3. Consume the REST API established in Chapter 21 through explicit client contracts.
4. Separate presentation state from clinical domain state.
5. Provide secure authentication and authorization-aware navigation.
6. Provide nutrition assessment interfaces.
7. Present personalized recommendations and meal-planning information.
8. Provide transparent explanation interfaces for symbolic reasoning results.
9. Support longitudinal monitoring and recommendation history.
10. Provide administrative interfaces for governed knowledge management where authorized.
11. Support analytics and dashboard visualization.
12. Provide responsive and accessible interfaces across supported devices.
13. Handle loading, validation, inference, and failure states safely.
14. Prevent frontend implementation details from becoming a substitute for backend security or clinical validation.
15. Establish a maintainable component architecture capable of evolving with future neuro-symbolic extensions.

These objectives directly realize the architectural mapping that assigns the React Client, Dashboard, and Explainability Interface to Chapter 22.

---

# 22.3 Architectural Context

The frontend occupies the outermost presentation boundary of NutriLogic.

```text
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                    PRESENTATION LAYER                        │
│                                                              │
│                       React SPA                              │
│                                                              │
│  Authentication │ Assessment │ Recommendations │ Monitoring  │
│  Dashboard      │ Meal Plans │ Explanations     │ History    │
└──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘
                               │
                         HTTPS / JSON
                               │
                               ▼
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                    APPLICATION BOUNDARY                      │
│                                                              │
│                  Versioned REST API                          │
│                    Chapter 21                                 │
└──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                 APPLICATION / DOMAIN LAYERS                  │
│                                                              │
│ Application Services │ Domain Model │ Repositories            │
└──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘
                               │
                  ┌────────────┴─────────────┐
                  ▼                          ▼
          PostgreSQL / Data          Symbolic Reasoning
                                      SWI-Prolog
```

The existing architecture explicitly identifies React as the presentation client communicating with the backend through HTTPS/JSON.

The frontend therefore depends on the API but **never directly depends on**:

* Django ORM models;
* PostgreSQL;
* SWI-Prolog;
* PySWIP;
* Prolog predicates;
* knowledge-base files;
* internal repository implementations.

This dependency boundary is essential for preserving the Hexagonal Architecture established in Chapter 19.

---

# 22.4 Frontend Architectural Principles

The frontend follows the following principles.

## 22.4.1 Presentation Separation

The frontend shall contain presentation and interaction logic but shall not reproduce domain reasoning.

For example, the frontend may display:

```text
BMI: 28.4
Risk classification: Overweight
```

but should not independently implement the clinical rule:

```text
IF BMI >= 25 AND BMI < 30
THEN classify_as(overweight)
```

That decision belongs to the domain and symbolic reasoning architecture.

---

## 22.4.2 API-First Integration

All clinical data exchange occurs through the REST API.

```text
React Component
      │
      ▼
Frontend Service
      │
      ▼
HTTP Client
      │
      ▼
REST API
      │
      ▼
Application Service
```

Components therefore do not construct raw API requests throughout the interface.

Instead, API access should be centralized through dedicated client/service modules.

---

## 22.4.3 Backend-Enforced Security

The frontend may hide or disable controls based on the current user's role, but this is strictly a usability mechanism.

It is **not a security boundary**.

Authorization must remain enforced by the backend established in Chapter 21.

A malicious user can modify the React application or invoke the REST API directly; therefore, frontend route guards cannot substitute for server-side authorization.

---

## 22.4.4 Explainability by Default

Because explainability is a defining property of NutriLogic, recommendations should provide an accessible path to their associated `ExplanationSession`.

The architecture therefore treats:

```text
Recommendation
      │
      ▼
ExplanationSession
      │
      ▼
ExplanationChain
      │
      ├── Evidence
      ├── Triggered Rules
      ├── Knowledge Source
      └── Reasoning Trace
```

as a first-class user experience.

The underlying integration architecture already defines an explanation contract containing triggered rules, evidence, knowledge source, confidence, and reasoning trace.

---

## 22.4.5 Clinical Safety in Presentation

The interface must clearly distinguish:

* successful recommendation generation;
* incomplete assessment;
* unavailable inference;
* validation failure;
* system failure;
* recommendation requiring additional information.

The frontend must never make a failed inference appear to be a successful clinical recommendation.

---

# 22.5 Frontend Logical Architecture

The frontend is divided into several conceptual layers.

```text
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                     UI / Pages                           │
│ Dashboard │ Assessment │ Recommendations │ Monitoring    │
└──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘
                               │
┌──────────────────────────────▼───────────────────────────┐
│                  Presentation Components                  │
│ Forms │ Tables │ Cards │ Charts │ Explanation Views       │
└──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘
                               │
┌──────────────────────────────▼───────────────────────────┐
│                    View / UI State                        │
│ Authentication │ Loading │ Errors │ Filters │ Navigation  │
└──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘
                               │
┌──────────────────────────────▼───────────────────────────┐
│                     API Client                            │
│ Auth │ Assessment │ Recommendation │ Monitoring │ History │
└──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
                         REST API
```

This separation prevents individual components from becoming responsible for networking, authentication, business interpretation, and presentation simultaneously.

---

# 22.6 Frontend Component Architecture

The React application should use reusable components rather than placing all functionality inside page-level components.

A conceptual structure is:

```text
frontend/
├── src/
│   ├── app/
│   │   ├── routes/
│   │   └── providers/
│   │
│   ├── components/
│   │   ├── common/
│   │   ├── forms/
│   │   ├── charts/
│   │   ├── nutrition/
│   │   ├── recommendations/
│   │   ├── explanations/
│   │   └── monitoring/
│   │
│   ├── features/
│   │   ├── auth/
│   │   ├── profile/
│   │   ├── assessment/
│   │   ├── recommendations/
│   │   ├── monitoring/
│   │   ├── history/
│   │   ├── knowledge/
│   │   └── analytics/
│   │
│   ├── services/
│   │   ├── api/
│   │   └── auth/
│   │
│   ├── hooks/
│   ├── state/
│   ├── types/
│   ├── utils/
│   └── styles/
```

The exact folder structure may be adapted to the implementation, but the architectural principle is that frontend functionality should be organized around features and presentation responsibilities rather than a single collection of large components.

---

# 22.7 Feature-Based Organization

The bounded contexts established in Chapter 19 provide a useful organizational basis for frontend features.

| Domain Context   | Frontend Feature                |
| ---------------- | ------------------------------- |
| Users & Profiles | Profile Management              |
| Assessment       | Nutrition Assessment            |
| Inference        | Recommendation Processing       |
| Intervention     | Recommendations / Meal Planning |
| Explainability   | Explanation Viewer              |
| Monitoring       | Progress Monitoring             |
| Analytics        | Analytics Dashboard             |
| Knowledge        | Knowledge Administration        |

The existing dissertation explicitly defines this cross-layer realization, mapping Users, Assessment, Knowledge, Inference, Intervention, Monitoring, Explainability, and Analytics across database, API, frontend, and testing layers.

---

# 22.8 Application Shell

The application shell provides persistent UI infrastructure around individual workflows.

Conceptually:

```text
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Header                                                      │
│ Logo │ Search │ Notifications │ User Menu                  │
├──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│              │                                              │
│ Navigation   │              Main Content                    │
│              │                                              │
│ Dashboard    │                                              │
│ Assessment   │                                              │
│ Recommend.   │                                              │
│ Monitoring   │                                              │
│ History      │                                              │
│ Profile      │                                              │
│              │                                              │
├──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Status / Accessibility / Support                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The shell should remain independent of individual clinical workflows.

---

# 22.9 Authentication and Session Architecture

The frontend consumes the authentication services defined by Chapter 21.

The conceptual flow is:

```text
Login Form
    │
    ▼
Authentication API
    │
    ▼
Access Token
    │
    ▼
Authentication State
    │
    ├── User Identity
    ├── Roles / Permissions
    └── Session State
```

The frontend should provide:

* login;
* registration;
* logout;
* token refresh;
* session expiration handling;
* protected navigation;
* authorization-aware presentation.

Sensitive credentials and tokens must not be exposed through unnecessary client-side persistence.

---

# 22.10 Protected Route Architecture

The frontend shall distinguish public and protected routes.

### Public

```text
/
 /login
 /register
 /about
```

### Protected

```text
/dashboard
/profile
/assessment
/recommendations
/monitoring
/history
```

### Privileged

```text
/admin/knowledge
/admin/audit
/admin/analytics
```

Route guards provide navigation control, while actual permission enforcement remains on the backend.

---

# 22.11 Dashboard Architecture

The dashboard is the principal post-authentication workspace.

The existing requirements and architecture identify dashboards, recommendation visualization, progress monitoring, and explanation views as major presentation responsibilities.

A patient-oriented dashboard may contain:

```text
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Nutrition Overview                           │
├──────────────┬──────────────┬────────────────┤
│ Current      │ Active       │ Next           │
│ Assessment   │ Goals        │ Follow-up      │
├──────────────┴──────────────┴────────────────┤
│ Latest Recommendations                        │
│                                              │
│ Recommendation → Why?                        │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Progress Trends                              │
│                                              │
│ Weight / Goals / Dietary Indicators          │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Recent Assessment History                    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The dashboard should summarize information rather than attempt to reproduce the complete clinical reasoning process.

---

# 22.12 Nutrition Assessment Interface

The assessment interface provides the frontend representation of the Nutrition Care Process assessment stage.

The workflow is:

```text
Assessment Form
      │
      ▼
Client Validation
      │
      ▼
Assessment DTO
      │
      ▼
Assessment API
      │
      ▼
Backend Validation
      │
      ▼
NutritionAssessment
```

The form may capture:

* demographic information;
* anthropometric measurements;
* dietary observations;
* clinical observations;
* lifestyle information;
* relevant contextual information.

The architecture established earlier explicitly identifies demographic, clinical, dietary, and lifestyle information as components of the assessment contract.

---

# 22.13 Multi-Step Assessment Workflow

Because nutrition assessment can contain many data elements, the interface should use logically grouped sections rather than a single excessively long form.

A possible structure is:

```text
Step 1 — Personal Information
        ↓
Step 2 — Anthropometry
        ↓
Step 3 — Dietary Assessment
        ↓
Step 4 — Clinical Observations
        ↓
Step 5 — Lifestyle
        ↓
Step 6 — Review
        ↓
Submit Assessment
```

The interface should preserve entered information between steps and provide clear validation feedback.

---

# 22.14 Validation Architecture

Frontend validation improves usability but does not replace backend validation.

Therefore:

```text
Frontend Validation
        │
        ▼
Improved User Experience
        │
        ▼
Backend Validation
        │
        ▼
Authoritative Acceptance
```

Frontend validation should catch obvious errors such as:

* missing required fields;
* malformed values;
* invalid numeric input;
* impossible measurement formats.

The backend remains authoritative for domain and clinical constraints.

---

# 22.15 Recommendation Presentation

Recommendations should be presented as structured decision-support information.

A conceptual recommendation card may contain:

```text
┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Recommendation                             │
│                                            │
│ Increase intake of locally appropriate     │
│ nutrient-dense foods.                      │
│                                            │
│ Reason                                     │
│ ─────────────────────────────────────────  │
│ Nutritional assessment identified ...      │
│                                            │
│ [ Why this recommendation? ]               │
│ [ View details ]                           │
└────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The interface should distinguish recommendation categories where appropriate:

* dietary recommendations;
* meal-planning recommendations;
* lifestyle recommendations;
* monitoring recommendations;
* follow-up recommendations.

The recommendation contract defined in Chapter 17 includes diagnoses, interventions, meal plans, explanations, and monitoring plans.

---

# 22.16 Explainability Interface

The explainability interface is one of the most important frontend components in NutriLogic.

A conventional application might display:

> "Recommended because of your condition."

NutriLogic should instead allow the user to inspect the reasoning chain.

Conceptually:

```text
Recommendation
      │
      ▼
Why was this recommended?
      │
      ▼
Evidence
      │
      ▼
Assessment Facts
      │
      ▼
Triggered Rules
      │
      ▼
Knowledge Source
      │
      ▼
Reasoning Trace
```

The frontend therefore translates the machine-readable explanation contract into a human-readable explanation.

---

# 22.17 Explanation Levels

The interface should support progressive disclosure.

### Level 1 — Simple explanation

```text
Why this recommendation?

Your assessment indicated X, and the
nutrition knowledge base contains a rule
linking X with Y.
```

### Level 2 — Evidence

```text
Evidence:
• Observation A
• Observation B
• Assessment Fact C
```

### Level 3 — Rule

```text
Rule activated:
R-NUTR-014
```

### Level 4 — Reasoning trace

```text
Fact
 ↓
Intermediate conclusion
 ↓
Rule activation
 ↓
Final conclusion
```

This allows ordinary users to understand recommendations without forcing them to understand Prolog.

---

# 22.18 Explanation Safety

The explanation interface must not imply certainty beyond what the reasoning system actually establishes.

For example, it should distinguish:

```text
Rule-derived recommendation
```

from:

```text
Clinical diagnosis confirmed by a physician
```

The interface should not convert a decision-support output into an authoritative diagnosis merely through wording or visual presentation.

---

# 22.19 Monitoring Interface

The Monitoring Context identified in Chapter 19 is realized through a longitudinal frontend experience.

The interface may present:

```text
Current Goals
     │
     ▼
Progress Indicators
     │
     ▼
Historical Assessments
     │
     ▼
Recommendation History
     │
     ▼
Next Follow-Up
```

The monitoring interface should allow users to view progress without modifying historical records in a way that compromises their auditability.

---

# 22.20 Recommendation History

The system explicitly defines **FR-011: Recommendation History**, requiring the system to maintain complete recommendation history.

The frontend therefore requires a dedicated history interface.

Example:

| Date        | Recommendation         | Status    | Explanation |
| ----------- | ---------------------- | --------- | ----------- |
| 08 Aug 2026 | Dietary intervention   | Active    | View        |
| 25 Jul 2026 | Meal-plan adjustment   | Completed | View        |
| 10 Jul 2026 | Lifestyle intervention | Completed | View        |

Historical recommendations should remain distinguishable from current recommendations.

---

# 22.21 Meal Planning Interface

The intervention architecture identifies meal planning as a frontend capability.

The interface may organize meal information by:

* day;
* meal;
* food item;
* portion;
* nutritional category;
* recommendation rationale.

However, the frontend should not independently calculate clinical appropriateness.

The meal plan displayed to the user should originate from the recommendation/intervention service.

---

# 22.22 Analytics Dashboard

The architecture identifies analytics as a distinct bounded context and associates it with clinical dashboards, population nutrition trends, recommendation effectiveness, county-level statistics, research reporting, performance analytics, and quality-improvement metrics.

Analytics interfaces should therefore be role-sensitive.

For example:

### Patient

```text
Personal progress
Personal recommendation history
Personal goals
```

### Nutrition professional

```text
Patient trends
Assessment history
Recommendation effectiveness
```

### Administrator / researcher

```text
Population statistics
County-level trends
System performance
Knowledge utilisation
```

Access to aggregated analytics must not become an indirect mechanism for exposing identifiable patient information.

---

# 22.23 Knowledge Administration Interface

The Knowledge Context may require an administrative interface for authorized users.

Possible capabilities include:

```text
Knowledge Modules
      │
      ├── View
      ├── Validate
      ├── Version
      ├── Approve
      └── Activate
```

However, the frontend must not directly modify Prolog source files.

Instead:

```text
Knowledge UI
     ↓
Knowledge API
     ↓
Knowledge Application Service
     ↓
Governed Knowledge Repository
```

Knowledge modifications remain subject to the governance process established in earlier chapters. The architecture explicitly requires knowledge-base modifications to undergo clinical validation and version control before deployment.

---

# 22.24 Frontend State Architecture

The frontend should distinguish several classes of state.

## Server State

Data obtained from the API:

* assessments;
* recommendations;
* explanations;
* monitoring records;
* history.

## Authentication State

* authenticated user;
* access state;
* roles;
* permissions.

## UI State

* active tab;
* modal visibility;
* form step;
* selected filters.

## Form State

* assessment inputs;
* profile changes;
* meal-plan interactions.

These categories should not be unnecessarily merged into a single global state structure.

---

# 22.25 API Client Architecture

API communication should be centralized.

Conceptually:

```text
services/api/
├── authApi
├── profileApi
├── assessmentApi
├── recommendationApi
├── explanationApi
├── monitoringApi
├── historyApi
├── knowledgeApi
└── analyticsApi
```

A shared HTTP client should handle cross-cutting concerns such as:

* base URL;
* authentication headers;
* serialization;
* common error handling;
* timeout behaviour;
* token refresh.

Feature-specific services should handle resource-specific operations.

---

# 22.26 Data Transfer Types

The frontend should use explicit types corresponding to API contracts.

For example:

```typescript
interface Recommendation {
    id: string;
    assessmentId: string;
    status: string;
    createdAt: string;
    explanationSessionId?: string;
}
```

The exact implementation technology may vary, but the principle is important:

> API representations should be explicitly modeled rather than passed through the interface as untyped arbitrary objects.

This reduces accidental coupling and improves maintainability.

---

# 22.27 Loading States

Clinical workflows may require non-trivial inference time.

The frontend must distinguish:

```text
Submitting Assessment
        ↓
Assessment Accepted
        ↓
Inference in Progress
        ↓
Recommendation Generated
        ↓
Explanation Available
```

The user should not be left wondering whether the system has failed.

---

# 22.28 Inference Progress

Where the backend supports asynchronous inference, the frontend may present:

```text
Assessment received ✓

Preparing assessment facts ✓

Running symbolic reasoning …

Generating recommendation …

Preparing explanation …
```

The frontend must not fabricate inference progress.

If real-time progress is not implemented, the UI should instead use a truthful processing state.

This distinction will become important when Chapter 23 and Chapter 24 document deployment and verification.

---

# 22.29 Error States

The frontend should provide dedicated states for:

### Validation failure

```text
Some information requires correction.
```

### Authentication failure

```text
Your session has expired. Please sign in again.
```

### Authorization failure

```text
You are not authorized to access this resource.
```

### Inference failure

```text
The assessment was received, but a recommendation
could not be generated at this time.
```

### Network failure

```text
The service is temporarily unavailable.
```

### Unknown failure

```text
Something went wrong. Please try again later.
```

Internal stack traces and infrastructure details must never be shown to users.

---

# 22.30 Accessibility Architecture

The frontend should follow accessibility principles including:

* semantic HTML;
* keyboard navigation;
* visible focus states;
* sufficient text contrast;
* accessible form labels;
* meaningful error messages;
* screen-reader-compatible controls;
* non-colour-dependent status indicators;
* appropriate heading hierarchy;
* responsive layouts.

Accessibility is particularly important because NutriLogic is intended for diverse users rather than a narrowly defined technical audience.

---

# 22.31 Responsive Design

The frontend should support the major device classes relevant to the intended deployment environment.

```text
Desktop
   │
   ├── Clinical Dashboard
   │
Tablet
   │
   ├── Assessment / Monitoring
   │
Mobile
   │
   ├── Profile
   ├── Recommendations
   └── Follow-up
```

Responsive design should adapt information density rather than simply shrinking desktop interfaces.

---

# 22.32 Localization and Kenyan Context

Because NutriLogic is explicitly designed for personalized nutrition in Kenya, the frontend should avoid assuming a generic Western nutrition context.

The interface should accommodate:

* Kenyan foods and food terminology;
* Kenyan counties;
* locally meaningful demographic fields;
* metric measurement units;
* locally relevant dietary descriptions;
* appropriate date and number formats;
* culturally appropriate examples.

However, cultural adaptation in the interface must remain consistent with the governed knowledge model. The frontend should not independently invent culturally specific clinical recommendations.

---

# 22.33 Frontend Privacy Architecture

The frontend handles sensitive health information and should therefore minimize unnecessary exposure.

Principles include:

* do not persist unnecessary clinical information in browser storage;
* avoid exposing sensitive information in URLs;
* avoid logging health data to browser consoles;
* clear sensitive state appropriately on logout;
* avoid embedding patient identifiers unnecessarily in UI routes;
* ensure screenshots and exports do not expose data unintentionally;
* prevent unauthorized access through route and component guards.

Frontend privacy complements but does not replace backend privacy controls.

---

# 22.34 Clinical Report Generation

Where the system provides clinical reports, the frontend should request generated reports from the backend rather than independently constructing authoritative clinical documents from partial client state.

The workflow is:

```text
User
 ↓
Request Report
 ↓
Report API
 ↓
Application Service
 ↓
Authoritative Data
 ↓
Generated Report
 ↓
Frontend Presentation / Download
```

This preserves consistency with the persisted clinical record.

---

# 22.35 Frontend Security Boundaries

The following rule is mandatory:

```text
Frontend = Presentation Security
Backend  = Authoritative Security
```

Frontend controls may improve user experience:

```text
if role === "admin":
    show knowledge administration
```

but the backend must independently enforce:

```text
if user is not authorized:
    reject request
```

The frontend must therefore be treated as an **untrusted client**.

---

# 22.36 Performance Architecture

Frontend performance mechanisms should include:

* lazy loading of large feature modules;
* pagination;
* controlled rendering;
* caching of suitable server data;
* image optimization;
* efficient chart rendering;
* request deduplication;
* debounced search;
* avoiding unnecessary global state updates.

Performance optimization must not compromise clinical correctness or data freshness.

For example, historical recommendations may be cached safely, while an actively generated recommendation should not be displayed from stale client state as though it were newly generated.

---

# 22.37 Observability

Frontend observability should capture technical events without unnecessarily capturing sensitive clinical content.

Useful metrics include:

* page-load performance;
* API request latency;
* API error rates;
* failed authentication attempts;
* frontend exceptions;
* feature usage;
* failed assessment submissions.

Logs should avoid:

```text
Patient name
Medical history
Detailed assessment values
```

unless explicitly justified by the governance architecture.

---

# 22.38 Frontend Technology Mapping

| Architectural Responsibility | Technology                                  |
| ---------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- |
| UI Framework                 | React                                       |
| Build Tool                   | Vite                                        |
| Language                     | JavaScript / TypeScript                     |
| API Communication            | HTTP / REST / JSON                          |
| Routing                      | React-compatible routing layer              |
| State Management             | Feature/server-state approach               |
| Visualization                | Charting library where required             |
| Styling                      | CSS / project-defined styling system        |
| Authentication               | JWT-compatible API integration              |
| Testing                      | Frontend unit/component/E2E tooling         |
| API Contract                 | OpenAPI-derived or explicitly modeled types |

The exact libraries used in implementation should be documented in the implementation chapter and verified against the actual project rather than being presented as implemented merely because they are architecturally possible.

---

# 22.39 Frontend–Backend Contract

Chapter 21 establishes the API boundary; Chapter 22 consumes it.

The relationship is:

```text
                  API Contract
                       │
          ┌────────────┴────────────┐
          ▼                         ▼
     Django API                 React Client
          │                         │
     Request DTO               API Types
          │                         │
     Domain Model              View Model
```

The frontend should therefore maintain its own presentation representations where necessary rather than assuming that backend domain entities and frontend view models are identical.

---

# 22.40 End-to-End Frontend Clinical Workflow

The principal workflow is:

```text
┌─────────────────────┐
│ User Authentication │
└──────────┬──────────┘
           ▼
┌─────────────────────┐
│ Patient Dashboard   │
└──────────┬──────────┘
           ▼
┌─────────────────────┐
│ Nutrition Assessment│
└──────────┬──────────┘
           ▼
┌─────────────────────┐
│ Review & Submit     │
└──────────┬──────────┘
           ▼
┌─────────────────────┐
│ Backend Processing  │
└──────────┬──────────┘
           ▼
┌─────────────────────┐
│ Symbolic Inference  │
└──────────┬──────────┘
           ▼
┌─────────────────────┐
│ Recommendation      │
└──────────┬──────────┘
           ▼
┌─────────────────────┐
│ Explanation Viewer  │
└──────────┬──────────┘
           ▼
┌─────────────────────┐
│ Monitoring / Goals  │
└─────────────────────┘
```

This workflow realizes the existing architecture's progression from React through the recommendation API, service layer, inference components, explanation generation, persistence, and REST response back to the React dashboard.

---

# 22.41 Frontend Architectural Boundaries

The following dependencies are permitted:

```text
UI Component
    ↓
Feature Module
    ↓
API Client
    ↓
REST API
```

The following dependencies are prohibited:

```text
React Component
    ↓
Django ORM                    ✗

React Component
    ↓
PostgreSQL                    ✗

React Component
    ↓
PySWIP                        ✗

React Component
    ↓
Prolog Predicate              ✗

React Component
    ↓
Clinical Rule Implementation  ✗
```

This preserves the architectural rule that clinical reasoning remains outside the presentation layer. The existing architecture explicitly states that the presentation layer contains no clinical reasoning logic.

---

# 22.42 Frontend Testing Strategy

The frontend shall eventually be tested at several levels.

## Component Tests

Verify:

* form rendering;
* validation feedback;
* recommendation cards;
* explanation components;
* navigation;
* accessibility behaviour.

## Feature Tests

Verify:

* authentication workflow;
* assessment submission;
* recommendation retrieval;
* explanation display;
* monitoring workflows.

## Integration Tests

Verify:

```text
Component
 ↓
Frontend Service
 ↓
Mock / Test API
```

## End-to-End Tests

Verify:

```text
Browser
 ↓
React
 ↓
REST API
 ↓
Application Services
 ↓
Persistence / Inference
```

The comprehensive testing strategy will be developed in Chapter 24, which the dissertation roadmap assigns to the Testing Framework.

---

# 22.43 Quality Attributes

| Quality Attribute | Frontend Mechanism                              |
| ----------------- | ----------------------------------------------- |
| Usability         | Task-oriented workflows                         |
| Accessibility     | Semantic and accessible UI                      |
| Maintainability   | Feature/component separation                    |
| Security          | Secure session handling + backend authorization |
| Performance       | Lazy loading, pagination, caching               |
| Explainability    | Dedicated explanation interface                 |
| Reliability       | Explicit loading/error states                   |
| Responsiveness    | Adaptive layouts                                |
| Interoperability  | REST API contracts                              |
| Privacy           | Minimal client-side sensitive-data exposure     |
| Clinical safety   | Truthful inference and recommendation states    |
| Extensibility     | Feature-oriented architecture                   |

---

# 22.44 Architectural Decision Record

## ADR-022: Adoption of a React-Based Presentation Architecture

### Status

**Accepted**

### Context

NutriLogic requires a responsive, interactive presentation layer capable of supporting nutrition assessment, recommendation visualization, explanation, monitoring, dashboards, and future feature expansion.

The system's architectural separation requires clinical reasoning to remain outside the presentation layer.

### Decision

NutriLogic shall use a React-based Single Page Application as the principal presentation client.

The frontend shall communicate with the backend exclusively through the versioned REST API and shall not directly access the database, Django ORM, or SWI-Prolog reasoning engine.

Frontend organization shall be feature-oriented, with reusable presentation components and centralized API integration.

### Rationale

The decision:

* provides a component-based UI architecture;
* supports complex interactive workflows;
* aligns with the existing React/Vite implementation;
* maintains separation between presentation and clinical reasoning;
* enables reusable dashboards and visualization components;
* supports future client-side evolution without changing the domain layer.

### Consequences

**Positive**

* Clear separation of presentation and domain responsibilities.
* Reusable UI components.
* Strong API boundary.
* Easier frontend testing.
* Suitable architecture for dashboards and explanation interfaces.

**Trade-offs**

* Requires careful state management.
* Introduces client-side application complexity.
* Requires explicit API contract management.
* Frontend security cannot be treated as a substitute for backend authorization.

The architectural benefits outweigh these costs because the system requires interactive clinical workflows rather than a simple server-rendered CRUD interface.

---

| FR-001 User Registration           | Registration Interface                    | Chapter 21             |

| NFR-Explainability                 | Explanation Interface                     | Chapter 16             |

The current requirements explicitly define FR-011 as maintenance of complete recommendation history, which is therefore a concrete frontend responsibility rather than merely a backend storage feature.

---

# 22.46 Cross-Chapter Traceability

Chapter 22 realizes the architectural decisions established in previous chapters.

| Previous Chapter | Frontend Realization                                                               |
| ---------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Chapter 5        | Functional requirements become user workflows                                      |
| Chapter 6        | System architecture becomes client interaction                                     |
| Chapter 10       | Nutrition Care Process becomes assessment, intervention, and monitoring interfaces |

| Chapter 16       | Security, governance, and explainability become UI constraints                     |

The dissertation's implementation mapping explicitly assigns REST APIs and authentication to Chapter 21 and the React Client, Dashboard, and Explainability Interface to Chapter 22, establishing this transition between backend and frontend realization.

---

# 22.47 Architectural Conformance Rules

The following rules shall govern frontend implementation.

### Rule 1 — No Clinical Reasoning in React

React components shall not implement clinical diagnosis or recommendation rules.

### Rule 2 — No Direct Infrastructure Access

The frontend shall not access PostgreSQL, Django ORM, SWI-Prolog, or filesystem knowledge bases.

### Rule 3 — API Boundary

Clinical data shall cross the frontend/backend boundary through documented API contracts.

### Rule 4 — Backend Authorization

Frontend visibility controls shall never be considered sufficient authorization.

### Rule 5 — Explainability Preservation

Recommendation interfaces shall retain access to their associated explanation information where available.

### Rule 6 — Truthful System State

The interface shall not represent failed, pending, or incomplete reasoning as successful recommendations.

### Rule 7 — Sensitive Data Minimization

The frontend shall minimize persistent storage and unnecessary logging of health information.

### Rule 8 — Accessibility

Critical workflows shall remain usable through accessible interaction mechanisms.

### Rule 9 — Knowledge Governance

Knowledge administration interfaces shall invoke governed backend workflows rather than directly manipulating knowledge artefacts.

### Rule 10 — Architectural Traceability

New frontend features shall be mapped to an existing bounded context or justified as a new architectural capability.

These rules reinforce the broader governance principle that business rules remain in the domain layer, external integrations use ports and adapters, clinical reasoning remains in the symbolic subsystem, and architectural changes are governed through ADRs.

---

# 22.48 Chapter Summary

This chapter has established the Frontend Architecture for NutriLogic as a React-based presentation layer operating above the versioned REST API established in Chapter 21.

The architecture organizes the frontend around the system's clinical capabilities, including authentication, user profiles, nutrition assessment, recommendations, meal planning, monitoring, recommendation history, analytics, and explainability. This approach corresponds to the bounded contexts established in Chapter 19 and the cross-layer realization matrix that maps each capability across persistence, API, frontend, and testing layers.

The frontend is explicitly prevented from implementing clinical reasoning. Instead, it presents the outputs of the symbolic reasoning engine through structured recommendation and explanation interfaces. This preserves the architectural distinction between presentation, application orchestration, semantic integration, and symbolic reasoning.

Particular attention has been given to the Explainability Interface because explainability is a defining property of NutriLogic. The frontend provides progressive access to recommendation explanations, evidence, triggered rules, knowledge sources, and reasoning traces while avoiding unnecessary exposure of internal implementation details.

The chapter has also established frontend security, privacy, accessibility, responsiveness, performance, error handling, state management, and API integration principles. These ensure that the user interface remains a controlled presentation boundary rather than becoming an accidental location for clinical logic or security enforcement.

The resulting architecture realizes the Presentation View of NutriLogic and provides the client-side foundation required for subsequent deployment and verification.

---

# Chapter 23: Deployment and Operations Architecture

## 23.1 Introduction

Chapters 19–22 transformed the conceptual NutriLogic architecture into an application-oriented software structure. Chapter 19 established the Domain-Driven Application Architecture; Chapter 20 defined persistence; Chapter 21 established the REST API boundary; and Chapter 22 defined the React presentation architecture.

Chapter 23 addresses the next architectural concern: **how the complete NutriLogic platform is deployed, operated, monitored, secured, and maintained as an executable system**.

The Deployment and Operations Architecture realizes the Infrastructure View of NutriLogic.

The purpose of this chapter is not merely to specify servers or containers. It defines the operational environment within which the React frontend, Django application, PostgreSQL persistence layer, semantic knowledge infrastructure, SWI-Prolog reasoning engine, logging facilities, and supporting services cooperate.

The deployment architecture must preserve the architectural principles established throughout the dissertation:

* clinical reasoning remains within the symbolic reasoning layer;
* application orchestration remains within Django;
* persistence remains behind repository abstractions;
* the frontend communicates through the REST API;
* knowledge remains governed and versioned;
* security and privacy controls apply across the deployment boundary;
* operational failures must not be represented as successful clinical recommendations.

The existing system architecture identifies PostgreSQL, optional Redis, SWI-Prolog, file storage, logging, and Docker as infrastructure components.

---

# 23.2 Objectives

The Deployment and Operations Architecture aims to:

1. Define the production topology of NutriLogic.
2. Establish clear runtime boundaries between frontend, backend, persistence, and reasoning infrastructure.
3. Define development, testing, staging, and production environments.
4. Establish secure configuration and secrets management.
5. Define deployment of the React application.
6. Define deployment of the Django application.
7. Define deployment and lifecycle management of PostgreSQL.
8. Define operational integration with SWI-Prolog.
9. Define process, worker, and concurrency constraints.
10. Establish HTTPS and network-security requirements.
11. Define health checks and readiness mechanisms.
12. Establish logging and monitoring.
13. Define backup and recovery procedures.
14. Define CI/CD principles.
15. Establish deployment rollback procedures.
16. Support controlled knowledge-base updates.
17. Provide a foundation for scalability without prematurely introducing unnecessary distributed-system complexity.

---

# 23.3 Architectural Context

NutriLogic is deployed as a multi-component system.

```text
                         Internet / Users
                                │
                                ▼
                    ┌─────────────────────┐
                    │ Reverse Proxy / TLS │
                    └──────────┬──────────┘
                               │
                 ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐
                 ▼                           ▼
        ┌────────────────┐          ┌─────────────────┐
        │ React Frontend │          │ Django REST API │
        │      SPA       │          │ Application     │
        └────────────────┘          └────────┬────────┘
```
